# OCR A-level Geology (H414)

Complete notes for this qualification, generated from `curriculum/notes` by `curriculum/scripts/compile.py`. Do not edit this file; edit the notes and run the script again. Spec references are not yet checked by a person (`refs_checked: false`).

Order and contents follow `notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/map.md`. 29 notes files.

## Spec map

# OCR A-level Geology H414: spec map

Every learning outcome of OCR A Level Geology (H414), in the board's order, with the notes file that covers it.

- Specification: version 3.6, March 2026 (first teaching 2017). Qualification number 603/0782/1. OCR is withdrawing the qualification: the last first-teaching cohort started in September 2026 and the last A-level exams are in summer 2028, with no resits.
- Content is in seven modules. Learning outcomes are numbered by section and letter, for example 3.2.1(b)(iii). Modules 1 to 4 (except 1.2, the Practical Endorsement) are also the AS (H014); modules 5 to 7 are A-level only. The Level column below says which.
- Practical Endorsement: at least 12 assessed practicals covering Practical Activity Groups PAG1 to PAG12, and at least four days of fieldwork. PAGs are listed in [28](28-practical-skills-fieldwork-and-pags.md) and tagged in each file where the spec points to them.
- Shared files: WJEC Eduqas A-level Geology (A480QS) covers much of the same ground, so files 01 to 27 here are written once for both boards and list both in their front matter. The Eduqas map is [../geology-eduqas-a480qs/map.md](../geology-eduqas-a480qs/map.md).
- Background at a lower level: [GCSE Geology (Eduqas C480QS)](../../../gcse/smaller-stem/geology-eduqas/map.md). Radioactive decay maths: [A-level Physics 18](../../physics/18-radioactivity-decay-and-nuclear-radius.md).
- `refs_checked: false`: references were taken from the version 3.6 spec PDF and still need a person to check them. Short titles below are paraphrases.

## Papers

| Component | What it examines |
| --- | --- |
| 01 Fundamentals of geology (2 h 15 min, 110 marks, 41%) | All modules. Section A 25 multiple choice; Section B 85 marks structured, problem solving, calculations, practical |
| 02 Scientific literacy in geology (2 h 15 min, 100 marks, 37%) | All modules, with emphasis on reading and using a passage and data |
| 03 Practical skills in geology (1 h 30 min, 60 marks, 22%) | All modules, with emphasis on practical skills; includes an excerpt from a small-scale geological map |
| 04 Practical Endorsement (non-exam, reported separately) | Pass/not classified, teacher assessed against the CPAC |

## Files

- [01-minerals.md](01-minerals.md): Minerals, silicate structures and mineral tests
- [02-igneous-rocks.md](02-igneous-rocks.md): Classifying igneous rocks and their textures
- [03-sedimentary-rocks-weathering-and-diagenesis.md](03-sedimentary-rocks-weathering-and-diagenesis.md): Weathering, transport, sedimentary rock classification and diagenesis
- [04-metamorphic-rocks.md](04-metamorphic-rocks.md): Metamorphism, metamorphic rocks and grade
- [05-fossils-and-the-fossil-record.md](05-fossils-and-the-fossil-record.md): Fossils, preservation, reliability of the record and palaeoenvironments
- [06-geological-time-and-dating.md](06-geological-time-and-dating.md): Radiometric dating, the geological column, relative dating and biostratigraphy
- [07-physical-structure-of-the-earth.md](07-physical-structure-of-the-earth.md): Seismology, gravity, isostasy, EM surveys, density and the geodynamo
- [08-origin-and-geochemistry-of-the-earth.md](08-origin-and-geochemistry-of-the-earth.md): Bulk composition, nebular hypothesis, internal heat, Goldschmidt and differentiation
- [09-plate-tectonics.md](09-plate-tectonics.md): Heat flow, seismic evidence, plate reconstruction, plumes, GPS, driving forces and history of the theory
- [10-magma-intrusion-and-volcanism.md](10-magma-intrusion-and-volcanism.md): Magma generation, intrusion, eruption, volcanic landforms and hazards
- [11-rock-deformation-and-structures.md](11-rock-deformation-and-structures.md): Stress, strain, folds, faults, cleavage and plate-boundary tectonics
- [12-uniformitarianism-facies-and-sedimentary-environments.md](12-uniformitarianism-facies-and-sedimentary-environments.md): Rock cycle, uniformitarianism, facies, structures, graphic logs and environments
- [13-turbidites-deltas-and-banded-iron-formations.md](13-turbidites-deltas-and-banded-iron-formations.md): Sediment physics, turbidites, deltas, Walther's law, BIFs
- [14-fluids-in-rocks-and-hydrogeology.md](14-fluids-in-rocks-and-hydrogeology.md): Porosity, permeability, Darcy's law, groundwater quality and aquifers
- [15-igneous-petrology.md](15-igneous-petrology.md): Solid solution, Bowen, phase diagrams, magma evolution and layered intrusions
- [16-mid-ocean-ridges.md](16-mid-ocean-ridges.md): Spreading rates, ophiolites, oceanic crust and massive sulfides
- [17-metamorphic-petrology.md](17-metamorphic-petrology.md): Facies, index minerals, isograds, fabrics and deformation style
- [18-mining-geology.md](18-mining-geology.md): Ore concentration, exploration, mining, processing, minewater and repositories
- [19-earthquakes-and-geohazard-risk.md](19-earthquakes-and-geohazard-risk.md): Earthquake impact, magnitude, intensity, liquefaction, forecasting and prediction
- [20-geohazards-in-the-british-isles.md](20-geohazards-in-the-british-isles.md): Shrink-swell clays, subsidence, landslides and tsunamis
- [21-engineering-geology.md](21-engineering-geology.md): Rock strength, site investigation, tunnels, dams and contaminated land
- [22-the-changing-earth-climate-and-sea-level.md](22-the-changing-earth-climate-and-sea-level.md): Continents, climate, sea level, the Wilson cycle, isotopes and the Anthropocene
- [23-evolution-and-applied-palaeontology.md](23-evolution-and-applied-palaeontology.md): Trilobites, corals, brachiopods, bivalves, amphibians, dinosaurs and birds
- [24-mass-extinctions.md](24-mass-extinctions.md): End-Ordovician, end-Permian and end-Cretaceous extinctions
- [25-lagerstatten.md](25-lagerstatten.md): Burgess Shale, Chengjiang and Solnhofen
- [26-oil-and-gas-basins.md](26-oil-and-gas-basins.md): North Sea petroleum system, exploration and onshore basins
- [27-whole-basin-facies-analysis.md](27-whole-basin-facies-analysis.md): The Welsh Basin and the UK Jurassic
- [28-practical-skills-fieldwork-and-pags.md](28-practical-skills-fieldwork-and-pags.md): Module 1, PAG1 to PAG12 and fieldwork

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/28-practical-skills-fieldwork-and-pags.md -->

## Practical skills, the Practical Endorsement and fieldwork

Practical skills are examined in all three OCR papers (especially Component 03, Practical skills in geology) and are assessed separately through the Practical Endorsement (pass or not classified). Learners must complete at least 12 assessed practicals covering PAG1 to PAG12 and at least four days of fieldwork, including virtual fieldwork, local fieldwork and fieldwork on unfamiliar outcrops. Eduqas students: see [Eduqas 06](../geology-eduqas-a480qs/06-specified-practicals-and-fieldwork.md).

### 1.1 Practical skills assessed in the written papers

#### What you need to know

- Planning (1.1.1): experimental design to solve problems, choosing apparatus and techniques; identifying variables to control; evaluating whether a method fits the aim.
- Implementing (1.1.2): using apparatus correctly; appropriate units; presenting observations and data.
- Analysis (1.1.3): processing qualitative and quantitative results and drawing valid conclusions; maths skills; significant figures; plotting graphs with labelled axes and scales; gradients and intercepts.
- Evaluation (1.1.4): evaluating results and conclusions; anomalies; limitations; precision, accuracy, margins of error, percentage error, uncertainty in apparatus; suggesting improvements.

#### Explained

**Planning**: state the independent variable (changed), dependent variable (measured) and control variables (kept constant). In fieldwork many variables cannot be controlled, so choose sites to reduce differences and record conditions.

**Data**: tables with units in headings; consistent significant figures; graphs with the independent variable on the x-axis, a sensible scale filling over half the grid, and a best-fit line. Gradient = Δy ÷ Δx from a large triangle.

**Accuracy and precision**: accurate results are close to the true value; precise results are close together (small spread). Repeat readings improve reliability and allow anomalies to be identified.

**Uncertainty**: half the smallest division for a single reading on a scale (or the stated tolerance); for a difference between two readings, add the uncertainties. Percentage uncertainty = uncertainty ÷ value × 100. For products and quotients, add percentage uncertainties.

#### Worked example

Density of a rock: mass 52.4 ± 0.1 g, volume 20 ± 1 cm³. Percentage uncertainties: 0.19% and 5%. Density = 2.62 g cm⁻³ ± 5.2%, so ± 0.14 g cm⁻³. The volume measurement dominates: improve it with a finer measuring cylinder or by weighing in water.

### 1.2 The Practical Endorsement (A level only)

#### What you need to know

- 1.2.1: apply investigative approaches; use equipment safely (identify hazards, minimise risk); follow written instructions; make and record observations; keep records; present data scientifically; use software to process data; research with online and offline sources; cite sources; use a wide range of instruments and techniques.
- 1.2.2 apparatus and techniques (asterisked ones must be shown on unfamiliar outcrops):
  - (a)* locate features in the field with traditional navigation without GPS
  - (b)* identify structures in the field and record them in field sketches
  - (c)* use a compass-clinometer to measure dip and strike of planar surfaces and apparent dip of fold limbs
  - (d)* construct graphic logs with appropriate scale and symbols
  - (e)* use sampling techniques in fieldwork
  - (f) use classification systems to identify unknown minerals and fossils
  - (g) produce annotated scientific drawings of fossils or small features from hand samples with a microscope or hand lens
  - (h)* produce full rock descriptions of macro and micro features
  - (i) use photomicrographs to identify minerals and textures
  - (j) use apparatus to measure mass, time, volume, temperature and length
  - (k) physical and chemical tests on minerals, including density and Mohs hardness
  - (l)* increase accuracy, for example timing multiple observations or using a fiducial scale in photographs and sketches
  - (m) use ICT and GIS to compile, process and model geological data.
- The Common Practical Assessment Criteria (CPAC), shared across OCR sciences: follows written procedures; applies investigative approaches when using instruments; uses equipment safely; makes and records observations; researches, references and reports.

#### Practical Activity Groups

| PAG | Title | Example activity | Notes file |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Investigating minerals and rocks | Identify rocks and minerals from hand specimens, keys and photomicrographs | [01](01-minerals.md), [02](02-igneous-rocks.md), [03](03-sedimentary-rocks-weathering-and-diagenesis.md), [04](04-metamorphic-rocks.md) |
| 2 | Investigating seismology | Measure seismic wave speeds with a hammer source and phone geophone app | [09](09-plate-tectonics.md), [19](19-earthquakes-and-geohazard-risk.md) |
| 3 | Investigating crystalline processes | Crystal size and cooling rate using salol | [02](02-igneous-rocks.md), [15](15-igneous-petrology.md) |
| 4 | Investigating sedimentary processes | Sieve analysis of sands from contrasting environments | [03](03-sedimentary-rocks-weathering-and-diagenesis.md), [13](13-turbidites-deltas-and-banded-iron-formations.md) |
| 5 | Investigating fossils | Identify and draw fossils; interpret mode of life | [05](05-fossils-and-the-fossil-record.md), [23](23-evolution-and-applied-palaeontology.md) |
| 6 | Investigating geological sequences | Field sketches, logs, dip and strike, relative dating | [11](11-rock-deformation-and-structures.md), [12](12-uniformitarianism-facies-and-sedimentary-environments.md) |
| 7 | Investigating orogenic processes | Metamorphic minerals, isograds, deformation | [17](17-metamorphic-petrology.md) |
| 8 | Investigating fluid movement | Porosity and permeability; modelling migration | [14](14-fluids-in-rocks-and-hydrogeology.md) |
| 9 | Site investigations | Rock strength; slope stability; GIS hazard maps | [20](20-geohazards-in-the-british-isles.md), [21](21-engineering-geology.md) |
| 10 | Investigating geological resources | Geophysical and geochemical exploration data | [18](18-mining-geology.md), [26](26-oil-and-gas-basins.md) |
| 11 | Investigation | An independent investigation applying investigative approaches (can be met throughout the course, often in the field) | This file |
| 12 | Research skills | Research and cite sources; integrate field data with secondary data | [27](27-whole-basin-facies-analysis.md) |

(PAG titles are paraphrased.)

### 1.3 Fieldwork skills (AS and A level)

#### What you need to know

- (a) Measure and describe diagnostic properties of rocks in the field: colour, composition, grain or crystal size and shape, sorting, igneous texture, metamorphic fabric.
- (b) Collect valid data on the processes that formed the rocks: random, stratified and systematic sampling; sampling validity.
- (c) Measure and describe deformation in the field, with valid sampling.
- (d) Use geochronological principles in the field to place events in relative time order.

#### Explained

**Field description**: describe from a fresh surface; use a hand lens and grain-size card (comparator); measure crystal and grain sizes against a scale; test with acid where appropriate; note structures and fossils; take photographs with a scale (coin, hammer, scale bar).

**Sampling**:

- Random: every location has an equal chance of selection (random numbers on a grid); avoids bias.
- Systematic: measurements at regular intervals (every metre along a traverse or log).
- Stratified: divide the outcrop into units (beds, zones) and sample each in proportion.
- Validity: enough measurements (for example many dip readings to average, since bedding surfaces are uneven); sample representative parts of the outcrop; record locations by grid reference.

**Deformation**: measure many dip and strike readings on each fold limb; record fault displacements, joint orientations, cleavage; plot data (stereonets, rose diagrams).

**Relative dating in the field**: apply superposition, way-up criteria, cross-cutting relationships, included fragments and unconformities ([06](06-geological-time-and-dating.md)).

**Navigation without GPS**: use a topographic map, compass bearings, pacing, and six-figure grid references.

**Field sketches**: title, location (grid reference), orientation (direction of view), scale, labels and interpretation; show the geology, not every detail of vegetation.

**Safety and conduct**: risk assessment (cliffs, tides, quarries, weather); hard hats near faces; goggles for hammering; minimal hammering and sample collection (follow the geological fieldwork code; respect SSSIs and landowners).

### Maths skills (summary)

Units and prefixes; standard form; ratios, fractions, percentages; significant figures; mean, median, mode, standard deviation; frequency tables, histograms, cumulative frequency; scatter graphs; probability; logarithms (phi scale, magnitude); rearranging equations; y = mx + c, gradients and intercepts; rates of change; areas and volumes; trigonometry for dip and thickness; reading circular data (rose diagrams).

#### Common mistakes

- Taking one dip reading and treating it as representative.
- Field sketches with no scale or orientation.
- Confusing precision with accuracy.

### Check questions

1. Distinguish between accuracy and precision. (2)
2. A student measured the dip of one bed at five points: 22°, 25°, 21°, 34°, 23°. Identify the anomaly, suggest a cause and calculate the mean of the remaining values. (3)
3. Explain why stratified sampling may be better than random sampling on a cliff of alternating sandstone and shale. (2)
4. A grain is 0.8 ± 0.05 mm and a second 1.6 ± 0.05 mm. Calculate the percentage uncertainty in each and comment. (3)
5. List four things that must appear on a field sketch. (4)
6. Describe how to measure the strike and dip of a bedding plane with a compass-clinometer. (4)
7. Plan a field investigation to test whether grain size changes upwards through a sandstone sequence. (6)

### Mark scheme

1. Accuracy: closeness to the true value (1); precision: closeness of repeated readings to each other (1).
2. 34° anomalous (1); uneven surface / misread / measured a cross-bed not bedding (1); mean = (22 + 25 + 21 + 23) ÷ 4 = 22.75 ≈ 23° (1).
3. Ensures both rock types are sampled in proportion (1); random may miss thin beds / one lithology (1).
4. 6.25% (1); 3.1% (1); smaller measurements have larger percentage uncertainty / use magnification or a finer scale (1).
5. Any four: title; grid reference / location; direction of view; scale; labels; interpretation; date.
6. Place compass edge horizontally on the bedding plane / level with spirit level and read strike bearing (1); rotate 90° to point down the steepest slope (1); read dip angle with the clinometer (1); record dip direction; repeat and average (1).
7. Level-marked: systematic sampling at measured intervals up the log (1); use grain-size comparator / collect samples for sieving (1); many grains / repeat measurements at each level (1); control: same bed type / avoid weathered surfaces (1); plot grain size against height; Spearman's rank (1); risk assessment and conduct (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/physics/18-radioactivity-decay-and-nuclear-radius.md -->

## Radioactivity: Rutherford scattering, alpha, beta and gamma radiation, decay, nuclear instability and nuclear radius

This file covers how alpha scattering revealed the nucleus, the properties of alpha, beta and gamma radiation, the inverse-square law for gamma (AQA required practical 12), background radiation, the random exponential decay of nuclei, half-life and activity, decay modes and the N–Z plot, and how the size of the nucleus is measured.

### Overview

A-level only for AQA, Edexcel, OCR A and OCR B. Eduqas has Rutherford scattering (C3.7(a), (b)) at AS and nuclear decay (C3.6) at A-level. Basic alpha and beta decay equations and the strong force are in file 02. Binding energy, fission, fusion and reactors are in file 19. Medical uses of radionuclides are in file 23.

#### Board differences

- AQA 3.8.1.1 to 3.8.1.5, RP12 · Edexcel 131, 168 to 173, CP15 · OCR A 6.4.1, 6.4.3 · OCR B 5.1.1 (decay as exponential), 6.2.1, 6.2.2 · Eduqas C3.6, C3.7(a), (b).
- Inverse-square law for gamma: AQA RP12 and Eduqas specified practical. Edexcel CP15 instead investigates absorption of gamma by lead (I = I₀e^(−μx)).
- Half-life of protactinium: OCR B 5.1.1(d)(iv), OCR A 6.4.3(e)(ii) (PAG; or a simulation). Dice simulation: OCR A 6.4.3(f), Eduqas specified practical, suggested by AQA.
- OCR B 6.2.2(c)(ii), (iii): absorbed dose (gray) and effective dose (sievert = gray × quality factor). Also in Eduqas option B (file 23).
- Nuclear radius R = R₀A^(1/3), electron diffraction by nuclei and nuclear density: AQA 3.8.1.5, OCR A 6.4.1(f), (g). Eduqas C3.7(b) asks for the closest-approach estimate only.
- OCR A 6.4.3(h): radioactive dating (carbon-14).
- AQA 3.8.1.4: decay modes on the N–Z graph, nuclear excited states and technetium-99m. OCR B 6.2.2(a)(ii): "nuclear valley" of stability.

### 3.8.1.1 Rutherford scattering

*Refs: AQA 3.8.1.1 · Edexcel 131 · OCR A 6.4.1(a), (c) · OCR B 6.2.1(a)(ii), (b)(ii), (c)(ii) · Eduqas C3.7(a), (b). Level: A-level only (Eduqas AS & A).*

#### What you need to know

- Rutherford scattering, qualitatively.
- How understanding of the structure of the atom changed over time.
- (Eduqas C3.7(b)) Estimating the maximum Coulomb force on an alpha particle in the plum-pudding and nuclear models.

#### Explained

**The experiment** (Geiger and Marsden, 1909). A narrow beam of alpha particles from a radium source was aimed at a very thin gold foil in a vacuum. A zinc sulfide screen viewed through a microscope detected the alpha particles at different angles (each one made a tiny flash).

**Observations and conclusions.**

| Observation | Conclusion |
| --- | --- |
| Most alpha particles went straight through | Most of the atom is empty space |
| Some were deflected through small angles | There is a concentrated positive charge that repels them |
| A very few (about 1 in 8000) bounced back through more than 90° | The positive charge and most of the mass are concentrated in a tiny nucleus |

**History.** Thomson's plum-pudding model (1904) had positive charge spread through the atom with electrons dotted in it. In that model the electric field inside the atom is weak (the charge is spread out), so alphas could only be deflected slightly. Rutherford's nuclear model (1911) explained large-angle scattering. Later: Bohr added electron energy levels (1913); Chadwick discovered the neutron (1932).

**Eduqas estimate.** Maximum Coulomb force on an alpha (charge 2e) from a gold atom's charge (79e):

- Plum pudding: charge spread over the atom (r ≈ 10⁻¹⁰ m); maximum force at the surface: F = k(2e)(79e)/r² ≈ 3.6 × 10⁻⁶ N. Too small to reverse a fast alpha.
- Nuclear: all the charge within about 10⁻¹⁴ m: F ≈ 3.6 × 10² N, 10⁸ times larger, enough to turn it back.

**Path of an alpha** (OCR B 6.2.1(b)(ii)). Alphas follow hyperbolic paths around the nucleus. The closer the original line of travel is to the nucleus, the bigger the deflection; a head-on alpha comes straight back.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the alpha particles hit electrons and bounced back.
- Saying "most went through, so the nucleus is small" (this shows the atom is mostly empty; the large-angle back-scattering shows the nucleus is small, dense and positive).

### 3.8.1.2 α, β and γ radiation

*Refs: AQA 3.8.1.2, RP12 · Edexcel 168, 169, 170, 171, CP15 · OCR A 6.4.3(a), (b), (c) · OCR B 6.2.2(a)(i), (b)(i), (c)(ii), (c)(iii), (d)(i) · Eduqas C3.6(a), (b), (c). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Properties of alpha, beta and gamma radiation and how to identify them with simple absorption experiments.
- Applications: relative hazards to humans, thickness monitoring (aluminium foil, paper, steel), safe handling.
- The inverse-square law for gamma radiation: I = k/x². Verifying it experimentally.
- Background radiation: sources and correcting for it.
- Balancing risks and benefits of radiation in medicine.
- (OCR B) Absorbed dose (Gy) and effective dose (Sv).

#### Explained

| | Alpha α | Beta-minus β⁻ | Gamma γ |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Nature | Helium nucleus ⁴₂He | Fast electron | EM radiation (photon) |
| Charge | +2e | −e | 0 |
| Speed | About 0.05c | Up to about 0.99c | c |
| Ionising | Strongly (about 10⁴ ion pairs per cm in air) | Moderately (about 10² per cm) | Weakly (about 1 per cm) |
| Range in air | A few cm (fixed range for each source) | About 1 m (spread of ranges) | Very large; intensity falls as 1/x² |
| Stopped by | Paper or skin | A few mm of aluminium | Reduced by several cm of lead or metres of concrete (never fully stopped) |
| Deflected in fields | Yes, slightly (heavy) | Yes, strongly, opposite way to α | No |

(β⁺ particles are positrons; they annihilate almost at once with an electron, producing two gamma photons.)

**Why they differ.** Alpha particles are big, slow and doubly charged, so they interact strongly with every atom they pass, ionising heavily and losing energy quickly. Gamma photons are uncharged and interact only occasionally.

**Identifying radiation.** Use a Geiger–Müller tube and counter close to the source. Measure the count rate with nothing in between, then with paper, then with 3 mm of aluminium, then with lead. Correct each count for background. A drop with paper shows α; a further drop with aluminium shows β; what remains through aluminium (and is reduced by lead) is γ.

**Hazards.** Outside the body, alpha is the least dangerous (it cannot penetrate skin), gamma the most. Inside the body (swallowed or breathed in), alpha is the most dangerous because all its energy is deposited in a small volume of tissue, damaging cells and DNA.

**Safe handling.** Use long-handled tongs; never point a source at anyone; keep the time of exposure short; keep as far away as possible (inverse-square law for gamma); use shielding (lead-lined boxes); store sources in lead-lined containers; wash hands after.

**Thickness monitoring.** A source on one side of a sheet and a detector on the other. If the count rate changes, the thickness has changed, and rollers are adjusted. Use β for paper and thin aluminium foil (α would be stopped completely; γ would pass with almost no change). Use γ for steel sheet.

**Inverse-square law for gamma.** A point source emits gamma photons equally in all directions; the energy spreads over a sphere of area 4πx², so intensity (and count rate) I = k/x². (Alpha and beta are absorbed by air, so they do not follow this law.)

**Background radiation.** Always present. Sources: radon gas from rocks (the largest part in the UK), cosmic rays, rocks and buildings, food and drink (e.g. potassium-40), medical uses, nuclear industry and past weapons tests. Measure the background count rate (with no source, over a long time, e.g. 10 minutes) and subtract it from every reading.

**Dose** (OCR B 6.2.2(c)(ii), (iii)). Absorbed dose = energy absorbed per kilogram of tissue, in gray (1 Gy = 1 J kg⁻¹). Effective dose = absorbed dose × quality (weighting) factor, in sieverts (Sv). The quality factor is 1 for β and γ, 20 for α, because α does much more damage per joule. Risk of cancer rises roughly in proportion to effective dose (about 5% per Sv).

**Risks and benefits in medicine.** X-rays, CT scans and radioactive tracers give a dose that carries a small risk of cancer, so they are used only when the diagnostic or treatment benefit outweighs it, with the lowest dose possible.

#### Worked example

1. A gamma source gives a corrected count rate of 480 counts per minute at 0.20 m. Predict the count rate at 0.50 m.

   I ∝ 1/x²: 480 × (0.20/0.50)² = 77 counts per minute.

2. A 70 kg worker absorbs 2.1 mJ from alpha radiation. Find the absorbed and effective dose (quality factor 20).

   Absorbed dose = 2.1 × 10⁻³/70 = 3.0 × 10⁻⁵ Gy. Effective = 6.0 × 10⁻⁴ Sv (0.6 mSv).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying gamma radiation is completely stopped by lead.
- Saying alpha is the most dangerous in all cases.
- Forgetting to subtract background before testing the inverse-square law.
- Saying beta particles come from the electron shells (they come from the nucleus).

#### Practical

**AQA required practical 12** (also Eduqas specified practical): investigate the inverse-square law for gamma radiation.

1. Measure the background count with the GM tube for at least 5 minutes, with all sources away. Find the background count rate.
2. Place a sealed gamma source (cobalt-60) using tongs in a holder facing the GM tube along a metre rule.
3. Record the count over a fixed time (e.g. 2 minutes) at distances from about 5 cm to 1 m. Repeat and average.
4. Subtract the background rate to get the corrected count rate C.
5. The source is inside its holder and the GM tube detects some distance inside its window, so the true distance is x + d, where d is an unknown offset. Plot 1/√C against x: a straight line whose x-intercept gives −d. (Or plot C against 1/x² if d is negligible.)

Errors: radioactive decay is random, so count for longer at larger distances to reduce percentage uncertainty (uncertainty in a count N is about √N). Dead time of the GM tube at close range lowers counts. Safety: handle the source with tongs, keep it at arm's length, return it to its lead box promptly, do not point it at anyone, and keep exposure time short.

**Edexcel CP15** (also OCR B 6.2.2(d)(i)): absorption of gamma by lead. Place lead sheets of increasing thickness x between a gamma source and a GM tube at a fixed distance. Measure count rates, correct for background. The corrected count rate falls exponentially: C = C₀e^(−μx), where μ is the linear attenuation (absorption) coefficient. Plot ln C against x: straight line, gradient −μ. Half-value thickness = ln 2/μ.

### 3.8.1.3 Radioactive decay

*Refs: AQA 3.8.1.3 · Edexcel 168, 172, 173 · OCR A 6.4.3(a), (d) to (h) · OCR B 5.1.1(a)(iv), (b)(ii), (iv), (v), (c)(i), (ii), (d)(iv), 6.2.2(c)(i), (d)(ii) · Eduqas C3.6(a), (d) to (h). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- Decay is random and spontaneous; each nucleus has a constant probability of decaying per unit time (the decay constant λ).
- ΔN/Δt = −λN; N = N₀e^(−λt); activity A = λN; A = A₀e^(−λt).
- Half-life T½ = ln 2/λ.
- Finding half-life from decay curves and log graphs.
- Questions may use molar mass and the Avogadro constant.
- Modelling with constant decay probability (dice, spreadsheets, iterative methods).
- Applications: storing radioactive waste, radioactive dating.

#### Explained

**Random and spontaneous.** It is impossible to predict when a particular nucleus will decay (random), and decay is not affected by external conditions such as temperature, pressure or chemical state (spontaneous). But for a large number of nuclei, the fraction that decays per second is constant: the decay constant λ (s⁻¹) is the probability that a given nucleus decays in one second.

**Activity** A is the number of decays per second, in becquerels (1 Bq = 1 decay per second). A = λN.

**Exponential decay.** The rate of decay is proportional to the number of undecayed nuclei: dN/dt = −λN. Solution:

- N = N₀e^(−λt)
- A = A₀e^(−λt)
- (also count rate C = C₀e^(−λt), and mass of the parent remaining)

**Half-life** T½: the average time for the number of undecayed nuclei (or the activity) of an isotope to halve. Setting N = N₀/2 gives T½ = ln 2/λ ≈ 0.693/λ. After n half-lives, N = N₀/2ⁿ.

**Graphs.**

- N (or A) against t: exponential curve; read off T½ as the time to fall to half, then half again (check it is constant).
- ln N (or ln A) against t: straight line, ln N = ln N₀ − λt. Gradient = −λ.

**Using moles.** N = (mass/molar mass) × N_A. For example 1.0 μg of radium-226: N = (1.0 × 10⁻⁹ kg / 0.226 kg mol⁻¹) × 6.02 × 10²³ = 2.66 × 10¹⁵ nuclei.

**Modelling** (OCR A 6.4.3(f), (g), OCR B 5.1.1(c)(ii), Eduqas practical, AQA suggested).

- Dice: roll 100 dice; remove all that show a six (each has probability 1/6 of "decaying" per throw); count the remainder; repeat. A graph of number remaining against throw number is an exponential decay with a "half-life" of about 3.8 throws (ln 2/ln(6/5)).
- Spreadsheet: ΔN = −λNΔt each step; N_new = N − λNΔt.

**Applications.**

- *Radioactive dating* (OCR A 6.4.3(h)). Living things take in carbon, including a constant small fraction of carbon-14 (T½ = 5730 years). When they die, intake stops and ¹⁴C decays. Measuring the activity per gram of carbon and comparing with living material gives the age. Rocks are dated using uranium/lead or potassium/argon ratios.
- *Medical tracers*: short half-lives (hours) so the patient is not exposed for long, but long enough for the test.
- *Radioactive waste*: some isotopes have half-lives of thousands of years, so waste must be stored securely for very long times (file 19).

#### Worked example

1. Iodine-131 has a half-life of 8.0 days. Find λ, and the activity after 20 days of a sample with initial activity 4.0 × 10⁶ Bq.

   λ = 0.693/(8.0 × 86 400) = 1.0 × 10⁻⁶ s⁻¹. A = 4.0 × 10⁶ e^(−0.693 × 20/8.0) = 4.0 × 10⁶ × 0.177 = 7.1 × 10⁵ Bq.

2. Find the activity of 1.0 μg of radium-226 (T½ = 1600 years).

   λ = 0.693/(1600 × 3.16 × 10⁷) = 1.37 × 10⁻¹¹ s⁻¹. N = 2.66 × 10¹⁵. A = 3.6 × 10⁴ Bq.

3. Wood from an ancient boat has 0.60 of the ¹⁴C activity per gram of living wood. Find its age.

   0.60 = e^(−λt); t = −ln 0.60/λ = 0.511 × 5730/0.693 = 4200 years.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the half-life is half the time it takes for all the nuclei to decay.
- Using days for T½ but seconds for t (or the reverse).
- Saying that after two half-lives the sample has completely decayed.
- Forgetting to subtract background from count rates before finding half-life.

#### Practical

**Half-life of protactinium-234** (OCR B 5.1.1(d)(iv), OCR A). A sealed bottle contains a uranium salt in water and an organic solvent. Shaking separates protactinium-234 into the organic layer (on top). Place a GM tube next to the top layer and record counts every 10 s for about 5 minutes. Subtract background. Plot ln(count rate) against t: gradient = −λ; T½ ≈ 70 s. (Done by teachers or with simulations in many schools; the bottle must not be opened.)

### 3.8.1.4 Nuclear instability

*Refs: AQA 3.8.1.4 · Edexcel 170 · OCR A 6.4.3(c) · OCR B 6.2.2(a)(ii) · Eduqas C3.6(a). Level: A-level only.*

#### What you need to know

- The graph of N (neutron number) against Z (proton number) for stable nuclei.
- Decay modes of unstable nuclei: α, β⁺, β⁻ and electron capture.
- Changes in N and Z caused by each decay, in decay equations.
- Nuclear energy level diagrams; nuclear excited states and γ emission; technetium-99m in medical diagnosis.

#### Explained

**The N–Z plot.** Stable nuclei lie along a band. For light nuclei (Z < 20) the band follows N = Z. For heavier nuclei, more neutrons than protons are needed (N > Z) because the extra neutrons add strong-force attraction without adding electrostatic repulsion. Above Z = 83 no nucleus is fully stable.

**Decay modes and where they occur.**

| Mode | Where on the plot | Change | Example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| α | Very heavy nuclei (Z > 82) | Z − 2, N − 2, A − 4 | ²²⁶₈₈Ra → ²²²₈₆Rn + ⁴₂α |
| β⁻ | Above the band (too many neutrons) | Z + 1, N − 1, A same | ¹⁴₆C → ¹⁴₇N + ⁰₋₁e + ν̄ₑ |
| β⁺ | Below the band (too many protons) | Z − 1, N + 1, A same | ¹¹₆C → ¹¹₅B + ⁰₊₁e + νₑ |
| Electron capture | Below the band (proton-rich; competes with β⁺) | Z − 1, N + 1 | ⁷₄Be + ⁰₋₁e → ⁷₃Li + νₑ |
| γ | Excited nucleus | No change in Z or N | ⁹⁹ᵐ₄₃Tc → ⁹⁹₄₃Tc + γ |

On an N–Z graph, α moves a nucleus 2 down and 2 left; β⁻ moves it 1 down and 1 right; β⁺ and electron capture move it 1 up and 1 left.

**Nuclear excited states and gamma.** After an α or β decay, the daughter nucleus is often left in an excited state (with extra energy). It drops to its ground state by emitting a gamma photon, whose energy equals the gap between nuclear energy levels (typically keV to MeV, much bigger than atomic levels). A nuclear energy level diagram shows parent and daughter levels with arrows for each decay.

**Technetium-99m** (the m means metastable). Made from molybdenum-99 (T½ = 66 h), which decays by β⁻ to Tc-99m. Tc-99m then emits a 140 keV gamma photon, with T½ = 6 hours. Ideal as a medical tracer: pure gamma (penetrates the body, little ionisation inside), easy to detect with a gamma camera, short half-life (low dose), long enough to do the scan, can be attached to many chemicals to target organs, and can be made on site in a hospital "generator".

**Nuclear valley** (OCR B). If binding energy is plotted against N and Z as a surface, stable nuclei sit along the floor of a valley. Nuclei on the valley sides decay "downhill" towards the floor by β⁻ or β⁺; very heavy nuclei at the far end decay by α.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying β⁻ decay reduces Z.
- Saying gamma emission changes the element.
- Drawing the stability band as N = Z for heavy nuclei.

### 3.8.1.5 Nuclear radius

*Refs: AQA 3.8.1.5 · OCR A 6.4.1(c), (f), (g) · Eduqas C3.7(b). Level: A-level only (Eduqas AS & A).*

#### What you need to know

- Estimating nuclear radius from the closest approach of alpha particles (using the Coulomb equation); finding it from electron diffraction.
- Typical values of nuclear radius (about 10⁻¹⁵ m).
- R = R₀A^(1/3), found from experiment; evidence that nuclear density is constant.
- Calculating nuclear density.
- The graph of intensity against angle for electron diffraction by nuclei.

#### Explained

**Closest approach.** An alpha particle fired head-on at a nucleus slows as it approaches and stops momentarily at distance r, where all its kinetic energy has become electric potential energy:

Ek = (2e)(Ze)/(4πε₀r) → r = 2Ze²/(4πε₀Ek)

This gives an upper limit for the nuclear radius (the alpha does not touch the nucleus). Limitations: the alpha's charge is spread out; the recoil of the nucleus is ignored; the strong force may act if it gets close enough.

**Electron diffraction.** High-energy electrons (hundreds of MeV) have de Broglie wavelengths about 10⁻¹⁵ m, similar to a nucleus. Fired at a thin film, they diffract around the nuclei. The intensity against angle falls from a central maximum to a first minimum (not zero) at θ, where sin θ ≈ 0.61λ/R. So R = 0.61λ/sin θ. Electrons are better than alphas because they are leptons (not affected by the strong force) and they probe the whole nucleus. At these energies, λ ≈ hc/E (relativistic).

**R = R₀A^(1/3).** Experiments show R₀ ≈ 1.2 fm (1.2 × 10⁻¹⁵ m; data booklet value 1.05 to 1.4 fm depending on method). Plotting R against A^(1/3) gives a straight line through the origin, gradient R₀. Or plot ln R against ln A: gradient 1/3.

**Constant density.** Volume = (4/3)πR³ = (4/3)πR₀³A, so volume ∝ A. Mass ≈ A × u. So density = Au/((4/3)πR₀³A) = 3u/(4πR₀³), the same for all nuclei: about 2.3 × 10¹⁷ kg m⁻³. This shows nucleons are packed together at a fixed spacing, as in a liquid drop, and that the nucleus is far denser than the atom (most of the atom's volume is empty).

#### Worked example

1. Find the closest approach of a 7.7 MeV alpha particle to a gold nucleus (Z = 79).

   r = 2 × 79 × (1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹)² × 8.99 × 10⁹/(7.7 × 1.60 × 10⁻¹³) = 3.64 × 10⁻²⁶/1.23 × 10⁻¹² = 3.0 × 10⁻¹⁴ m.

2. Find the radius of a ¹⁹⁷Au nucleus with R₀ = 1.2 fm, and compare with example 1.

   R = 1.2 × 10⁻¹⁵ × 197^(1/3) = 1.2 × 10⁻¹⁵ × 5.82 = 7.0 × 10⁻¹⁵ m. The closest approach is about 4 times larger, so it is only an upper limit.

3. Find the density of nuclear matter.

   ρ = 3 × 1.66 × 10⁻²⁷/(4π × (1.2 × 10⁻¹⁵)³) = 2.3 × 10¹⁷ kg m⁻³.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the closest approach equals the nuclear radius.
- Forgetting the cube root.
- Saying the first minimum of electron diffraction by nuclei goes to zero.

### Check questions

1. Describe the alpha scattering experiment and explain what each observation showed about the atom. (6 marks)
2. Describe how to show that a source emits only beta and gamma radiation. (4 marks)
3. Explain why alpha radiation is the most dangerous when a source is inside the body. (2 marks)
4. A corrected gamma count rate is 900 min⁻¹ at 0.10 m. At what distance would it be 100 min⁻¹? (2 marks)
5. Define half-life and decay constant, and show that T½ = ln 2/λ. (4 marks)
6. A sample of cobalt-60 (T½ = 5.27 years) has activity 2.0 × 10⁵ Bq. Find the number of cobalt nuclei and the mass of cobalt. (4 marks)
7. Strontium-90 decays by β⁻ emission. On an N–Z diagram, describe how the nucleus moves, and explain why it is unstable. (3 marks)
8. Calculate the radius of a ⁵⁶Fe nucleus (R₀ = 1.2 fm) and the ratio of its density to that of a ²⁰⁸Pb nucleus. (3 marks)
9. Explain why technetium-99m is suitable as a medical tracer. (4 marks)

### Mark scheme

1. Alpha beam at thin gold foil in vacuum; detector at various angles (1); most pass straight through (1): atom mostly empty space (1); a few deflected through large angles / back (1): small, dense, positively charged nucleus (1); containing most of the mass (1).
2. Measure background; GM tube close to source (1); paper causes no drop in count, so no alpha (1); a few mm of aluminium causes a large drop: beta (1); remaining count above background only reduced by thick lead: gamma (1).
3. Alpha is the most strongly ionising (1); all its energy is deposited in a small volume of tissue near the source, damaging cells/DNA (1).
4. Count rate ∝ 1/x², 900/100 = 9, so distance × 3 (1) = 0.30 m (1).
5. Half-life: average time for the number of undecayed nuclei (activity) to halve (1); decay constant: probability per unit time that a nucleus decays (1); N₀/2 = N₀e^(−λT½) (1); ln 2 = λT½ (1).
6. λ = 0.693/(5.27 × 3.16 × 10⁷) = 4.16 × 10⁻⁹ s⁻¹ (1); N = A/λ = 4.8 × 10¹³ (1); moles = N/N_A = 8.0 × 10⁻¹¹ (1); mass = 8.0 × 10⁻¹¹ × 0.060 = 4.8 × 10⁻¹² kg (1).
7. Moves one down (N − 1) and one right (Z + 1) (1); it is above the stability band / has too many neutrons for its protons (1); β⁻ moves it towards the band (1).
8. R = 1.2 × 10⁻¹⁵ × 56^(1/3) = 4.6 × 10⁻¹⁵ m (2); ratio = 1, since density is the same for all nuclei (1).
9. Emits only gamma, which passes out of the body to be detected (1); low ionisation inside the body (1); half-life 6 hours: long enough for the scan, short enough to keep dose low (1); can be combined with different compounds to target organs / made on site (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/01-minerals.md -->

## Minerals, silicate structures and mineral identification

Minerals are the building blocks of rocks. This file covers what a mineral is, how silicate minerals are built, how to identify minerals in hand specimen and thin section, how to measure density and hardness, and how rocks are grouped. The crust's eight main elements and the Goldschmidt classification are in [08](08-origin-and-geochemistry-of-the-earth.md).

### What a mineral is (OCR 2.1.1(a); Eduqas F1.1e)

#### What you need to know

- A mineral is a naturally occurring, inorganic, crystalline element or compound with a composition that can be written as a chemical formula (within a limited range) and an ordered atomic structure.
- Minerals have distinct compositions, structures and physical properties by which they can be identified.
- OCR examples with formulae: native sulfur (S), native copper (Cu), calcite (CaCO₃), quartz (SiO₂), pyrite (FeS₂), galena (PbS). Other formulae need not be recalled.

#### Explained

- Native elements: one element on its own (sulfur, copper, gold, graphite, diamond).
- Compounds: oxides (haematite Fe₂O₃), sulfides (pyrite, galena, chalcopyrite CuFeS₂), carbonates (calcite), sulfates (gypsum CaSO₄·2H₂O, barite BaSO₄), halides (halite NaCl, fluorite CaF₂) and silicates (most rock-forming minerals).
- "Crystalline" means atoms are arranged in a repeating 3D pattern. This pattern controls crystal shape, cleavage and hardness. Glass (obsidian) has no ordered structure, so it is not a mineral.

### Silicate structures (OCR 2.1.1(b); Eduqas F1.1d)

#### What you need to know

- Silicates are the most common rock-forming minerals.
- They are built from silicon-oxygen tetrahedra (SiO₄)⁴⁻: one silicon atom surrounded by four oxygen atoms.
- Tetrahedra may be isolated (olivine, garnet), linked in single chains (pyroxene, for example augite), double chains (amphibole, for example hornblende), sheets (micas, clays) or 3D frameworks (quartz, feldspars).
- Compositions can vary (qualitative only): ions of similar size and charge substitute for one another.

#### Explained

Tetrahedra link by sharing oxygen atoms. The more oxygens shared, the more silica-rich and the more polymerised the structure.

| Structure | Oxygens shared per tetrahedron | Example | Consequence |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Isolated | 0 | Olivine (Mg,Fe)₂SiO₄, garnet | Tetrahedra joined by metal ions (Fe, Mg); dense, no cleavage |
| Single chain | 2 | Pyroxene (augite) | Two cleavages at about 90° |
| Double chain | 2 and 3 alternately | Amphibole (hornblende) | Two cleavages at about 60° and 120° |
| Sheet | 3 | Micas (biotite, muscovite), clays | One perfect cleavage; splits into thin flakes |
| Framework | 4 | Quartz, feldspars | Strong in all directions; quartz has no cleavage, feldspars have two |

Cleavage forms along the weakest bonds, which in silicates are the bonds between the tetrahedral units and metal ions, not the strong Si–O bonds.

Substitution: Fe²⁺ and Mg²⁺ are similar in size, so olivine ranges from Mg-rich to Fe-rich. In feldspars, Na⁺ + Si⁴⁺ substitute for Ca²⁺ + Al³⁺ (plagioclase). More in [15](15-igneous-petrology.md).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying micas cleave because they are soft. They cleave because of the sheet structure.
- Calling quartz a framework of SiO₂ "molecules". It is a continuous network.

### Diagnostic properties and identification (OCR 2.1.1(c)(i), (ii); Eduqas F1.1e, SP1, SP3)

#### What you need to know

- Diagnostic properties: colour, lustre, crystal shape (habit), streak, cleavage or fracture, density (relative density), hardness, reaction with cold dilute (0.5 mol dm⁻³) hydrochloric acid.
- Classify minerals from samples, photographs and thin-section diagrams using their diagnostic properties, and use classification keys and data sheets (Eduqas provides a mineral data sheet in Component 1).
- Eduqas list for recognition: quartz, calcite, feldspars (orthoclase, plagioclase), augite, hornblende, olivine, micas (biotite, muscovite), haematite, galena, pyrite, chalcopyrite, fluorite, barite, halite, gypsum, garnet, chiastolite/andalusite.

#### Explained

| Property | How to test | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Colour | Look | Often unreliable: impurities change it (quartz can be clear, milky, pink, purple) |
| Lustre | How it reflects light | Metallic (galena, pyrite) or non-metallic: vitreous (glassy), pearly, silky, earthy, resinous |
| Habit | Crystal shape | Cubic (halite, pyrite, galena, fluorite), hexagonal prisms (quartz), rhombs (calcite) |
| Streak | Rub on unglazed white porcelain | Colour of powder is constant: haematite red-brown, pyrite greenish-black, galena grey |
| Cleavage / fracture | Look for flat, repeated planes; count and measure angles | Fracture is irregular breakage; quartz has conchoidal (curved) fracture |
| Hardness | Scratch test against Mohs scale objects | Fingernail about 2.5, copper coin about 3.5, steel about 5.5, glass about 5.5 |
| Density | Heft (feel) or measure | Galena and barite feel heavy |
| Acid | Drop of cold dilute HCl | Calcite fizzes (CO₂ released) |

**Key minerals** (summary):

| Mineral | Key features |
| --- | --- |
| Quartz | Hardness 7, glassy, no cleavage, conchoidal fracture, hexagonal prisms |
| Calcite | Hardness 3, rhombic cleavage (three directions), fizzes in cold acid |
| Orthoclase feldspar | Hardness 6, pink or white, two cleavages at 90° |
| Plagioclase feldspar | Hardness 6, white or grey, fine parallel striations on cleavage faces |
| Augite (pyroxene) | Dark green-black, two cleavages near 90°, stubby crystals |
| Hornblende (amphibole) | Dark, elongate crystals, cleavages at about 60°/120° |
| Olivine | Olive green, glassy, granular, no cleavage |
| Biotite | Black mica, one perfect cleavage, thin flexible sheets |
| Muscovite | Silvery-colourless mica, one perfect cleavage |
| Haematite | Red-brown streak, metallic or earthy, dense |
| Galena | Grey, metallic, cubic, three cleavages at 90°, very dense (about 7.5) |
| Pyrite | Brassy yellow, cubic, hardness 6 to 6.5, greenish-black streak |
| Chalcopyrite | Brassy yellow (often tarnished), softer than pyrite (3.5 to 4), greenish-black streak |
| Fluorite | Cubic, four cleavages (octahedral), hardness 4, often purple or green |
| Barite | White, very dense for a non-metal (about 4.5), tabular crystals |
| Halite | Cubic, three cleavages at 90°, salty taste, hardness 2.5 |
| Gypsum | Hardness 2 (scratched by fingernail), one perfect cleavage |
| Garnet | Twelve-sided rounded crystals, red-brown, hardness 7 to 7.5, no cleavage |
| Chiastolite / andalusite | Elongate crystals; chiastolite shows a dark cross in cross-section |

**Thin section**: in a polarising microscope, minerals are identified by colour in plane-polarised light (pleochroism: biotite changes colour as the stage turns), relief, cleavage, and interference colours and extinction under crossed polars. Quartz is clear with low relief and grey-white interference colours; olivine shows bright interference colours and cracks; plagioclase shows stripes (twinning).

A classification key asks yes/no questions in order (for example "Does it fizz in acid?", "Is it harder than glass?") to reach a name.

### Measuring density and hardness (OCR 2.1.1(c)(iii), (iv); Eduqas SP2)

#### What you need to know

- Practical investigations of density and hardness: the Mohs hardness test and the density test.
- Techniques to measure mass, length and volume.

#### Explained

**Mohs scale** (relative scratch hardness): 1 talc, 2 gypsum, 3 calcite, 4 fluorite, 5 apatite, 6 orthoclase, 7 quartz, 8 topaz, 9 corundum, 10 diamond. A mineral scratches anything softer than itself. The scale is ordinal: diamond is far more than ten times harder than talc.

**Density** = mass ÷ volume.

1. Measure mass on a balance (dry sample).
2. Measure volume by displacement: lower the sample into water in a measuring cylinder (or a Eureka can) and record the volume change; or weigh the sample suspended in water (apparent loss of mass = mass of water displaced, numerically equal to the volume in cm³).
3. Relative density = density of mineral ÷ density of water.

Improve accuracy: use a larger sample (smaller percentage uncertainty); remove air bubbles; use a cylinder with fine graduations; repeat and average.

#### Worked example

A galena sample has mass 45.0 g. Water level rises from 50.0 cm³ to 56.0 cm³. Density = 45.0 ÷ 6.0 = 7.5 g cm⁻³. If the cylinder reads to ±0.5 cm³, each reading carries ±0.5, so the volume uncertainty is ±1.0 cm³ (17%), a large uncertainty; a balance-in-water method would be better.

#### Practical

**PAG1 / SP1, SP2, SP3**: identify unknown minerals using a property table and key, testing streak, hardness (fingernail, coin, steel, glass), acid reaction and density. Safety: wear eye protection with acid; handle sharp specimens carefully.

### Rocks and the three rock classes (OCR 2.1.1(d); Eduqas F1.1f, F1.1g, SP4)

#### What you need to know

- Rocks are aggregates of one or more minerals, fragments of earlier rocks, or fossils.
- Igneous, sedimentary and metamorphic rocks differ in composition and texture, which reflect their origin, and can be related to temperature and pressure in the rock cycle.
- Eduqas: interpret composition, colour and texture (crystalline or clastic; crystal or grain size and shape; sorting; foliation; mineral alignment; bedding; banding) to decide the origin. Produce scaled, annotated drawings using a hand lens or microscope (SP4).

#### Explained

| Feature | Igneous | Sedimentary | Metamorphic |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Texture | Interlocking crystals, random orientation | Usually grains (clastic) with cement; may have fossils | Interlocking crystals, often aligned (foliation) or granular |
| Structure | Massive; may have vesicles | Bedding, laminations, cross-bedding | Foliation, banding |
| Formation conditions | Crystallised from melt at high temperature | Formed at surface temperature and pressure | Recrystallised in the solid state at raised temperature and/or pressure |

Magnification = size of image ÷ size of real object. On a drawing, include a scale bar.

#### Worked example

A drawing of a crystal is 36 mm long; the real crystal is 4.5 mm. Magnification = 36 ÷ 4.5 = ×8.

#### Common mistakes

- Using colour as the main test.
- Confusing streak (colour of powder) with colour.
- Saying a mineral with hardness 6 cannot be scratched by anything at hardness 5.5 or below; correct, but it can be scratched by quartz (7).

### Check questions

1. Define the term mineral. (2)
2. Explain why mica has one perfect cleavage but quartz has none. (3)
3. Describe tests that would distinguish calcite from quartz. (3)
4. A brassy yellow cubic mineral could be pyrite or chalcopyrite. Suggest two tests to tell them apart. (2)
5. A mineral sample has mass 18.4 g. Suspended in water its apparent mass is 13.7 g. Calculate its density and suggest, with a reason, whether it could be barite (4.5 g cm⁻³) or quartz (2.65 g cm⁻³). (3)
6. Explain why the Mohs scale is described as a relative scale. (2)
7. Describe how the texture of a rock can be used to decide whether it is igneous or sedimentary. (3)
8. A scale drawing of a garnet crystal is 54 mm across at a magnification of ×12. Calculate the real size. (1)

### Mark scheme

1. Naturally occurring, inorganic, crystalline solid (1); definite chemical composition / formula (1).
2. Mica has a sheet structure (1); weak bonds between sheets so splits parallel to them (1); quartz is a 3D framework with strong Si–O bonds in all directions so no plane of weakness (1).
3. Calcite fizzes in dilute HCl, quartz does not (1); calcite scratched by a steel knife / hardness 3, quartz scratches glass / hardness 7 (1); calcite has rhombic cleavage, quartz conchoidal fracture (1).
4. Hardness: pyrite harder (about 6), scratches glass / not scratched by knife; chalcopyrite softer (3.5 to 4) (1); chalcopyrite often tarnished iridescent / pyrite forms cubes (1).
5. Mass of water displaced = 4.7 g so volume 4.7 cm³ (1); density = 18.4 ÷ 4.7 = 3.9 g cm⁻³ (1); closer to barite but lower, so possibly impure barite, not quartz (1).
6. Minerals are ranked only by which scratches which (1); intervals between numbers are not equal (1).
7. Igneous: interlocking crystals (1); sedimentary: separate grains held by cement / pore space (1); sedimentary may show bedding / fossils / rounded grains (1).
8. 54 ÷ 12 = 4.5 mm (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/02-igneous-rocks.md -->

## Igneous rocks: classification and textures

Igneous rocks form when magma or lava cools and crystallises. Composition tells you about the source of the magma; crystal size, shape and texture tell you how and where it cooled. Magma generation and igneous bodies are in [10](10-magma-intrusion-and-volcanism.md); magma evolution is in [15](15-igneous-petrology.md).

### Classification by composition and crystal size (OCR 2.1.2(a); Eduqas F2.2b)

#### What you need to know

- Composition groups: silicic (felsic, acid), intermediate, mafic (basic), ultramafic.
- Crystal size boundaries. OCR: coarse > 5 mm, medium 1 to 5 mm, fine < 1 mm. Eduqas: coarse > 3 mm, medium 1 to 3 mm, fine < 1 mm. Use the boundaries for your board.
- OCR rocks: granite, microgranite, rhyolite; diorite, microdiorite, andesite; gabbro, dolerite, basalt; peridotite. Eduqas rocks: granite (silicic); gabbro, dolerite, basalt (mafic); peridotite (ultramafic).
- Identify igneous rocks in hand specimen, photographs and thin-section diagrams from their diagnostic properties.

#### Explained

| | Coarse (plutonic) | Medium (hypabyssal) | Fine (volcanic) | Main minerals | Colour |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Silicic (> 66% SiO₂) | Granite | Microgranite | Rhyolite | Quartz, orthoclase, Na-plagioclase, muscovite, biotite | Pale (pink, white, grey) |
| Intermediate (52 to 66%) | Diorite | Microdiorite | Andesite | Plagioclase, hornblende, some biotite, little quartz | Medium grey, speckled |
| Mafic (45 to 52%) | Gabbro | Dolerite | Basalt | Ca-plagioclase, pyroxene (augite), olivine | Dark grey to black |
| Ultramafic (< 45%) | Peridotite | | | Olivine, pyroxene | Dark green |

Colour index: the percentage of dark (ferromagnesian) minerals. Silicic rocks are pale (low colour index); mafic rocks are dark. Ultramafic rocks have the highest density, silicic the lowest.

Name an igneous rock by first judging crystal size (with a hand lens and scale), then estimating composition from colour and minerals.

### Textures, crystal shape and cooling history (OCR 2.1.2(b); Eduqas F2.2b, SP8, SP9)

#### What you need to know

- Texture, crystal shape and crystal size are evidence of the depth of formation and the rate of cooling.
- Textures: equicrystalline (equigranular), glassy, vesicular, amygdaloidal, flow banding, porphyritic; Eduqas also fragmental (tuff).
- Crystal shape: euhedral (perfect faces), subhedral (some faces), anhedral (no faces).
- Structures (Eduqas): pillow structure, aa and pahoehoe surfaces, columnar joints.
- Draw and annotate textures; measure temperature (OCR, linked to cooling experiments).

#### Explained

**Cooling rate and crystal size**: crystals grow as atoms move through the melt and attach to growing crystals. Slow cooling (deep plutons insulated by surrounding rock) gives time for few, large crystals. Fast cooling (lava at the surface) produces many nuclei and little time for growth, so small crystals. Instant quenching (lava into water, or very viscous silicic lava) gives glass with no crystals (obsidian).

| Texture | Description | Interpretation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Equicrystalline | Crystals all about the same size | Cooled at a steady rate |
| Porphyritic | Large crystals (phenocrysts) in a finer groundmass | Two-stage cooling: slow at depth, then faster after rising |
| Glassy | No crystals, conchoidal fracture | Very rapid cooling (obsidian) |
| Vesicular | Gas holes (vesicles) | Gas came out of solution as pressure fell near the surface; lava |
| Amygdaloidal | Vesicles filled later with minerals (calcite, quartz, zeolites) | Lava altered by later fluids |
| Flow banding | Bands of different colour or crystal content | Viscous lava flowing (rhyolite) |
| Fragmental (tuff) | Broken crystals, rock and glass fragments | Pyroclastic: explosive eruption deposit |

**Crystal shape**: the first minerals to crystallise grow freely in the melt, so they are euhedral. Later minerals fill the spaces between, so they are anhedral. In granite, biotite and feldspar may be euhedral to subhedral while quartz, crystallising last, is anhedral. Shape therefore shows the order of crystallisation.

**Structures**:

- Pillow lava: rounded, bulbous masses formed when lava erupts under water and the surface chills immediately; shows submarine eruption, and the shape of the pillows can show way-up.
- Pahoehoe: smooth, ropy surface of fluid basalt lava; aa: rough, clinkery, spiny surface of cooler, more viscous basalt.
- Columnar joints: polygonal (often hexagonal) columns formed by contraction as a lava flow or sill cools evenly; columns are perpendicular to the cooling surfaces.

#### Worked example

A thin section shows plagioclase phenocrysts up to 4 mm in a groundmass of crystals less than 0.1 mm, with vesicles. Interpretation: porphyritic and vesicular, so the magma partly crystallised slowly at depth (phenocrysts), then erupted as lava, cooled quickly (fine groundmass) and degassed near the surface (vesicles). If the groundmass is dark with plagioclase, augite and olivine, the rock is a porphyritic basalt.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG3 (crystalline processes)**: investigate crystal size against cooling rate using salol (phenyl salicylate). Melt salol and place drops on microscope slides at different temperatures (warm, room temperature, chilled on ice). Measure slide temperature with a thermometer or probe; observe crystal size with a microscope or hand lens with a scale. Colder slides give smaller crystals. Safety: salol irritates eyes; avoid hot surfaces.

**Eduqas SP8 and SP9**: full rock descriptions of igneous hand specimens and field exposures (colour, crystal size, shape, minerals, texture, structure) and identification of minerals and textures from photomicrographs to deduce cooling history.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying porphyritic rocks cooled slowly. They had two cooling rates.
- Calling dolerite "medium-grained basalt" without naming it.
- Saying vesicles mean the rock is a lava. Shallow intrusions can be vesicular too, though it is uncommon; vesicles usually indicate lava.

### Check questions

1. Name the coarse-grained and fine-grained equivalents of dolerite. (2)
2. Explain why granite is pale but gabbro is dark. (2)
3. Explain why crystals in a plutonic rock are larger than those in a lava. (3)
4. Describe and explain a porphyritic texture. (3)
5. In a granite, feldspar crystals are euhedral and quartz is anhedral. Explain what this shows. (2)
6. Explain the formation of columnar joints. (3)
7. A rock is fine-grained, dark grey and contains vesicles filled with white calcite. Name the rock and the texture, and outline its history. (4)
8. Describe how you would use salol to investigate the effect of cooling rate on crystal size, and name two variables to control. (5)

### Mark scheme

1. Gabbro (1); basalt (1).
2. Granite is silicic / rich in quartz and feldspar, which are pale (1); gabbro is mafic / rich in ferromagnesian minerals (pyroxene, olivine) which are dark (1).
3. Plutonic rocks cool slowly at depth, insulated by country rock (1); ions have time to migrate to existing crystals (1); fewer nuclei so fewer, larger crystals (1).
4. Large crystals (phenocrysts) in a fine-grained groundmass (1); phenocrysts grew slowly at depth (1); groundmass formed when magma rose and cooled quickly (1).
5. Feldspar crystallised first, growing freely (1); quartz crystallised last, filling remaining space (1).
6. Lava flow / sill cools and contracts (1); tension cracks form in a polygonal pattern (1); cracks grow perpendicular to cooling surfaces giving columns (1).
7. Basalt (1); amygdaloidal (1); lava cooled quickly at the surface / degassed forming vesicles (1); later fluids deposited calcite in the vesicles (1).
8. Melt salol, put drops on slides at different temperatures (1); measure temperature (1); observe and measure crystal size with microscope and scale (1); repeat (1); control: drop volume, slide type, melt temperature (any two for 1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/03-sedimentary-rocks-weathering-and-diagenesis.md -->

## Sedimentary rocks: weathering, transport, classification and diagenesis

Sedimentary rocks record surface processes driven by external (solar) energy. This file follows sediment from weathering through transport and deposition to lithification, and covers how to classify and describe sedimentary rocks. Sedimentary environments and structures are in [12](12-uniformitarianism-facies-and-sedimentary-environments.md); turbidites, Stokes' law and flume work in [13](13-turbidites-deltas-and-banded-iron-formations.md).

### External energy (Eduqas F2.1a)

Solar heating drives the water cycle (evaporation, rain, rivers, glaciers), winds and waves, and so drives weathering and erosion. Gravity moves material downhill. This is the "surface" half of the rock cycle; internal heat drives the other half.

### Weathering (OCR 2.1.3(a); Eduqas F2.1b)

#### What you need to know

- Physical (mechanical) weathering breaks rock into fragments: insolation (heating and cooling), freeze-thaw.
- Chemical weathering of silicate and carbonate rocks: hydrolysis, carbonation (and solution), oxidation; it produces new minerals (clays), solutions and resistant residual minerals.
- Biological weathering combines physical and chemical changes.
- Products: clastic material, clays, evaporites (gypsum, halite) and carbonate sediment. Recognise a mineral in a given equation (OCR: no need to recall formulae).

#### Explained

**Physical**:

- Freeze-thaw: water in cracks expands by about 9% on freezing, wedging the rock apart; repeated cycles in cold, wet climates produce angular scree.
- Insolation: in deserts, daily heating and cooling expand and contract the outer layers and different minerals by different amounts, so the rock flakes or crumbles.

**Chemical**:

- Hydrolysis: water (with H⁺ ions) reacts with silicates such as feldspar, producing clay minerals (kaolinite) and ions in solution. Feldspar in granite weathers to clay, which is why granite breaks down into sand-sized quartz grains plus clay.
- Carbonation and solution: rainwater with dissolved CO₂ forms weak carbonic acid, which dissolves calcite: CaCO₃ + H₂O + CO₂ → Ca²⁺ + 2HCO₃⁻. Limestone is removed in solution.
- Oxidation: iron in minerals (pyroxene, olivine, biotite) reacts with oxygen, forming iron oxides (red-brown staining).
- Quartz is very resistant, so it survives as a residual mineral.

**Biological**: roots wedge cracks open (physical); burrowing animals expose rock; lichens and decaying organic matter release acids (chemical).

**Climate control**: chemical weathering is fastest in hot, wet climates; physical weathering dominates in cold or dry climates.

**Products**: clastic grains (rock fragments, quartz); clay minerals; ions in solution, which may later precipitate as evaporites (halite, gypsum) when water evaporates, or be used by organisms to build carbonate shells (carbonate sediment).

### Erosion, transport and deposition (OCR 2.1.3(b)(i); Eduqas F2.1c)

#### What you need to know

- Erosion (abrasion, attrition); transport (traction, saltation, suspension, solution).
- The Hjulström curve; length of transport; maturity.
- Deposition sorts sediment: grain size relates to the energy of the environment; quartz and muscovite dominate coarse fractions and clay minerals the fine fraction; flocculation; precipitation.

#### Explained

- Abrasion: particles carried by water, wind or ice scrape the bed. Attrition: particles knock into each other, becoming smaller and rounder.
- Traction: large grains roll and slide along the bed. Saltation: sand-sized grains bounce. Suspension: fine silt and clay are carried within the flow. Solution: dissolved ions.

**Hjulström curve** (velocity against grain size, log scales):

- The erosion curve shows the velocity needed to pick up a grain. It is lowest for fine sand (about 0.1 to 0.5 mm). Clay needs a higher velocity because clay particles are cohesive and lie smoothly on the bed.
- The settling (deposition) curve shows the velocity below which a moving grain is deposited. Fine clay stays in suspension at very low velocities.
- Between the curves, grains already moving keep moving (transport).

**Maturity**: the longer and more energetic the transport, the more the sediment is sorted, rounded and enriched in resistant minerals.

| | Immature | Mature |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Grain shape | Angular | Rounded |
| Sorting | Poor (mixed sizes) | Good (similar sizes) |
| Composition | Many minerals, including feldspar and rock fragments | Mostly quartz |
| Matrix | Much clay matrix | Little matrix |
| Example | Breccia, greywacke, arkose | Orthoquartzite |

**Flocculation**: clay particles carry negative charges and repel each other in fresh water. In salt water, positive ions neutralise the charge, so particles clump into larger flocs that settle quickly, for example in estuaries.

**Precipitation**: dissolved ions crystallise out when water evaporates (evaporites) or when conditions change (carbonate precipitation in warm, shallow seas).

### Grain size and sieve analysis (OCR 2.1.3(b)(ii), (c); Eduqas F2.1d, F2.1e)

#### What you need to know

- Grain size classes: gravel (> 2 mm), sand (1/16 to 2 mm), silt (1/256 to 1/16 mm), clay (< 1/256 mm). Eduqas: coarse > 2 mm, fine < 1/16 mm.
- Sieve analysis; the phi scale (at AS, qualitative only for OCR); Folk and Ward formulae need not be recalled.
- Use of sediment comparators to judge size, shape (roundness) and sphericity.
- Statistics: frequency tables, histograms, cumulative frequency, mean, median, mode, standard deviation, interquartile range, normal and skewed distributions.

#### Explained

**Phi scale**: φ = −log₂(D/D₀), where D is grain diameter in mm and D₀ = 1 mm. Each whole phi step halves or doubles the size: 1 mm = 0 φ, 2 mm = −1 φ, 0.5 mm = 1 φ, 0.25 mm = 2 φ, 1/16 mm = 4 φ. Coarse grains have negative phi. It converts a geometric scale to equal steps, so sieve data plot neatly.

**Sieve analysis**:

1. Dry and weigh a sample (disaggregate gently).
2. Shake through a stack of sieves with mesh sizes decreasing downwards (for example 2, 1, 0.5, 0.25, 0.125, 0.063 mm) for a fixed time.
3. Weigh the sediment retained on each sieve.
4. Calculate the percentage mass in each class; draw a histogram and a cumulative frequency curve.
5. Read median (φ50) and spread (for example φ84 − φ16) from the curve. Well-sorted sediment has a narrow spread (steep cumulative curve).

**Skewness**: beach sands tend to lose fines (negatively skewed, tail towards coarse grains); river and dune sands differ. Dune sands are very well sorted and fine to medium; river sands less well sorted.

#### Worked example

A 200 g sample: 2 mm sieve 6 g, 1 mm 14 g, 0.5 mm 52 g, 0.25 mm 98 g, 0.125 mm 24 g, pan 6 g. Percentages: 3, 7, 26, 49, 12, 3. Modal class: 0.25 to 0.5 mm (1 to 2 φ, medium sand). Most of the sample is in two classes, so it is moderately well sorted.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG4 / Eduqas F2.1e**: grain size analysis of sands from contrasting environments (river, beach, dune). Control sample mass, shaking time and sieve set; repeat; compare with a statistical test (for example a t-test or Mann-Whitney U on mean grain size). Safety: dust (mask), heavy sieve stacks.

### Classifying sedimentary rocks (OCR 2.1.3(d); Eduqas F2.1e, F2.1f, SP5, SP7)

#### What you need to know

- OCR: siliciclastic rocks classified on colour, composition, grain size, grain shape and sorting (orthoquartzite, arkose, greywacke); carbonates by grain size, cement, composition, fossil content and sorting using the Dunham scheme (mudstone, wackestone, packstone, grainstone).
- Eduqas: identify sandstones (orthoquartzite, arkose, greywacke), shale/mudstone, limestones (shelly, oolitic, chalk), conglomerate, breccia; organic sedimentary rocks (limestone, coal) and chemical rocks (evaporites); analyse biogenic components.
- Describe macro and micro features from hand specimens, exposures and photomicrographs.

#### Explained

**Siliciclastic rocks**:

| Rock | Grain size | Description | Origin |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Breccia | > 2 mm | Angular clasts, poorly sorted | Little transport: scree, fault zones, alluvial fans |
| Conglomerate | > 2 mm | Rounded pebbles in sand matrix | High-energy rivers, beaches |
| Orthoquartzite | Sand | Over 90% quartz, well rounded and sorted, silica cement, pale | Mature: beaches, deserts, after long transport |
| Arkose | Sand | Over 25% feldspar, angular, pink or red, poorly sorted | Rapid erosion of granite, arid climate, short transport |
| Greywacke | Sand with > 15% clay matrix | Dark, poorly sorted, angular, rock fragments | Turbidity currents (rapid deposition) |
| Mudstone / shale | < 1/16 mm | Fine; shale is fissile (splits into thin layers) | Quiet water: deep sea, lakes, lagoons |

**Carbonate rocks**: limestones are mostly calcite. Dunham classification is based on texture: is the rock mud-supported or grain-supported?

| Dunham name | Texture |
| --- | --- |
| Mudstone (carbonate) | Mud-supported, less than 10% grains |
| Wackestone | Mud-supported, more than 10% grains |
| Packstone | Grain-supported, with mud between grains |
| Grainstone | Grain-supported, no mud (spaces filled by calcite cement) |

Mud content shows energy: grainstones form where currents winnow away mud (high energy); mudstones in quiet water.

Common types: shelly (bioclastic) limestone of fossil fragments; oolitic limestone of ooids (spherical grains built up in concentric layers by rolling in warm, shallow, agitated water); chalk of microscopic coccoliths (deep, quiet, clear water); reef limestone of corals in growth position.

**Organic and chemical rocks**: coal forms from plant material buried in swamps (see the Eduqas coal file, [Eduqas 01](../geology-eduqas-a480qs/01-economic-minerals-coal-and-bulk-materials.md)). Evaporites (halite, gypsum, anhydrite) precipitate when sea or lake water evaporates in arid climates.

### Diagenesis, lithification, porosity and permeability (OCR 2.1.3(e); Eduqas F2.1g)

#### What you need to know

- Diagenesis: all physical and chemical changes after deposition at low temperature and pressure; lithification turns sediment into rock.
- Mechanical compaction; chemical compaction by pressure solution and recrystallisation; growth of cements (silica, calcite, haematite, clay minerals).
- Effects on siliciclastic and carbonate grains, organic material and mud; how these change porosity and permeability.
- Texture (grain angularity, sphericity, size, sorting) controls porosity and permeability, reflecting source rock, climate and processes.

#### Explained

1. **Mechanical compaction**: the weight of overlying sediment squeezes grains closer and drives out water. Muds lose most volume (from about 60 to 80% water to about 10 to 20%), turning into mudstone and shale with aligned clay flakes.
2. **Pressure solution (chemical compaction)**: at grain contacts the pressure is highest, so minerals dissolve there and reprecipitate in pore spaces. Grains interlock (sutured contacts); stylolites form in limestones.
3. **Cementation**: minerals precipitate from pore water and bind grains: silica (quartz overgrowths), calcite, haematite (red cement in desert sandstones), clay minerals.
4. **Recrystallisation**: unstable minerals change to stable ones (aragonite shells to calcite).
5. Organic material: buried plant material and plankton change to peat, lignite and coal, or to kerogen and then oil and gas, with rising temperature.

**Porosity** = volume of pore space ÷ total volume × 100. **Permeability** is the ability to let fluids flow through, needing connected pores. Well-sorted, rounded grains have high porosity (packing leaves large pores); poorly sorted sediment has small grains filling spaces between large ones, so porosity is low. Compaction and cementation both reduce porosity and permeability. Clay has high porosity but very low permeability because its pores are tiny. Detail and Darcy's law are in [14](14-fluids-in-rocks-and-hydrogeology.md).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying clay is eroded at the lowest velocities. It needs higher velocities to erode than sand, but settles only at very low velocities.
- Mixing up roundness (corner sharpness) and sphericity (how close to a sphere).
- Saying mature sediment is old. Maturity refers to how much it has been weathered and transported.

### Check questions

1. Explain how hydrolysis of feldspar in granite leads to the formation of a quartz sand and a clay. (3)
2. Using the Hjulström curve, explain why sand is eroded at lower velocities than clay. (3)
3. Convert 0.125 mm to phi units. (1)
4. A sandstone is pink, poorly sorted, with angular quartz and 35% feldspar grains. Name it and deduce its source and climate. (4)
5. Distinguish between a packstone and a grainstone, and explain what each shows about energy. (3)
6. Describe three processes of diagenesis that turn sand into sandstone. (3)
7. Explain why a well-sorted sand has a higher porosity than a poorly sorted sand. (2)
8. Explain why clay particles settle faster in an estuary than in a river. (3)

### Mark scheme

1. Carbonic acid / H⁺ reacts with feldspar (1); feldspar converted to clay minerals (kaolinite) and ions in solution (1); quartz resistant so released as sand grains (1).
2. Clay particles cohesive / electrostatically bonded (1); smooth bed, particles lie within the boundary layer (1); sand grains not cohesive so lifted at lower velocity (1).
3. φ = −log₂(0.125) = 3 φ (1).
4. Arkose (1); feldspar unweathered so short transport / rapid erosion (1); granite source (feldspar and quartz) (1); arid climate, little chemical weathering, red / pink (1).
5. Both grain-supported (1); packstone has mud between grains, grainstone has none / cement (1); grainstone: higher energy winnowing away mud (1).
6. Compaction (1); pressure solution at grain contacts (1); cementation by silica / calcite / haematite (1).
7. Grains similar in size so pore spaces not filled (1); in poorly sorted sand small grains fill spaces between large ones (1).
8. Clay particles negatively charged and repel in fresh water (1); salt water ions neutralise charges (1); particles clump (flocculate) into larger flocs which settle faster (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/04-metamorphic-rocks.md -->

## Metamorphic rocks

Metamorphic rocks form when an existing (parent) rock adjusts to new temperature and/or pressure conditions without melting. The shale-to-gneiss series shows the main ideas. Facies, isograds, P-T paths and advanced fabrics are in [17](17-metamorphic-petrology.md).

### Metamorphism (OCR 2.1.4(a); Eduqas F2.2f)

#### What you need to know

- Metamorphism is a solid-state, isochemical process: the rock recrystallises and new minerals grow, but the overall chemical composition stays the same (apart from water and CO₂).
- Types: contact (thermal), regional and dynamic.
- It is a response to changes in temperature and/or pressure; Eduqas: interpret contact aureoles and metamorphic foliations on maps and photographs.

#### Explained

Minerals are stable only over a range of temperature and pressure. When conditions change, atoms rearrange into minerals stable in the new conditions. This happens in the solid state, by diffusion, often helped by small amounts of fluid. If the rock melts, it becomes igneous instead.

| Type | Main cause | Setting | Scale | Typical texture |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Contact (thermal) | High temperature, low pressure | Around igneous intrusions | Metres to a few kilometres (the aureole) | Non-foliated, granular, hard, may be spotted |
| Regional | Raised temperature and directed pressure | Deep in mountain belts at convergent boundaries | Hundreds to thousands of square kilometres | Foliated |
| Dynamic | High shear stress, often at lower temperature | Fault and shear zones | Narrow zones | Crushed, sheared (mylonite) |

**Contact aureole**: the zone of metamorphosed country rock around an intrusion. Grade is highest at the contact and falls outwards. Width depends on the size and temperature of the intrusion, the country rock type and fluids. On a map, an aureole is a band of metamorphic rock around a granite, wider where the contact dips gently.

### Metamorphic rocks and their parents (OCR 2.1.4(b); Eduqas F2.2g, SP10, SP11)

#### What you need to know

- OCR: metaquartzite and marble; slate, phyllite, schist and gneiss from fine-grained rocks.
- Eduqas: marble, metaquartzite, spotted rock, hornfels, slate, schist, gneiss.
- Non-foliated textures in contact metamorphism; foliation (slaty cleavage, schistosity, gneissose banding) in regional metamorphism.
- Use mineralogy and fabric to infer the parent rock.

#### Explained

**Non-foliated rocks**:

| Rock | Parent | Features |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Marble | Limestone | Interlocking calcite crystals (sugary), fizzes in acid, fossils destroyed, hardness 3 |
| Metaquartzite | Quartz sandstone (orthoquartzite) | Interlocking quartz, very hard, breaks through grains not round them |
| Spotted rock | Mudstone or shale, outer aureole | Dark spots of new minerals (for example cordierite, andalusite) growing in the original rock |
| Hornfels | Mudstone or shale, inner aureole | Very hard, fine-grained, splintery, dark, no foliation; may contain andalusite or chiastolite |

**Foliated rocks from mudstone (pelite)**, in order of increasing grade:

| Rock | Grain size | Foliation | Key minerals |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Slate | Very fine | Slaty cleavage: splits into thin flat sheets | Tiny aligned clays, chlorite, white mica |
| Phyllite | Fine | Shiny, wavy cleavage surfaces (sheen) | Fine micas |
| Schist | Medium to coarse | Schistosity: visible aligned micas, wavy | Mica, quartz, garnet porphyroblasts |
| Gneiss | Coarse | Gneissose banding: alternating light (quartz, feldspar) and dark (biotite, hornblende) bands | Feldspar, quartz, biotite |
