**Foliation** forms when platy and elongate minerals (micas, chlorite) grow perpendicular to the greatest stress. In slate the cleavage may cut across the original bedding at any angle, which shows it is caused by stress not deposition.

**Inferring the parent**: metamorphism is isochemical, so the minerals show the chemistry. Calcite only means limestone; quartz only means sandstone; aluminium-rich minerals (micas, garnet, andalusite, kyanite) mean mudstone; hornblende and plagioclase with no quartz mean a basalt or dolerite (forming amphibolite).

### Metamorphic grade and index minerals (OCR 2.1.4(c); Eduqas F2.2g)

#### What you need to know

- As the intensity of metamorphism (grade) increases, different minerals form; index minerals can be used to reconstruct the conditions.

#### Explained

Grade is the intensity of metamorphism, mainly temperature. In regionally metamorphosed mudstones, the sequence of index minerals with increasing grade is: chlorite, biotite, garnet, (staurolite), kyanite, sillimanite. In contact aureoles in mudstone, andalusite (chiastolite) and cordierite are typical. The first appearance of an index mineral on a map is joined to form an isograd (see [17](17-metamorphic-petrology.md)).

Low grade: fine-grained, many hydrous minerals (chlorite, clays). High grade: coarse grains, fewer hydrous minerals (water driven off), foliation becomes banding.

#### Worked example

Moving away from a granite contact into mudstone, a student records: hornfels with andalusite at 0 to 150 m; spotted rock at 150 to 400 m; unaltered mudstone beyond 400 m. The aureole is 400 m wide; grade decreases outward because temperature fell with distance from the hot intrusion.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG1 / Eduqas SP10, SP11**: full description of metamorphic hand specimens and field exposures (colour, crystal size, minerals, fabric) and identification of minerals and textures from photomicrographs, to name the rock and deduce temperature and pressure conditions.

#### Common mistakes

- Calling slate a sedimentary rock because it is fine and splits. Slaty cleavage is metamorphic.
- Saying metamorphism involves melting.
- Confusing cleavage in minerals with slaty cleavage in rocks.

### Check questions

1. Explain what is meant by an isochemical process. (2)
2. Compare contact and regional metamorphism. (4)
3. Name the metamorphic rock formed from limestone and give two features to identify it. (3)
4. Explain how slaty cleavage forms. (3)
5. Describe the changes as a mudstone is metamorphosed through increasing grade from slate to gneiss. (4)
6. A metamorphic rock contains hornblende and plagioclase but no quartz or mica. Suggest its parent rock and explain. (2)
7. Explain why the width of a contact aureole varies around an intrusion. (3)

### Mark scheme

1. Overall chemical composition of the rock does not change (1); only mineralogy / texture changes (apart from volatiles) (1).
2. Contact: heat from an intrusion, little pressure (1); local aureole, non-foliated (1). Regional: high temperature and directed pressure in mountain belts (1); large areas, foliated (1).
3. Marble (1); any two: interlocking calcite crystals / sugary texture; fizzes in dilute acid; scratched by steel / hardness 3; no fossils (2).
4. Compression / directed pressure (1); platy minerals (clays, micas) grow or rotate (1); aligned perpendicular to maximum stress so rock splits along them (1).
5. Grain size increases (1); slate fine cleavage, phyllite sheen, schist visible micas (1); new minerals appear, e.g. biotite, garnet (1); gneiss shows segregated light and dark bands (1).
6. Basalt / dolerite / mafic igneous rock (1); minerals rich in Ca, Fe, Mg and no free quartz, matching mafic composition (1).
7. Size / temperature of intrusion varies (1); dip of contact: gentle dip gives wider outcrop of aureole at the surface (1); country rock type / fluids differ (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/05-fossils-and-the-fossil-record.md -->

## Fossils and the fossil record

Fossils are evidence of past life preserved in rocks. They tell us about ancient organisms, how they lived and the environments where they lived, and they are the basis of relative dating ([06](06-geological-time-and-dating.md)). Evolution of specific groups is in [23](23-evolution-and-applied-palaeontology.md).

### Body and trace fossils; preservation (OCR 2.2.1(a); Eduqas F3.1f)

#### What you need to know

- Fossils are the preserved remains of organisms (body fossils) or traces of their activity (trace fossils: burrows, tracks, trails; Eduqas also coprolites).
- Modes of preservation: actual remains, unaltered hard parts, petrification by replacement (calcification, silicification, pyritisation), carbonisation, moulds and casts.

#### Explained

| Mode | What happens | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Actual remains | Soft tissue preserved by freezing, amber or tar | Insects in amber; mammoths in permafrost |
| Unaltered hard parts | Original shell or bone kept | Young bivalve shells |
| Replacement (petrification) | Original material dissolved and replaced molecule by molecule by another mineral, keeping detail | Calcite (calcification), silica (silicification, for example petrified wood), pyrite (pyritisation, in oxygen-poor muds, for example pyritised ammonites) |
| Carbonisation | Volatiles driven off under pressure, leaving a thin carbon film | Plant leaves, graptolites |
| Mould | Shell dissolves leaving its impression; external mould (outside), internal mould (infill of the inside) | Bivalve moulds in sandstone |
| Cast | A mould later filled by sediment or mineral | Cast of a shell |

Trace fossils are formed in place, so they cannot be transported without being destroyed. This makes them reliable evidence of the environment where the organism lived.

### The fossil record: bias and reliability (OCR 2.2.1(b); Eduqas F3.1g, F3.1h)

#### What you need to know

- Taphonomy: what happens to an organism between death and discovery.
- Life assemblages (preserved where they lived, without much transport) and death assemblages (transported before burial); derived fossils (eroded from older rock and redeposited).
- Preservation potential; the record is biased (towards marine organisms with resistant hard parts living in low-energy environments and buried rapidly) and incomplete (predation, scavenging, bacterial decay, diagenesis, weathering, erosion, metamorphism destroy evidence).
- Morphological definition of species (species recognised from their form, since breeding cannot be tested).

#### Explained

**Preservation potential** is high when: the organism has hard parts (shell, bone, wood); it lives where sediment accumulates (seas, lakes); burial is rapid (protects from scavengers and decay); conditions are anoxic (slow decay). Most soft-bodied organisms and land animals in eroding upland areas are rarely preserved. Exceptional deposits that preserve soft parts are Lagerstätten ([25](25-lagerstatten.md)).

| Life assemblage | Death assemblage |
| --- | --- |
| Fossils in growth or life position (burrowers upright in burrows, corals attached) | Fossils broken, worn, sorted by size, or jumbled |
| Range of ages and sizes | Often similar sizes (sorted by currents) |
| Bivalves with both valves together, closed | Valves separated; may be concave-up or convex-up depending on current |
| Delicate parts intact | Fragments, possibly from different environments mixed |
| Low energy | Higher energy, transport |

**Derived fossils** are older fossils eroded and redeposited in younger sediment. They can give a false (too old) age.

**Morphological species**: palaeontologists define fossil species by shape (morphology). This may split one variable species into several, or join different species that look alike.

### Fossil groups (OCR 2.2.2(b) basic identification; Eduqas F3.1d, F3.1e, SP16, SP17)

#### What you need to know

Identify the main groups from their hard parts (Eduqas lists these features in detail):

- Brachiopods (marine): shell shape and symmetry, pedicle and brachial valves, foramen, hinge line, muscle scars.
- Bivalves (marine and freshwater): shape and symmetry, number and size of muscle scars, hinge line, teeth and sockets, gape, pallial line and sinus, umbones.
- Cephalopods (marine): coiled and chambered shell, suture lines.
- Corals (marine): colonial or solitary, septa.
- Trilobites (marine): cephalon, glabella, genal spines, eyes, thorax (number of segments), pygidium.
- Graptolites (marine): stipes, thecae.
- Plants (land): leaf, stem, root.
- Trace fossils: tracks and trails, burrows, coprolites.
- Eduqas: function and mode of life from morphology for bivalves (burrowing or not) and trilobites (benthonic or pelagic).

#### Explained

**Brachiopods vs bivalves** (both have two valves):

| Feature | Brachiopod | Bivalve |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Symmetry | Plane of symmetry cuts through each valve (each valve symmetrical); valves differ in size | Plane of symmetry between the valves (valves mirror each other); each valve usually asymmetrical |
| Valves | Pedicle valve (larger, with a hole, the foramen, for the fleshy stalk) and brachial valve | Left and right valves |
| Attachment | Pedicle through foramen | Byssus threads, cementing, or free |
| Hinge | Teeth and sockets on hinge line | Teeth and sockets; ligament |
| Muscle scars | Several small scars | One or two large adductor scars |
| Habitat | Marine only | Marine and freshwater |

Bivalve mode of life: deep burrowers have elongate, thin, smooth shells, a gape (gap where the siphons and foot protrude) and a pallial sinus (indent in the pallial line, where siphons were withdrawn), so a deep sinus means long siphons and deep burrowing. Shallow burrowers have thicker, rounded, ornamented shells and little or no sinus. Non-burrowers may be attached by byssus (mussels), cemented (oysters, irregular shape), or free-lying, sometimes swimming (scallops, with a flat shape).

**Cephalopods** (ammonoids, nautiloids, belemnites): chambered shell; septa divide chambers; where a septum meets the outer shell it forms a suture line. The animal lived in the last (body) chamber; the others held gas for buoyancy, linked by a tube (siphuncle). Free-swimming (nektonic).

**Corals**: solitary (one horn-shaped corallite) or colonial (many corallites). Septa are radial vertical plates. Groups: rugose (solitary or colonial, septa in multiples of four on the hand specimen, Palaeozoic), tabulate (always colonial, tiny septa, horizontal tabulae, Palaeozoic), scleractinian (modern and Mesozoic onward, six-fold septa).

**Trilobites**: three lobes along the body (a central axis and two side pleural lobes) and three parts head to tail: cephalon (head, with the raised glabella in the centre, eyes and often genal spines), thorax (segments, allowing enrolment), pygidium (tail shield). Benthonic (bottom-dwelling) forms: flattened, often with large or no eyes, wide genal spines spreading weight on soft mud; burrowers may be blind. Pelagic (swimming) forms: streamlined, large wrap-around eyes, light bodies. Detail in [23](23-evolution-and-applied-palaeontology.md).

**Graptolites**: colonial, planktonic; each stipe (branch) carries cups (thecae) each housing an individual. Preserved as carbon films in black shales. Number of stipes and shape of thecae change rapidly through time (see [06](06-geological-time-and-dating.md)).

**Plants**: leaves, stems and roots (for example roots in seat-earths below coal seams, *Stigmaria*).

**Trace fossils**: tracks (footprints) and trails (continuous grooves), burrows (vertical dwelling burrows in high-energy sands; horizontal feeding burrows in quiet muds), coprolites (fossil dung).

### Fossils as palaeoenvironmental indicators (OCR 2.2.1(c); Eduqas F3.1d)

#### What you need to know

- Trace fossils show behaviour (dwelling, protection, feeding, qualitative locomotion) and environment.
- Body fossils: skeleton thickness, robustness, ornament, sensory organs, and geopetal structures (way-up indicators inside fossils).

#### Explained

- Vertical dwelling burrows (for example U-shaped burrows) protect animals in shifting, high-energy sands of shorelines; horizontal grazing traces and spiral feeding patterns occur in quiet, deep, food-poor muds where animals search systematically. Tracks show whether an animal walked, crawled or swam.
- Thick, robust shells with strong ribs indicate high-energy water; thin, delicate shells indicate quiet water. Spines may stop sinking into soft mud or deter predators.
- Large eyes suggest active swimmers or dim light; no eyes suggest burrowing or deep, dark water.
- Corals need warm, shallow, clear, well-lit marine water.
- Geopetal structures: sediment partly fills a shell cavity, the rest filled later by calcite. The flat top of the sediment was horizontal when deposited, so it shows the way-up and original horizontal.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG5 / Eduqas SP16, SP17**: classify unknown fossils with a key using distinguishing features; produce scaled, annotated drawings using a hand lens or microscope, including a scale bar and labels of diagnostic features. Calculate magnification as image size ÷ real size.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying brachiopods and bivalves are closely related because both have two shells. They are different phyla.
- Saying all fossils are found where the organism lived.
- Drawing fossils with shading and no scale.

### Check questions

1. Explain how a fossil can be preserved by pyritisation. (2)
2. Distinguish between a mould and a cast. (2)
3. Give three reasons why the fossil record is biased. (3)
4. Describe how to tell a brachiopod from a bivalve using symmetry. (2)
5. A bivalve shell is elongate, thin and has a deep pallial sinus. Deduce its mode of life and explain. (3)
6. Describe evidence that a fossil assemblage is a death assemblage. (3)
7. Explain how a geopetal structure shows the way-up of a bed. (2)
8. Explain why trace fossils are useful palaeoenvironmental indicators. (2)

### Mark scheme

1. Burial in oxygen-poor mud with sulfate-reducing bacteria (1); original material replaced / infilled by pyrite (1).
2. Mould: impression left after the shell dissolves (1); cast: mould later filled with sediment or mineral giving a replica (1).
3. Any three: hard parts favoured; marine favoured; low-energy / depositional environments favoured; rapid burial needed; soft-bodied organisms rarely preserved; later destruction by erosion / metamorphism.
4. Brachiopod: plane of symmetry passes through each valve, valves unequal (1); bivalve: plane of symmetry between valves, valves mirror images (1).
5. Deep burrower (1); deep sinus means long siphons to reach the surface (1); elongate thin shell for moving through sediment (1).
6. Any three: broken / abraded fragments; separated valves; sorted by size; mixed species from different habitats; aligned by currents.
7. Sediment settled into the bottom of the cavity with a horizontal top (1); remaining space filled later with cement so flat surface points to original top / horizontal (1).
8. Formed in place / cannot be transported (1); record behaviour reflecting conditions such as energy, oxygen, food supply (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/06-geological-time-and-dating.md -->

## Geological time and dating

Geologists place events in order (relative dating) and give some of them ages in years (absolute or numerical dating). Combined, these build the geological timescale. Radioactive decay physics is also in [A-level Physics 18](../../physics/18-radioactivity-decay-and-nuclear-radius.md).

### Relative dating principles (OCR 4.2.1(a), (c); Eduqas F3.2a, F2.3f)

#### What you need to know

Superposition, original horizontality, way-up criteria, cross-cutting relationships, included fragments, unconformities, and evolutionary change in fossils. Apply them to outcrops, photographs, maps and cross-sections to work out geological histories. Know their limitations.

#### Explained

| Principle | Statement |
| --- | --- |
| Superposition | In an undisturbed sequence, younger beds lie on top of older beds |
| Original horizontality | Sediments are deposited in near-horizontal layers; tilted or folded beds were deformed later |
| Way-up criteria | Structures that show which way was up when deposited: graded bedding (coarse at base), cross-bedding (truncated tops, curved bases), ripple marks, desiccation cracks (V-shapes point down), load and flame structures, geopetals, pillow lavas, burrows. Needed when beds may be overturned |
| Cross-cutting relationships | A feature (fault, dyke, intrusion, unconformity) is younger than the rocks it cuts |
| Included fragments | Fragments (xenoliths, pebbles) are older than the rock that contains them |
| Unconformities | A surface of erosion or non-deposition representing a time gap; rocks below are older and have been uplifted and eroded |
| Faunal succession | Fossil species appear and disappear in a definite order, so the same fossils indicate the same age |

**Baked margins and chilled margins**: a sill is intruded if the beds above and below are both baked; a buried lava flow bakes only the bed below it, and the bed above may contain eroded fragments of the lava. This tells you the order of events.

**Unconformities**:

- Angular unconformity: older tilted or folded beds are overlain by younger beds at a different angle. History: deposition, deformation and uplift, erosion, subsidence, renewed deposition.
- Disconformity: parallel beds above and below, but an erosion surface between.
- Nonconformity: sediments on eroded igneous or metamorphic rock.

**Limitations of relative dating**: gives order not ages; unconformities hide unknown amounts of time; overturned or faulted sequences are hard to read; correlation between separated areas is uncertain.

#### Worked example

A cross-section shows folded sandstones and shales cut by a granite, both truncated by an erosion surface, overlain by horizontal limestones, all cut by a fault that displaces the limestones and is cut by a dyke. Order: (1) sandstones and shales deposited; (2) folding; (3) granite intrusion (cuts folds); (4) uplift and erosion (unconformity); (5) limestone deposition; (6) faulting; (7) dyke intrusion.

### Correlation (OCR 4.2.1(b), (d); Eduqas F3.2c, F3.2d)

#### What you need to know

- Lithostratigraphic correlation: matching rock units by sequence of beds, thickness and composition; lateral variation; diachronous beds.
- Biostratigraphic correlation: first appearance, stratigraphic range, extinction and fossil assemblages; zone fossils.
- Features of good zone fossils: wide geographic distribution, abundant, easily preserved, rapid evolution (short range), independent of facies, easily identified.
- Eduqas: graptolites (number and position of stipes, theca shape, early Palaeozoic) and cephalopod sutures (goniatite, ceratite, ammonite: late Palaeozoic and Mesozoic) in relative dating; assessing them as zone fossils.

#### Explained

**Lithostratigraphic correlation** matches beds between boreholes or outcrops by rock type and sequence, using distinctive marker beds (a volcanic ash, a coal seam). Problem: a rock unit may be **diachronous**, meaning it is a different age in different places. For example, a beach sand deposited as the sea advances (transgression) is older at the start and younger further inland. So matching rock type does not always match time.

**Biostratigraphy**: a zone is a body of rock defined by its fossil content, usually named after a zone fossil. Range zones use the first and last appearance of a species; assemblage zones use a group of fossils occurring together. The overlap of ranges of two species gives a narrow time band.

**Zone fossils**:

| Requirement | Why |
| --- | --- |
| Rapid evolution, short time range | Gives fine time resolution |
| Wide geographic distribution | Correlation over long distances |
| Abundant and readily preserved | Likely to be found |
| Facies independent (found in many rock types) | Correlates different environments |
| Easily identified | Reliable use |

**Graptolites** (Ordovician and Silurian): planktonic so widespread and facies-independent (but best preserved in black shales); evolved quickly. Trends: early forms had many stipes, hanging down (pendent); later forms had fewer stipes, becoming two-stiped and then single-stiped (monograptids, Silurian), and stipes changed from hanging down to growing upward (scandent). Thecal shapes became more complex (hooked, isolated). Weakness: fragile, mainly preserved in quiet anoxic muds.

**Cephalopod sutures**: sutures became more complex through time:

- Goniatite (Devonian to Permian): simple zigzag, with smooth saddles (pointing to the aperture) and lobes (pointing back).
- Ceratite (Triassic): saddles smooth, lobes frilled.
- Ammonite (Jurassic and Cretaceous): both saddles and lobes highly frilled.

Ammonites are excellent zone fossils for the Jurassic: free-swimming, widespread, rapidly evolving, many zones of under a million years each. Weakness: some lived in particular water depths, and shells can drift after death.

### Radiometric dating (OCR 2.2.2(a); Eduqas F3.2b, G1.1a)

#### What you need to know

- Radioactive decay of parent isotopes to daughter isotopes at a constant rate; half-life.
- Use decay to give the numerical age of minerals and rocks; plot and interpret half-life curves.
- Eduqas: potassium-argon (⁴⁰K → ⁴⁰Ar) and samarium-neodymium (¹⁴⁷Sm → ¹⁴³Nd); calculate ages; assumptions, accuracy and limitations. A level: N = N₀e^(−λt), logarithms, percentage error.
- OCR: qualitative history of other ways to estimate the Earth's age; suitable minerals; dating sedimentary and metamorphic rocks.

#### Explained

Radioactive (parent) isotopes decay to stable daughter isotopes at a rate unaffected by temperature, pressure or chemistry. **Half-life** (t½) is the time for half the parent atoms to decay.

| Half-lives passed | Parent left | Daughter : parent ratio |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 1/2 | 1 : 1 |
| 2 | 1/4 | 3 : 1 |
| 3 | 1/8 | 7 : 1 |
| 4 | 1/16 | 15 : 1 |

Equation: N = N₀e^(−λt), where λ = ln 2 ÷ t½. Rearranged: t = (1/λ) ln(N₀/N). If D is daughter produced: N₀ = N + D, so t = (1/λ) ln(1 + D/N).

**Methods**:

- K-Ar: half-life about 1.25 billion years. Used on K-bearing minerals (micas, feldspars) in igneous rocks, especially volcanic rocks. Argon is a gas, so the clock starts when the rock cools and traps argon. Weakness: argon can leak out if the rock is reheated (age too young) or excess argon can be trapped (too old).
- Sm-Nd: half-life about 106 billion years, so used for very old rocks (Precambrian), meteorites and mafic rocks. Both elements are rare-earth elements that behave similarly, so the system is hard to disturb.

**Which rocks can be dated**: the clock records when a mineral crystallised or last cooled below its "closure temperature". Igneous rocks give crystallisation ages. Metamorphic rocks give the age of metamorphism (minerals reset by heating). Sedimentary rocks usually cannot be dated directly, because their grains are older than the deposit; they are bracketed by dating igneous rocks above and below (lava flows, ash beds, cross-cutting dykes), or by dating minerals formed at deposition (glauconite).

**Assumptions**: closed system (no parent or daughter gained or lost); no daughter present at the start (or it can be corrected for); decay constant known accurately; the mineral formed at the event being dated.

**Earlier estimates of the Earth's age** (OCR, qualitative): counting generations in written records (a few thousand years); rates of sediment accumulation; salt accumulation in the oceans; Kelvin's cooling of the Earth (tens of millions of years, too short because he did not know about radioactive heating). Radiometric dating of meteorites gives about 4.6 billion years.

#### Worked example

A mica has ⁴⁰Ar : ⁴⁰K = 3 : 1 (ignoring the branch of K decay to Ca for simplicity). That is two half-lives: 2 × 1.25 = 2.5 billion years.

With the equation: a mineral has 60% of its original parent left; t½ = 700 million years. λ = 0.693 ÷ 700 = 9.9 × 10⁻⁴ per Ma. t = ln(1 ÷ 0.60) ÷ λ = 0.511 ÷ 9.9 × 10⁻⁴ ≈ 516 Ma.

Percentage error: an age of 516 ± 8 Ma has a percentage error of 8 ÷ 516 × 100 = 1.6%.

### The geological column (OCR 2.2.2(b); Eduqas F3.2e)

#### What you need to know

- The Phanerozoic is divided into eras and periods using a biostratigraphic relative sequence; numerical ages come from radiometric dating. Dates need not be memorised.
- Outline of Palaeozoic, Mesozoic and Cenozoic faunas; basic identification of trilobites, corals, brachiopods, bivalves and cephalopods (see [05](05-fossils-and-the-fossil-record.md)).
- Eduqas: use the column to place events in order and define the absolute age of some events.

#### Explained

| Era | Periods (oldest at bottom) | Typical fauna |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cenozoic | Quaternary, Neogene, Palaeogene | Mammals, birds, bivalves, gastropods, modern corals |
| Mesozoic | Cretaceous, Jurassic, Triassic | Ammonites, belemnites, dinosaurs, marine reptiles, scleractinian corals, bivalves |
| Palaeozoic | Permian, Carboniferous, Devonian, Silurian, Ordovician, Cambrian | Trilobites, graptolites, brachiopods, rugose and tabulate corals, goniatites; fish then amphibians and reptiles |

Boundaries between eras are mass extinctions (end-Permian, end-Cretaceous), so the faunas change sharply ([24](24-mass-extinctions.md)). The column was built from relative order using fossils (William Smith's faunal succession) and later calibrated with radiometric dates from interbedded volcanic rocks.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG5 and PAG6**: identify fossil groups and use them to assign relative ages; apply relative dating principles to field exposures, photographs and maps to construct a geological history.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying radiometric dating gives the age of a sandstone. It dates the original source minerals unless minerals grew at deposition.
- Saying a fault is older than the rocks it cuts.
- Saying "half-life is half the time the element lasts".

### Check questions

1. State the principle of cross-cutting relationships and give an example. (2)
2. Explain how you could show that a sheet of igneous rock between two beds is a sill rather than a lava flow. (3)
3. Describe the sequence of events needed to form an angular unconformity. (4)
4. Explain why a beach sandstone may be diachronous. (2)
5. Assess graptolites as zone fossils. (4)
6. A biotite has a daughter : parent ratio of 7 : 1. The half-life is 1.25 billion years. Calculate its age. (2)
7. Using N = N₀e^(−λt), calculate the age of a mineral with 85% of its parent remaining, if the half-life is 1.25 × 10⁹ years. (3)
8. Explain why sedimentary rocks are hard to date radiometrically and how their ages are found. (3)
9. Explain why K-Ar ages may be too young if a rock has been reheated. (2)

### Mark scheme

1. A feature that cuts across rocks is younger than them (1); example: dyke / fault / unconformity cutting beds (1).
2. Sill bakes rocks above and below (1); lava only bakes the rock below (1); lava may have vesicular / weathered top and fragments in the bed above; sill may be transgressive (1).
3. Deposition of horizontal beds (1); folding / tilting (by compression) and uplift (1); erosion forming an irregular surface (1); subsidence and deposition of new horizontal beds on top (1).
4. Sea advances over land gradually (1); beach deposited at different times at different places, so one rock unit has different ages (1).
5. Up to 4: rapid evolution, short ranges (1); planktonic so widespread / facies independent (1); abundant and recognisable (1); fragile, mainly preserved in black shales, limited to Ordovician to early Devonian (1).
6. 7 : 1 means 3 half-lives (1); 3 × 1.25 = 3.75 billion years (1).
7. λ = 0.693 ÷ 1.25 × 10⁹ = 5.54 × 10⁻¹⁰ y⁻¹ (1); t = ln(1/0.85) ÷ λ (1); = 0.1625 ÷ 5.54 × 10⁻¹⁰ = 2.9 × 10⁸ years (1).
8. Grains formed earlier in source rocks, so give source age not deposition age (1); date interbedded lavas / ashes or cross-cutting intrusions (1); bracket the age between them / date minerals formed at deposition (1).
9. Argon is a gas (1); escapes when heated so less daughter measured, giving a younger age (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/07-physical-structure-of-the-earth.md -->

## The physical structure of the Earth

We cannot sample deeper than about 12 km, so most knowledge of the Earth's interior comes from indirect evidence: seismic waves, gravity, magnetism, electrical conductivity and density. Two separate ways of dividing the Earth must be kept apart: by **composition** (crust, mantle, core; see [08](08-origin-and-geochemistry-of-the-earth.md)) and by **mechanical behaviour** (lithosphere, asthenosphere, mesosphere, outer and inner core). The crust is not the lithosphere, and the Moho is not the base of the lithosphere.

### Layers by rheology (OCR 3.1.1(a), (c), (f); Eduqas F4.1a)

#### What you need to know

- Rheology is how a material deforms (flows or breaks) under stress.
- Layers: lithosphere (rigid, brittle; crust plus uppermost mantle, divided into plates); asthenosphere (weak, rheid, plastic, with 1 to 5% partial melt); lower mantle (mesosphere, stronger); liquid outer core; solid inner core.

#### Explained

| Layer | Depth (approx.) | Behaviour |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Lithosphere | 0 to about 100 km (thin under ridges, thick under old continents) | Cold, rigid; breaks (brittle) near the surface, moves as plates |
| Asthenosphere | about 100 to 250 km (up to 700 km in some definitions) | Hot, weak, flows slowly (a rheid: a solid that flows over long times); small partial melt |
| Mesosphere (lower mantle) | to 2900 km | Solid, stronger owing to pressure, but convects slowly |
| Outer core | 2900 to 5150 km | Liquid; flows |
| Inner core | 5150 to 6370 km | Solid, owing to pressure |

The asthenosphere's weakness allows the lithosphere plates to move over it.

### Seismic evidence (OCR 3.1.1(b); Eduqas F4.1a, F4.1b)

#### What you need to know

- P (primary, compressional) and S (secondary, shear) body waves, and surface waves; time-distance curves.
- Velocity changes show layers; refraction and the P and S shadow zones; how the state and depth of the outer and inner core are found.
- Velocity-depth models; density distribution with depth.
- Continental and oceanic crust: thickness, composition, structure; the Moho; ocean drilling (Eduqas: for example JOIDES Resolution expedition 360 attempting to drill to the Moho).

#### Explained

**Waves**:

- P waves: compressional; travel through solids, liquids and gases; fastest.
- S waves: shear; travel only through solids (liquids have no shear strength); about 0.6 times P speed.
- Surface waves: slowest, largest amplitude, cause most damage.

Velocity increases with rigidity and decreases with density in the wave equations; in practice velocities mostly increase with depth because rigidity rises faster than density. Waves refract (bend) at boundaries where velocity changes, and also curve gradually as velocity increases with depth.

**Key boundaries**:

- Mohorovičić discontinuity (Moho): base of the crust; P velocity jumps from about 6 to 7 to about 8 km s⁻¹. About 7 km under oceans, 30 to 70 km under continents.
- Low velocity zone (about 100 to 250 km): P and S slow down slightly; evidence for partial melt in the asthenosphere.
- Gutenberg discontinuity (2900 km): core-mantle boundary. P velocity drops sharply (from about 13.6 to 8 km s⁻¹) and S waves stop, so the outer core is liquid.
- Lehmann discontinuity (about 5150 km): P velocity increases; inner core solid (weak S waves converted from P can travel through it).

**Shadow zones**:

- S-wave shadow zone: beyond about 103° from the epicentre no direct S waves arrive, because they cannot pass through the liquid outer core. The size of the shadow tells us the radius of the core.
- P-wave shadow zone: between about 103° and 142°, P waves are refracted strongly at the core boundary (velocity drops), bending them away and leaving a zone with no direct P arrivals. Faint P waves found in the shadow zone revealed the solid inner core (Lehmann, 1936).

**Crust types**:

| | Oceanic crust | Continental crust |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Thickness | 6 to 10 km | 30 to 70 km |
| Composition | Basalt and gabbro (mafic) | Granitic upper crust, more mafic lower crust (average intermediate) |
| Density | about 3.0 g cm⁻³ | about 2.7 g cm⁻³ |
| Age | under 200 million years | up to 4 billion years |
| Structure | Layered (sediment, pillow lavas, sheeted dykes, gabbro; see [16](16-mid-ocean-ridges.md)) | Complex, deformed |

Ocean drilling recovers real samples: deep holes in oceanic crust have reached gabbro but not yet the Moho; results suggest the Moho in slow-spreading crust may be a boundary where altered mantle (serpentinite) meets fresh mantle, not always a clean crust-mantle boundary, so its interpretation is being revised.

### Gravity and isostasy (OCR 3.1.1(d))

#### What you need to know

- Gravity anomalies: free-air and Bouguer.
- Isostatic equilibrium and isostatic rebound as evidence of the behaviour of lithosphere and asthenosphere.

#### Explained

A gravity anomaly is the difference between measured gravity and the value expected for that place.

- **Free-air correction** removes the effect of height above sea level (gravity weakens with distance from the centre). The free-air anomaly is large over mountains if they are not compensated.
- **Bouguer correction** also removes the attraction of the rock between the station and sea level. The Bouguer anomaly reveals density differences below. Over large mountain ranges, Bouguer anomalies are strongly negative, showing low-density roots beneath them.

**Isostasy**: the lithosphere "floats" on the weaker asthenosphere, like blocks of wood in water. A thick, high-standing mountain range has a deep root of low-density crust. Where a region is in isostatic equilibrium, the free-air anomaly is close to zero.

**Isostatic rebound**: when a load is removed (ice sheets melting, erosion), the lithosphere slowly rises as asthenosphere flows back beneath. Scandinavia and Scotland are still rising after the last ice age; raised beaches show this. The rate of rebound gives the viscosity of the mantle. Adding a load (ice, sediment, volcanoes) makes the lithosphere sink.

#### Worked example

Ice 2000 m thick (density 0.9 g cm⁻³) sits on mantle (3.3 g cm⁻³). At equilibrium, depression = 2000 × 0.9 ÷ 3.3 ≈ 545 m. When the ice melts, the land can rise by up to about 545 m.

### EM surveys at ridges (OCR 3.1.1(e))

#### What you need to know

Electromagnetic surveys detect electrical conductivity; partially molten rock conducts much better than solid rock, so EM surveys can map the asthenosphere and melt beneath mid-ocean ridges.

#### Explained

Molten rock and saline fluids conduct electricity well. Magnetotelluric and controlled-source EM surveys measure how natural or induced electromagnetic fields vary with depth. Beneath mid-ocean ridges, a zone of high conductivity shows where partial melt is present, outlining the asthenosphere and the melt supply to the ridge (see [16](16-mid-ocean-ridges.md)).

### Density of the Earth (OCR 3.1.1(g); Eduqas F4.1a, SP18)

#### What you need to know

The whole-Earth mean density (about 5.5 g cm⁻³) is much greater than surface rocks (about 2.7 to 3.0 g cm⁻³), so the interior must be much denser: the core must be metallic.

#### Explained

Mean density = mass ÷ volume. Mass comes from gravity measurements; volume from the radius. 5.5 g cm⁻³ against crustal rocks of 2.7 to 3.0 means deeper material must average well above 5.5. Mantle rocks (peridotite) are about 3.3 at the surface and denser at depth; the core must be about 10 to 13 g cm⁻³, matching iron-nickel under pressure (and iron meteorites).

#### Worked example

Earth's radius 6.37 × 10⁶ m, mass 5.97 × 10²⁴ kg. Volume = (4/3)πr³ = 1.08 × 10²¹ m³. Density = 5.97 × 10²⁴ ÷ 1.08 × 10²¹ = 5510 kg m⁻³ = 5.5 g cm⁻³.

#### Practical

**Eduqas SP18**: measure the densities of representative rocks (granite, basalt, peridotite) by mass and displacement, and compare with the mean density of the Earth to argue for a dense core.

### The geodynamo (OCR 3.1.1(h); Eduqas F4.1a)

#### What you need to know

The Earth's magnetic field probably comes from a geodynamo: convection in a rotating, electrically conducting fluid (the liquid iron outer core). This is indirect evidence for a liquid metallic outer core.

#### Explained

Heat from the inner core and the latent heat of crystallisation drive convection in the liquid iron-nickel outer core. The Earth's rotation organises the flow into columns. Moving conducting fluid in a magnetic field induces electric currents, which sustain the field (a self-sustaining dynamo). The field reverses irregularly, recorded in rocks as palaeomagnetism ([09](09-plate-tectonics.md)). A permanent magnet is impossible because the interior is above the Curie temperature.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the asthenosphere is liquid or is the magma source for all volcanoes.
- Saying S waves are absorbed by the mantle. They pass through the solid mantle but not the liquid outer core.
- Saying lithosphere and crust are the same.

### Check questions

1. Explain why S waves do not pass through the outer core and what this shows. (2)
2. Explain how the P-wave shadow zone forms. (3)
3. Describe two differences between oceanic and continental crust. (2)
4. Distinguish between the free-air and Bouguer gravity anomalies. (2)
5. Explain why the Bouguer anomaly over the Himalayas is strongly negative. (3)
6. Explain why parts of Scotland are still rising today. (3)
7. The mean density of the Earth is 5.5 g cm⁻³. Explain what this suggests about the core. (3)
8. Explain how electromagnetic surveys provide evidence for partial melting beneath mid-ocean ridges. (2)
9. Outline the geodynamo model for the Earth's magnetic field. (3)

### Mark scheme

1. S waves are shear waves and liquids have no shear strength (1); so the outer core is liquid (1).
2. P waves slow down sharply at the core-mantle boundary (1); refracted / bent towards the normal (1); no direct P waves reach about 103° to 142° from the epicentre (1).
3. Any two: oceanic thinner; denser / basaltic vs granitic; younger.
4. Free-air corrects only for height (1); Bouguer also corrects for the mass of rock between station and sea level (1).
5. Mountains have deep roots of low-density crust (1); replacing denser mantle (1); after removing the topography's attraction, a mass deficit remains (1).
6. Ice sheets loaded and depressed the lithosphere (1); ice melted, load removed (1); lithosphere rebounds slowly as asthenosphere flows back (1).
7. Surface rocks only about 2.7 to 3.3 (1); so the interior must be much denser (1); core made of dense metal / iron-nickel, consistent with iron meteorites (1).
8. Molten rock conducts electricity better than solid rock (1); high conductivity zone found beneath ridges shows partial melt (1).
9. Liquid iron outer core convects (1); conducting fluid moving in a magnetic field induces currents (1); currents sustain the field; rotation organises flow (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/08-origin-and-geochemistry-of-the-earth.md -->

## The origin and geochemistry of the Earth

This file covers what the Earth is made of overall, how it formed, where its internal heat comes from, how elements behave chemically (the Goldschmidt classification) and how the Earth separated into compositional layers. Planet formation is also in the GCSE [Astronomy](../../../gcse/smaller-stem/astronomy-edexcel/12-formation-of-planetary-systems.md) notes.

### Bulk composition (OCR 3.1.2(a); Eduqas F1.1b)

#### What you need to know

- The bulk composition of the Earth is inferred from chondrite meteorites and the Sun's composition.
- Use normalised diagrams (element concentrations divided by chondrite values), qualitatively.

#### Explained

Chondrites are stony meteorites that were never melted. Apart from volatile elements (H, He, C, N, noble gases), their composition matches the Sun's (measured by spectroscopy of sunlight). Because the Sun holds over 99% of the solar system's mass, chondrites are taken as the original solid building material of the planets. The bulk Earth is therefore assumed to be broadly chondritic: mostly Fe, O, Si and Mg, with Ni, S, Ca and Al.

Normalised diagrams: dividing each element's concentration in a rock by its concentration in chondrites gives a ratio. A value of 1 means "same as the primitive Earth"; values above 1 mean the element is concentrated in that rock (crustal rocks are enriched in large ions such as K, U, Th, rare earths); below 1 means depleted (crust is depleted in Ni, Cr and Pt-group elements, which went to the core or stayed in the mantle).

### The nebular hypothesis (OCR 3.1.2(b))

#### What you need to know

A qualitative account of how the solar system and Earth formed, with evidence from protoplanetary discs and impact craters.

#### Explained

1. About 4.6 billion years ago, a cloud of gas and dust (a nebula) collapsed under gravity, perhaps triggered by a nearby supernova.
2. It spun faster and flattened into a disc; most mass gathered at the centre to form the Sun.
3. In the disc, dust grains stuck together into larger bodies (accretion), then planetesimals (kilometres across), then protoplanets by collisions.
4. Near the Sun it was too hot for ices to condense, so the inner planets are rocky and metallic; further out, ices condensed and giant planets formed.
5. Late, large impacts continued (the Moon probably formed from debris when a Mars-sized body hit the early Earth).

**Evidence**: telescopes image protoplanetary discs around young stars (with gaps cleared by forming planets); impact craters on the Moon, Mercury and Mars record intense early bombardment; meteorites date to about 4.57 billion years; planets orbit in the same direction in nearly the same plane.

### Internal heat (OCR 3.1.2(c); Eduqas F2.2a)

#### What you need to know

Geothermal energy comes from:

- heat of formation: gravitational potential energy released by differentiation (dense iron sinking to form the core); latent heat released as the inner core solidifies; kinetic energy of early bombardment
- radioactive decay of K, U and Th.

#### Explained

- Accretion: colliding bodies converted kinetic energy to heat, probably melting the outer Earth (a magma ocean).
- Differentiation: as molten iron sank to the centre, gravitational potential energy was converted to heat.
- Inner core growth: as the outer core slowly freezes onto the inner core, latent heat is released, helping drive outer-core convection.
- Radioactive decay: long-lived isotopes ⁴⁰K, ²³²Th, ²³⁵U and ²³⁸U release heat; they are concentrated in the continental crust and mantle. About half of the Earth's surface heat flow today comes from radioactive decay; the rest is primordial heat still escaping.

### Goldschmidt classification (OCR 3.1.2(d); Eduqas F1.1a)

#### What you need to know

- Four groups: lithophile, siderophile, chalcophile, atmophile.
- Qualitative understanding of preferred compounds (oxides and silicates, sulfides). Enthalpy and free energy are not needed.

#### Explained

| Group | Meaning | Examples | Where concentrated |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Lithophile ("rock-loving") | Bond with oxygen, forming silicates and oxides | Si, Al, Na, K, Ca, Mg, U, Th, rare earths | Crust and mantle |
| Siderophile ("iron-loving") | Dissolve in metallic iron | Fe, Ni, Co, Au, Pt, Pd | Core |
| Chalcophile ("copper-loving") | Bond with sulfur, forming sulfides | Cu, Zn, Pb, S, Ag, As, Hg | Sulfide ores, some in core and mantle |
| Atmophile ("vapour-loving") | Gases | H, N, C (as CO₂), noble gases | Atmosphere and hydrosphere |

Some elements fall in more than one group (iron is siderophile, lithophile and chalcophile depending on conditions).

### Differentiation (OCR 3.1.2(e); Eduqas F1.1a, F1.1c)

#### What you need to know

- Differentiation into layers of distinct composition and density by partitioning of each Goldschmidt group between core, mantle, crust, atmosphere and hydrosphere.
- Meteorites as a possible source of rare siderophiles in the crust; crustal abundance and concentration factor.
- Eduqas: the crust is mostly eight elements: O, Si, Al, Fe, Ca, Na, K, Mg; silicates are the main rock-forming minerals.

#### Explained

When the early Earth was largely molten, dense metallic iron (carrying siderophiles) sank to form the core. The remaining silicate mantle kept lithophiles. Partial melting of the mantle over time extracted the most incompatible lithophiles (K, U, Th, Si, Al, Na) into the crust, which is less dense. Gases (atmophiles) were released by volcanic outgassing to form the atmosphere and, as water condensed, the oceans.

Siderophile elements such as gold and platinum should have gone almost entirely into the core, yet the mantle and crust contain more than expected. One explanation is a "late veneer": meteorites added after core formation brought these elements to the surface.

**Crustal abundance** (approximate, by mass): O 46%, Si 28%, Al 8%, Fe 5%, Ca 4%, Na 3%, K 3%, Mg 2%. Together these make about 98%, so most minerals are silicates of these elements.

**Concentration factor** = concentration of an element in an ore ÷ its average crustal abundance. This links to economic geology ([18](18-mining-geology.md)).

#### Worked example

Average crustal abundance of copper is 0.006%. An ore has 0.9% copper. Concentration factor = 0.9 ÷ 0.006 = 150.

### Geochemical layers from direct evidence (OCR 3.1.2(f); Eduqas F4.1a)

#### What you need to know

The composition of the layers is inferred from direct evidence: deep mines and boreholes, ophiolites, kimberlite pipes, mantle xenoliths, and seismology (Lehmann, Gutenberg and Moho discontinuities).

#### Explained

- Deep mines and boreholes (the deepest reaches about 12 km) sample the upper crust only.
- Ophiolites: slices of oceanic crust and upper mantle thrust onto continents at collision zones (for example Troodos in Cyprus, Oman, the Lizard in Cornwall). They show the full sequence including mantle peridotite.
- Kimberlite pipes: volcanic pipes from very deep (over 150 km) that carry diamonds and mantle fragments to the surface.
- Mantle xenoliths: fragments of peridotite (olivine, pyroxene) carried up in basalt lavas and kimberlites show that the upper mantle is peridotite.
- Meteorites: stony meteorites resemble the mantle; iron meteorites resemble the core.
- Seismic discontinuities mark composition changes: Moho (crust to peridotite mantle), Gutenberg (silicate mantle to iron core), Lehmann (liquid to solid iron).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the core is made of siderophiles "because they are heavy". Siderophiles dissolve in iron metal; that is why they followed it.
- Treating kimberlites as samples of the core. They come from the upper mantle.

### Check questions

1. Explain why chondrite meteorites are used to estimate the bulk composition of the Earth. (3)
2. Describe two pieces of evidence for the nebular hypothesis. (2)
3. State four sources of the Earth's internal heat. (4)
4. Classify the following as lithophile, siderophile, chalcophile or atmophile: nickel, potassium, zinc, argon. (2)
5. Explain why the continental crust is enriched in potassium and uranium. (3)
6. Suggest why platinum is more abundant in the crust than expected from core formation. (2)
7. Gold has an average crustal abundance of 0.004 ppm. Calculate the concentration factor for an ore with 6 g of gold per tonne. (2)
8. Describe how mantle xenoliths provide direct evidence for the composition of the upper mantle. (2)

### Mark scheme

1. Chondrites never melted / undifferentiated (1); composition matches the Sun except volatiles (1); represent original material from which planets formed (1).
2. Any two: protoplanetary discs seen around young stars; craters showing early bombardment; planets orbit in one plane and direction; meteorites all about 4.57 billion years old.
3. Accretion / impacts (1); differentiation / core formation (1); latent heat from inner core crystallisation (1); radioactive decay of K, U, Th (1).
4. Siderophile, lithophile, chalcophile, atmophile (all four 2; two or three 1).
5. K and U are lithophile / form large ions that do not fit mantle minerals (1); concentrated in melts during partial melting (1); melts rise and form crust (1).
6. Added later by meteorite impacts after core formation (late veneer) (1); so not removed into the core (1).
7. 6 g t⁻¹ = 6 ppm (1); 6 ÷ 0.004 = 1500 (1).
8. Fragments of mantle carried rapidly to the surface in basalt or kimberlite (1); made of peridotite (olivine, pyroxene) showing upper mantle composition (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/09-plate-tectonics.md -->

## Plate tectonics

Plate tectonics is how the Earth loses its internal heat. Rigid lithospheric plates move over the weaker asthenosphere, and their boundaries control where earthquakes, volcanoes and mountain belts occur. GCSE background: [GCSE Geology 07](../../../gcse/smaller-stem/geology-eduqas/07-plate-tectonics.md).

### Heat transfer and the geotherm (OCR 3.2.1(a); Eduqas F2.2a)

#### What you need to know

- Energy from inside the Earth drives internal processes; evidence for the geothermal gradient; convection, conduction and advection; estimates of geothermal flux.
- Rocks generally stay below their melting point except locally.

#### Explained

- **Geothermal gradient (geotherm)**: temperature increase with depth. In continental crust near the surface it is about 25 to 30 °C per km (measured in mines and boreholes), decreasing with depth.
- **Conduction**: heat passes through solid rock from hot to cold; dominant through the lithosphere.
- **Convection**: hot, less dense mantle rises slowly and cold, dense material sinks, carrying heat.
- **Advection**: heat carried by moving material such as magma or hot water, for example at ridges and hot springs.
- **Heat flow** (W m⁻²) = thermal conductivity × geothermal gradient. Average about 0.09 W m⁻²; highest at mid-ocean ridges, lowest in old continental shields and over trenches.

#### Worked example

A borehole shows 18 °C at 200 m and 63 °C at 1700 m. Gradient = (63 − 18) ÷ (1700 − 200) = 45 ÷ 1500 = 0.030 °C m⁻¹ = 30 °C km⁻¹. With conductivity 2.5 W m⁻¹ K⁻¹, heat flow = 2.5 × 0.030 = 0.075 W m⁻².

### Seismic evidence for plates (OCR 3.2.1(b), (c); Eduqas F4.2b, SP19)

#### What you need to know

- Earthquakes define plate boundaries; plate interiors are largely aseismic.
- Benioff zones: earthquake foci on an inclined plane beneath trenches.
- Seismic tomography shows mantle structure (subducted slabs, upwelling).
- Interpret seismograms: distance from the epicentre from the S-P interval using time-distance curves; locate the epicentre; use seismograms to show shadow zones.
- Eduqas SP19: investigate relationships between focal depth, magnitude and distance from plate boundaries using Google Earth data; scatter graphs and correlation.

#### Explained

Earthquakes mostly occur in narrow belts that outline plates. Shallow earthquakes occur at ridges and transform faults; at convergent margins, foci lie along a plane dipping beneath the overriding plate down to about 700 km (the **Benioff zone**), tracing the subducting slab.

**Seismic tomography**: many earthquake records at many stations are combined to map small differences in wave speed through the mantle. Faster (colder, denser) regions show sinking slabs, some reaching the core-mantle boundary; slower (hotter) regions show upwellings and plumes.

**Locating an epicentre**:

1. On a seismogram, read the time between the first P and first S arrivals (S-P interval).
2. Use a time-distance graph: the gap between the P and S curves increases with distance; find the distance where the gap equals the S-P interval.
3. Draw a circle of that radius around the station on a map.
4. Repeat for three stations; the circles intersect at the epicentre (triangulation).

#### Worked example

P waves travel at 8 km s⁻¹ and S waves at 4.5 km s⁻¹. The S-P interval is 70 s. Time for distance d: d/4.5 − d/8 = 70, so d(0.2222 − 0.125) = 70; d = 70 ÷ 0.0972 ≈ 720 km.

### Reconstructing past plate movements (OCR 3.2.1(d); Eduqas F4.2b, F4.2d, F4.2e)

#### What you need to know

- OCR: global distribution of features of the same age: orogenic belts (Caledonian orogeny and the Iapetus Ocean), palaeoenvironments, geomagnetics (apparent polar wandering curves), ocean-floor magnetic anomalies, volcanic zones, palaeoecology and glacial geology.
- Eduqas: remanent magnetism, ferromagnetism and Curie temperature; palaeomagnetism shows changes in latitude; ocean-floor magnetic anomalies show sea-floor spreading; ocean floor drilling; geothermal data; geomagnetic and geoelectrical properties.

#### Explained

**Palaeomagnetism**: iron-bearing minerals (magnetite) are ferromagnetic. Above the **Curie temperature** (about 580 °C for magnetite) they are not magnetised; as a lava cools below it, their magnetic domains align with the Earth's field, locking in its direction (remanent magnetism). Sediments can also record the field as magnetic grains settle.

- The inclination (dip) of the recorded field depends on latitude: vertical at the magnetic poles, horizontal at the equator. So the inclination gives the palaeolatitude where the rock formed. tan I = 2 tan λ (inclination I, latitude λ).
- **Apparent polar wander (APW) curves**: plotting the pole position calculated from rocks of different ages on one continent gives a path. Since the pole did not really wander far, the continent moved. Different continents give different paths that line up when the continents are put back together, which is strong evidence for drift.

**Magnetic stripes**: the field reverses irregularly every few hundred thousand years. New basalt at a ridge records the current polarity, so parallel stripes of normal and reversed magnetisation form, symmetrical about the ridge. Their widths, matched to the dated reversal timescale, give spreading rates ([16](16-mid-ocean-ridges.md)).

**Other evidence**:

- Matching orogenic belts across oceans: the Caledonian mountain belt of Scotland, Scandinavia, Greenland and eastern North America formed when the Iapetus Ocean closed; the belts line up when the Atlantic is closed.
- Palaeoenvironments and palaeoecology: matching fossils (Glossopteris, Mesosaurus) and climate indicators (coal in Antarctica, glacial deposits in tropical Africa and India) in the wrong latitudes today.
- Ocean drilling: sediment is thin and young at ridges, older and thicker away from them; no ocean floor older than about 180 to 200 million years.
- Heat flow high at ridges, low at trenches.
- Volcanic zones along boundaries; hotspot chains.

### Mantle plumes (OCR 3.2.1(e))

#### What you need to know

Evidence: relative plate motion, geological features, heat flow and seismic tomography.

#### Explained

A mantle plume is a narrow column of hot mantle rising from deep (possibly the core-mantle boundary). Evidence:

- Hotspot island chains (Hawaii-Emperor) that become older away from the active volcano, showing the plate moving over a fixed source; the bend in the chain records a change in plate direction.
- Large volumes of basalt (flood basalts, Iceland's thick crust).
- High heat flow and a broad topographic swell.
- Seismic tomography shows slow (hot) columns under some hotspots.

The deep origin of plumes is still debated.

### GPS and geodesy (OCR 3.2.1(f); Eduqas F4.2b)

#### What you need to know

Resolution and precision of GPS allow direct measurement of present relative plate movement; basic geodesy: the ellipsoid model of the Earth, the geoid, terrestrial reference frames. How GPS works is not required.

#### Explained

- The **ellipsoid** is a smooth mathematical shape approximating the Earth (slightly flattened at the poles).
- The **geoid** is the shape the sea surface would take under gravity alone, extended under the continents; it has bumps and hollows of up to about 100 m caused by uneven mass distribution.
- A **terrestrial reference frame** is a fixed system of coordinates (defined by a global network of stations) against which positions can be measured over years.

Permanent GPS stations record their position to millimetre precision. Repeated measurements give velocities of a few cm per year, matching rates from magnetic stripes averaged over millions of years. GPS also shows strain building up across faults and ground deformation at volcanoes.

### Convection and driving forces (OCR 3.2.1(g), (h), (i); Eduqas F4.2c)

#### What you need to know

- Subduction zones, plates (cold thermal boundary layer) and plumes act as the active limbs of convection.
- Ridge push: gravity and density differences; passive upwelling at divergent boundaries and sea-floor spreading.
- Slab pull at subduction zones versus ridge push; the forces are still debated.

#### Explained

Older ideas pictured mantle convection cells dragging passive plates along. The modern view sees the plates themselves as the top of the convection system: the cold, dense lithosphere sinks at subduction zones (the downgoing limb), and plumes rise from below.

- **Slab pull**: old oceanic lithosphere is cold and denser than the asthenosphere below, so it sinks at subduction zones and pulls the rest of the plate. Plates with long subducting edges (Pacific) move fastest, suggesting slab pull is the largest force.
- **Ridge push**: the ridge stands high because the lithosphere there is hot and thin. The lithosphere slides down the gentle slope away from the ridge under gravity. Mantle beneath the ridge rises passively to fill the gap (decompression melting).
- Other forces: drag at the base of plates, resistance at collision zones.

### How the theory developed (OCR 3.2.1(j); Eduqas F4.2a)

#### What you need to know

- The paradigm emerged from earlier, less complete models: contraction theory, geosynclines, continental drift, mantle convection carrying passive plates. OCR does not require names and dates; Eduqas asks for the development from continental drift through active mantle convection carrying passive plates to slab pull and ridge push.
- Interpret developing models; evidence from ocean basin research, continental blocks, isostasy and radioactivity.

#### Explained

1. **Contraction theory** (19th century): the Earth was cooling and shrinking, wrinkling the crust like a drying apple to form mountains. Against it: mountains are concentrated in belts, not evenly spread; radioactivity (discovered 1896) showed the Earth was not simply cooling; isostasy showed continents float on denser material.
2. **Geosynclines**: thick sedimentary basins that subsided and were later squeezed into mountains, with vertical movements. They explained thick sequences but not the cause of compression.
3. **Continental drift** (early 20th century): continents moved across the ocean floor. Evidence: jigsaw fit, matching fossils, rocks and mountain belts, past climates. Rejected mainly because no plausible mechanism.
4. **Mantle convection** was proposed as a mechanism, with plates carried passively.
5. **Ocean basin research** (1950s and 1960s): mapping of ridges and trenches, magnetic stripes, young ocean floor and sea-floor spreading. Earthquakes along Benioff zones showed subduction.
6. **Plate tectonics** (late 1960s): rigid plates with three boundary types; then slab pull and ridge push recognised as major driving forces.

### Plate boundaries and the rock cycle (Eduqas F4.2a, F4.2b, F4.2f)

| Boundary | Motion | Features | Rock cycle links |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Divergent (constructive) | Apart | Mid-ocean ridges, rift valleys; shallow earthquakes; basalt volcanism | Basaltic magmatism at spreading centres |
| Convergent (destructive) ocean-ocean or ocean-continent | Together, with subduction | Trench, Benioff zone, volcanic arc (andesite), fold mountains | Andesitic and basaltic magmatism; regional metamorphism; sediment from rapid erosion of rising mountains |
| Convergent continent-continent | Together, collision | Fold mountains (Himalaya), thrusts, thick crust | Granitic magma from crustal melting; regional metamorphism |
| Conservative (transform) | Sideways | Transform faults, shallow earthquakes, no volcanism | |

Tectonics also controls sedimentation: uplift and erosion supply sediment; subsiding basins (rifts, foreland basins) collect it.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying convection currents in the mantle are the only or main driving force.
- Confusing true polar wander with apparent polar wander.
- Saying magnetic stripes are caused by the Earth's poles moving.

### Check questions

1. Explain the difference between conduction and advection of heat in the Earth. (2)
2. A seismic station records an S-P interval of 45 s. P waves travel at 7.8 km s⁻¹ and S waves at 4.4 km s⁻¹. Calculate the distance to the epicentre. (3)
3. Explain how three seismic stations can be used to find an epicentre. (3)
4. Explain how a Benioff zone provides evidence for subduction. (2)
5. Explain how remanent magnetism records the latitude at which a lava cooled. (3)
6. Explain why apparent polar wandering curves are evidence for continental drift. (3)
7. Describe evidence for a mantle plume beneath Hawaii. (3)
8. Compare slab pull and ridge push. (4)
9. Explain why continental drift was rejected when first proposed, and what new evidence led to plate tectonics being accepted. (6)

### Mark scheme

1. Conduction: heat passed through solid rock without movement of material (1); advection: heat carried by moving material such as magma or water (1).
2. d/4.4 − d/7.8 = 45 (1); d × 0.0991 = 45 (1); d ≈ 454 km (1).
3. S-P interval gives distance from each station (1); circle drawn around each station with that radius (1); epicentre where three circles intersect (1).
4. Earthquake foci lie on an inclined plane beneath the trench (1); showing a slab of cold, brittle lithosphere descending into the mantle (1).
5. Iron minerals become magnetised as lava cools below the Curie temperature (1); align with the Earth's field at that time (1); inclination depends on latitude so measuring it gives the palaeolatitude (1).
6. Each continent gives a different path for the pole over time (1); there was only one pole, so the continents moved (1); paths match when continents are reassembled (1).
7. Chain of volcanoes ages increasing away from the active volcano (1); large volumes of basalt / topographic swell / high heat flow (1); low seismic velocity column in tomography (1).
8. Slab pull: dense cold slab sinks at subduction zone, pulling the plate (1); ridge push: elevated ridge, plate slides down under gravity (1); slab pull thought larger (1); plates with long subduction zones move faster (1).
9. Level-marked: no mechanism (1); objection that continents could not plough through ocean floor (1); new ocean floor data: ridges, trenches mapped (1); magnetic stripes symmetrical (1); young ocean floor / thin sediment at ridges (1); Benioff zones / heat flow / GPS (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/10-magma-intrusion-and-volcanism.md -->

## Magma, intrusion and volcanism

This file explains why rocks melt in particular settings, how magma rises and forms intrusions, what controls the style of eruption and the shape of volcanoes, and the hazards volcanoes cause. Rock names and textures are in [02](02-igneous-rocks.md); magma evolution in [15](15-igneous-petrology.md).

### Generating magma (OCR 3.2.2(a), (b); Eduqas F2.2c)

#### What you need to know

- Solidus (temperature at which a rock starts to melt), liquidus (temperature at which it is fully molten), geotherm, adiabatic conditions.
- Mafic (basaltic) magma by partial melting of mantle at divergent boundaries and hotspots.
- Intermediate (andesitic) and silicic magma at convergent margins where crustal material and water are carried down; magma mixing.
- Eduqas: four settings: divergent margins, convergent margins, mantle plumes, deeply buried lower continental crust in orogenic belts (granite).

#### Explained

Normally the geotherm lies below the mantle solidus, so the mantle is solid. Melting needs the rock to cross its solidus. Three ways:

1. **Decompression melting**: hot mantle rises quickly (at ridges and in plumes), so it stays almost as hot (adiabatic: no heat lost) while pressure falls. The solidus falls with lower pressure, so the rising mantle crosses it and partially melts (about 10 to 20%), producing basaltic magma. This happens beneath **divergent margins** and **mantle plumes** (hotspots).
2. **Flux melting (adding water)**: at **convergent margins**, the subducting slab releases water as it is heated. Water lowers the melting point of the overlying mantle wedge, which partially melts to give basaltic magma that rises and interacts with crust to give andesitic magma. Partial melting of the subducted oceanic crust itself can also contribute.
3. **Heating crust**: in **orogenic belts** where continental crust is thickened, the deep crust heats up (radioactive heat, plus heat from basaltic magma underplating it) and partially melts, giving granitic (silicic) magma. Assimilation of crust by mafic magma also makes magmas more silicic.

Partial melting makes a melt richer in silica than its source, because low-melting minerals melt first. So partial melting of peridotite gives basalt; partial melting of basalt or crust gives andesite or granite. **Magma mixing**: when two magmas meet (for example basalt intruding a granite chamber), they can mix to give intermediate compositions, sometimes leaving blobs (enclaves) of one in the other.

#### Worked example

A graph shows the continental geotherm and the wet lower-crust solidus crossing at 700 °C. If the geotherm is 25 °C km⁻¹ with 10 °C at the surface, the depth of melting = (700 − 10) ÷ 25 = 27.6 km. Thickening the crust in a collision raises temperatures at that depth, so melting begins.

### Intrusion (OCR 3.2.2(c), (d), (f); Eduqas F2.2b)

#### What you need to know

- How magma rises through the crust and affects country rock; diapirs and transgressive sills.
- Major intrusions (plutons, batholiths) and minor intrusions (dykes, sills); chilled and baked margins; metamorphic aureoles.
- Diagnostic characteristics of dykes, sills and lava flows.
- Detecting intrusion with geodetic and geophysical data: harmonic tremor, 3D seismic visualisation, tiltmeters, GPS.

#### Explained

Magma is less dense than surrounding rock, so it rises by buoyancy. It moves by:

- **Diapirism**: a large, hot, viscous body (granite) rises like a teardrop, softening and pushing aside the country rock.
- **Stoping**: blocks of roof rock break off and sink into the magma (may remain as xenoliths).
- **Fracture propagation**: fluid basaltic magma forces open cracks, forming dykes (vertical, cut across bedding) and sills (horizontal, along bedding). A **transgressive sill** steps up from one bedding plane to a higher one.

| | Dyke | Sill | Lava flow |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Shape | Sheet, steep, discordant | Sheet, parallel to bedding, concordant | Sheet at the surface |
| Margins | Chilled on both sides; baked rock on both sides | Chilled top and base; baked rocks above and below | Chilled base and top; baked only beneath |
| Top surface | | No weathering; may be transgressive | Often vesicular, weathered, reddened top; fragments of it in the bed above |
| Crystal size | Fine to medium, coarsest in the centre | Medium (dolerite) | Fine, vesicular |

**Plutons and batholiths**: large, coarse-grained bodies (granite) emplaced at depth, with wide metamorphic aureoles. **Chilled margins** are finer-grained edges where magma cooled quickly against cold country rock; **baked margins** are country rock hardened and recrystallised by heat.

**Detecting moving magma**: rising magma causes swarms of small earthquakes and **harmonic tremor** (continuous vibration from fluid movement); 3D visualisation of earthquake locations shows magma paths; **tiltmeters** and GPS detect ground swelling (inflation) as magma accumulates.

### Eruption (OCR 3.2.2(e), (g); Eduqas F2.2e)

#### What you need to know

- Changes in magma properties affect buoyancy so magma reaches the surface: exsolution of volatiles, crystal content, recharge and groundwater changes.
- Practical models of magma properties and buoyancy.
- Silica content and temperature control viscosity and ability to release volatiles; qualitative role of OH⁻ ions on silicate polymerisation.

#### Explained

**Viscosity** is resistance to flow. Silica tetrahedra link into chains and networks (polymerisation) in the melt; more silica means more linking and higher viscosity. Higher temperature breaks links, lowering viscosity. Dissolved water (as OH⁻) breaks Si–O–Si links, lowering viscosity, until it escapes as gas.

| Magma | SiO₂ | Temperature | Viscosity | Gas content |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Basaltic | about 50% | 1100 to 1200 °C | Low | Low |
| Andesitic | about 60% | 900 to 1000 °C | Medium | Medium to high |
| Rhyolitic | over 70% | 700 to 850 °C | High | High |

**Why eruptions happen**: as magma rises, pressure falls and dissolved gases (H₂O, CO₂, SO₂) come out of solution as bubbles (exsolution). Bubbles reduce bulk density, increasing buoyancy, so magma rises faster. Crystallisation concentrates water in the remaining melt (second boiling). A new batch of hot magma from below (recharge) can heat and stir a chamber and trigger eruption. Groundwater meeting magma flashes to steam (phreatic eruptions).

In low-viscosity basalt, gas escapes easily: gentle (effusive) eruptions. In viscous silicic magma, gas cannot escape, so pressure builds until the magma fragments into ash: explosive eruptions.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG3**: model magma viscosity with syrup or wax at different temperatures (time the flow down a slope or the fall of a ball bearing), or model gas exsolution with carbonated drinks or bicarbonate and vinegar in a viscous liquid. Control volume, slope angle and temperature measurement.

### Volcanic landforms (OCR 3.2.2(h); Eduqas F2.2b)

#### What you need to know

Viscosity, rate of extrusion, gas content and eruption frequency control landforms: fissure and central eruptions; subaerial and submarine; plateau (flood) basalts, shield, composite and caldera volcanoes; pyroclastic deposits.

#### Explained

| Landform | Magma and style | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Fissure eruption and lava plateau | Very fluid basalt erupting from long cracks, large volumes | Deccan Traps; Antrim plateau |
| Shield volcano | Fluid basalt from a central vent, gentle slopes | Hawaii |
| Composite (strato) volcano | Andesite; alternating lava and pyroclastic layers, steep cone | Mount Fuji, Etna (mixed) |
| Caldera | Huge explosive eruption empties the magma chamber; summit collapses | Yellowstone, Santorini |
| Submarine eruptions | Pillow lavas, hyaloclastite (shattered glass) | Mid-ocean ridges |

### Volcanic hazards and monitoring (OCR 3.2.2(i); Eduqas F2.2d, F2.2e)

#### What you need to know

- Hazards: blast and explosion; ash fall, pyroclastic flows (nuées ardentes) and gases; lava flows; debris flows and mudflows (lahars).
- The hazard depends on composition, viscosity and gas content.
- OCR: plot and interpret isopachyte maps (lines of equal thickness of ash).
- Eduqas: monitoring (ground deformation, gravity and thermal anomalies, gas emissions, seismic activity) and the extent to which risk can be managed.

#### Explained

| Hazard | Description | Mitigation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Lateral blast | Sideways explosion when a bulging flank collapses | Exclusion zones |
| Ash fall | Fragments fall over large areas; roofs collapse, crops buried, aircraft engines damaged, breathing problems | Strengthened roofs, masks, flight bans |
| Pyroclastic flow (nuée ardente) | Hot (up to 700 °C), fast (over 100 km h⁻¹) avalanche of gas and ash down slopes | Evacuation only |
| Volcanic gases | CO₂ (can suffocate in hollows), SO₂ (acid rain), fluorine (poisons livestock) | Monitoring, evacuation |
| Lava flows | Usually slow; destroy property | Diversion barriers, water cooling |
| Lahars | Mudflows of ash and water (rain, melting ice), fast, follow valleys | Hazard maps, warning systems, dams |

Basaltic volcanoes mainly produce lava flows; andesitic and rhyolitic volcanoes produce the most dangerous explosive hazards.

**Isopachyte maps**: lines join points of equal ash thickness. They form elongate oval shapes stretched downwind; thickness decreases away from the vent. The vent lies near the thickest part; the shape shows wind direction; volume can be estimated from the area between isopachs.

**Monitoring**:

- Seismic: earthquake swarms and harmonic tremor as magma moves.
- Ground deformation: tiltmeters, GPS, satellite radar (InSAR) detect swelling.
- Gravity: increase as dense magma accumulates.
- Thermal: infrared satellite images and cameras show heating.
- Gas: increased SO₂ and CO₂ emissions, changes in their ratio.

Monitoring allows forecasting days to weeks ahead for many volcanoes, but timing and size of eruptions are hard to predict, and false alarms erode trust.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying the asthenosphere is molten and is the direct source of all magma.
- Saying basaltic eruptions are always harmless. Fissure eruptions can release huge amounts of gas.
- Confusing pyroclastic flows with lava flows.

### Check questions

1. Explain why decompression causes melting beneath a mid-ocean ridge. (3)
2. Explain why magma is generated above a subducting plate even though the mantle wedge is not unusually hot. (3)
3. Describe three differences between a sill and a buried lava flow. (3)
4. Explain why rhyolitic magma is more viscous than basaltic magma. (3)
5. Explain why andesitic volcanoes tend to erupt explosively. (4)
6. An isopachyte map shows ash thickness lines elongated towards the east. Describe what this shows and where the vent is likely to be. (2)
7. Describe how ground deformation and gas monitoring can help forecast an eruption. (4)
8. Evaluate the extent to which volcanic hazards can be managed. (6)

### Mark scheme

1. Mantle rises rapidly (1); temperature stays almost constant / adiabatic (1); pressure falls, lowering the solidus so mantle crosses it and partially melts (1).
2. Subducting slab releases water when heated (1); water lowers the melting point of the mantle wedge (1); wedge crosses its (wet) solidus and partially melts (1).
3. Any three: sill bakes above and below, lava only below; sill may be transgressive; lava has vesicular / weathered top; fragments of lava in the overlying bed; sill coarser in centre.
4. More silica (1); more polymerisation / linked tetrahedra (1); lower temperature (1).
5. High viscosity (1); high gas content (1); gas cannot escape easily so pressure builds (1); magma fragments explosively into ash / pyroclasts (1).
6. Wind was blowing to the east (1); vent at the west end where ash is thickest (1).
7. Rising magma causes swelling measured by tiltmeters / GPS / InSAR (1); rate of inflation shows magma accumulating (1); gas emissions such as SO₂ increase as magma nears the surface (1); changes in gas ratios / sudden drop may indicate blockage (1).
8. Level-marked: monitoring and forecasting (1); hazard mapping and land-use planning (1); evacuation success, e.g. warning before an eruption (1); engineering, e.g. lava diversion, lahar barriers (1); limits: pyroclastic flows cannot be stopped, timing uncertain, poor countries lack monitoring (1); judgement (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/11-rock-deformation-and-structures.md -->

## Rock deformation and geological structures

Tectonic forces deform rocks into folds and faults. This file covers how rocks respond to stress, how to describe and measure structures, and what structures form at each type of plate boundary. Map interpretation and thickness calculations are in [Eduqas 02 Geological maps](../geology-eduqas-a480qs/02-geological-maps.md); competence and metamorphic fabrics in [17](17-metamorphic-petrology.md).

### Stress, strain and rock behaviour (OCR 3.3.1(b); Eduqas F2.3a, G2.1a)

#### What you need to know

- Stress is force per unit area; strain is the change in shape or size. Tension, compression and shear.
- Hooke's law and stress-strain curves: elastic and brittle behaviour, ductile (plastic) behaviour, elastic limit, permanent strain, fracture point.
- Temperature, confining pressure, strain rate (and time) control whether rocks behave brittlely or plastically.
- Brittle structures: joints, slickensides, fault breccia.
- Competent rocks (strong, tend to fracture) and incompetent rocks (weak, tend to flow).

#### Explained

**Stress-strain curve**:

1. Elastic region: strain is proportional to stress (Hooke's law); removing the stress lets the rock recover.
2. Elastic limit (yield point): beyond it, deformation is permanent.
3. Ductile (plastic) region: permanent strain without breaking (folding).
4. Fracture point: the rock breaks.

A brittle rock reaches the fracture point soon after the elastic limit (little plastic strain). A ductile rock shows a long plastic region.

| Condition | Favours brittle (faults, joints) | Favours ductile (folds) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Temperature | Low (shallow) | High (deep) |
| Confining pressure | Low (shallow) | High (deep) |
| Strain rate | Fast | Slow (over long times) |
| Rock type | Competent: sandstone, limestone, granite | Incompetent: shale, salt, mudstone |

**Brittle features**: joints (fractures with no movement, often from cooling, unloading or folding); slickensides (polished, grooved fault surfaces showing the direction of slip); fault breccia (angular fragments broken in the fault zone); fault gouge (fine clay-rich crushed rock).

#### Practical

**OCR PAG9**: model deformation using layers of plasticine, sand and flour in a squeeze box, or test the strength of different rocks or models; relate behaviour to confining pressure and rate.

### Describing and measuring structures (OCR 3.3.1(a); Eduqas F2.3b to F2.3f, G2.1b)

#### What you need to know

- Bedding: strike, dip, apparent dip; true and apparent thickness.
- Faults: fault plane, throw, dip, dip-slip, strike-slip, hanging wall, footwall, upthrow, downthrow; normal, reverse, thrust; dextral and sinistral; displacement (net slip).
- Folds: limbs, hinge, crest, trough, axial plane, axial plane trace, plunge, symmetrical, asymmetrical, antiform, synform, anticline, syncline. Eduqas A level: amplitude, wavelength, interlimb angle (open, tight, isoclinal), axial plane attitude (upright, inclined, overturned, recumbent), plunging folds; axial planar cleavage; plot limb data as poles on an equal-area stereonet.
- Unconformities.
- Recognise structures in photographs, maps, cross-sections and the field; field sketches; construct cross-sections; use a compass-clinometer; calculate crustal shortening.

#### Explained

**Strike and dip**: strike is the compass direction of a horizontal line on a bedding plane. Dip is the angle of maximum slope (measured perpendicular to the strike) and its direction. Written as 040/25 SE (strike, dip, dip direction) or as dip direction/dip (130/25). Apparent dip, seen in a section not perpendicular to strike, is always less than true dip: tan(apparent dip) = tan(true dip) × sin(angle between section and strike).

**Folds**:

| Term | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Hinge | Line of greatest curvature |
