| Limbs | The sides of the fold |
| Axial plane | Plane through the hinges of successive layers; its trace on the ground is the axial plane trace |
| Anticline / syncline | Oldest rocks in the core / youngest in the core |
| Antiform / synform | Up-arched / down-arched, used when the age order is unknown |
| Symmetrical / asymmetrical | Limbs of equal / unequal length and dip |
| Plunge | Angle of the hinge line below horizontal |
| Interlimb angle | Open (wide), tight (small), isoclinal (limbs parallel) |
| Axial plane attitude | Upright (vertical), inclined, overturned (one limb past vertical), recumbent (axial plane nearly horizontal) |
| Amplitude, wavelength | Height from the median line to the crest; distance between successive crests |

**Stereonet polar plots** (Eduqas): plot the pole (perpendicular) to each limb on an equal-area net. Poles from two limbs fall on a great circle whose own pole is the fold axis (plunge direction and amount). Only plotting poles is needed.

**Faults**:

| Type | Stress | Movement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Normal | Tension (σmax vertical) | Hanging wall moves down relative to footwall; extension; dip usually about 60° |
| Reverse | Compression (σmin vertical) | Hanging wall moves up; shortening; dip over 45° |
| Thrust | Compression | Low-angle reverse fault (under 45°); older rocks pushed over younger |
| Strike-slip (wrench) | Shear (σint vertical) | Horizontal movement along strike; dextral (right-lateral: the far side moves right) or sinistral |

The hanging wall is the block above an inclined fault plane; the footwall is below. Throw is the vertical displacement; heave the horizontal. Net slip is the total displacement on the fault plane. On the upthrow side, older rocks are exposed at the surface after erosion.

**Principal stresses**: σmax (σ1), σint (σ2), σmin (σ3). Faults form at about 30° to σmax, containing σint. So the orientation of σmax, σint and σmin decides fault type.

**Unconformities**: see [06](06-geological-time-and-dating.md).

**Crustal shortening**: measure the length of a folded layer along its curves (original length L₀) and the present horizontal distance (L). Shortening = (L₀ − L) ÷ L₀ × 100%.

#### Worked example

A folded bed measured along its curves is 14.0 km long; the folds now span 9.8 km horizontally. Shortening = (14.0 − 9.8) ÷ 14.0 × 100 = 30%.

**Compass-clinometer**: place the edge on the bedding to read strike with the compass held level; rotate 90° and read dip with the clinometer down the steepest slope. Take several readings (valid sampling) and average; record in a field notebook with a grid reference.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG6**: measure dip and strike in the field or on models; field sketch structures with scale, orientation and labels; construct cross-sections from maps.

### Slaty cleavage (OCR 3.3.1(c); Eduqas G2.1b)

Compression causes platy minerals to align perpendicular to σmax, forming slaty cleavage parallel to the axial plane of folds formed at the same time (axial planar cleavage). In the field, where cleavage is steeper than bedding, the beds are the right way up on that limb; the cleavage-bedding intersection is parallel to the fold hinge, giving plunge.

### Structural reactivation and inversion (Eduqas G2.1d)

Older faults are planes of weakness that can move again under later stress (reactivation). Folds may be refolded, giving interference patterns. **Inversion**: normal faults formed during extension (bounding a basin) are later reactivated as reverse faults during compression, pushing the basin fill up (or thrusts reactivated as normal faults in extension). Evidence: a fault with normal displacement at depth but reverse at shallow levels; basin sediments thicker on one side of a fault but now uplifted.

### Earthquakes and tectonic environments (OCR 3.3.2)

#### What you need to know

- Elastic rebound theory.
- Transform boundaries: shear-dominated, stress transfer.
- Convergent boundaries: compression and shear; fold mountains, overfolds, isoclinal folds, nappes, thrusts.
- Divergent boundaries: tension; graben (rift), horst; spreading rate and topography; oceanic core complexes.

#### Explained

**Elastic rebound**: plates move steadily but faults are locked by friction. Rocks either side bend elastically, storing strain energy. When stress exceeds friction, the fault slips suddenly and the rocks spring back to an unstrained shape, releasing energy as seismic waves. Then strain builds again (the earthquake cycle). Movement on one segment transfers stress to neighbouring segments (**stress transfer**), so earthquakes can migrate along a fault (as along the North Anatolian Fault).

**Transform boundaries**: strike-slip faults with shear; bends in the fault produce local compression (pop-up ridges) or extension (pull-apart basins).

**Convergent boundaries**: compression shortens and thickens the crust: tight to isoclinal folds, overturned folds (overfolds), recumbent folds that may be sheared out into **nappes** (large sheets moved many kilometres), and **thrust faults** stacking slices. Gravity can cause sliding of thrust sheets off high mountains.

**Divergent boundaries**: tension produces normal faults. A **graben** is a down-dropped block between two inward-dipping normal faults (rift valley: East Africa); a **horst** is an up-standing block. Slow-spreading ridges (Mid-Atlantic) have a deep axial rift valley and rough topography; fast-spreading ridges (East Pacific Rise) have smooth, gentle topography and an axial high. At slow ridges, large low-angle normal faults (detachments) can expose lower crust and mantle at the sea floor, forming **oceanic core complexes**.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG7**: interpret deformation features in hand specimens and photographs from orogenic belts.

#### Common mistakes

- Using anticline when the age of the rocks is unknown (use antiform).
- Measuring dip along the strike.
- Saying a thrust is a normal fault because it is low-angle.

### Check questions

1. Sketch and label a stress-strain curve for a ductile rock and identify the elastic limit. (3)
2. Explain why the same sandstone may fold at depth but fault near the surface. (3)
3. Define strike and dip. (2)
4. Distinguish between an anticline and an antiform. (2)
5. Describe a recumbent isoclinal fold. (2)
6. A bed dips at 40°. A quarry face is cut at 30° to the strike. Calculate the apparent dip on the face. (2)
7. State the orientation of the principal stresses that produce a normal fault. (2)
8. Explain the elastic rebound theory. (4)
9. Describe the structures you would expect at a continental collision zone. (4)
10. Explain how a graben forms. (3)

### Mark scheme

1. Linear elastic region (1); elastic limit / yield point marked (1); long curved plastic region before fracture (1).
2. Deep: higher temperature and confining pressure (1); slow strain rate allows ductile flow / folding (1); near the surface low pressure and temperature so brittle failure / faulting (1).
3. Strike: direction of a horizontal line on the bedding plane (1); dip: angle and direction of maximum slope, perpendicular to strike (1).
4. Anticline: arch with oldest rocks in the core (1); antiform: arch shape, age order unknown (1).
5. Axial plane near horizontal (1); limbs parallel (1).
6. tan a = tan 40° × sin 30° = 0.839 × 0.5 = 0.420 (1); a = 22.8° (1).
7. σmax vertical (1); σmin horizontal (perpendicular to fault strike) (1).
8. Friction locks the fault while plates move (1); rocks deform elastically and store strain energy (1); stress exceeds friction and fault slips suddenly (1); rocks rebound, energy released as seismic waves (1).
9. Any four: tight / isoclinal folds; overturned and recumbent folds; nappes; thrust faults; slaty cleavage; regional metamorphism; thickened crust.
10. Tension / extension (1); two normal faults dipping towards each other (1); block between drops down (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/12-uniformitarianism-facies-and-sedimentary-environments.md -->

## Uniformitarianism, facies and sedimentary environments

"The present is the key to the past": by studying how sediments form today, geologists interpret ancient sedimentary rocks. This file covers the rock cycle and uniformitarianism, facies, sedimentary structures, graphic logs and the main depositional environments. Rock types and grain-size analysis are in [03](03-sedimentary-rocks-weathering-and-diagenesis.md); turbidites, deltas and Walther's law in [13](13-turbidites-deltas-and-banded-iron-formations.md).

### The rock cycle and uniformitarianism (OCR 4.1.1(a), (b); Eduqas F3.1a, F3.1b, F3.2f)

#### What you need to know

- Recognise the rock cycle in the field, photographs, diagrams and maps, including the origin of an angular unconformity.
- How uniformitarianism and the rock cycle model developed, including ideas of catastrophism, gradualism, mass extinctions and changing conditions and rates through time; the contributions of James Hutton and William Smith.

#### Explained

The **rock cycle** links surface processes (weathering, erosion, deposition, lithification), driven by solar energy, with internal processes (burial, metamorphism, melting, uplift), driven by internal heat. An **angular unconformity** shows a whole cycle: rocks were deposited, buried, deformed and uplifted, eroded, then new sediment was deposited on top. Hutton's famous unconformity at Siccar Point (Scotland), with gently dipping Devonian sandstones on near-vertical Silurian greywackes, convinced him that the Earth had gone through repeated cycles over immense time.

**James Hutton** (late 18th century): argued that present-day processes, acting slowly over vast time, explain rocks (later called uniformitarianism); recognised that granite was once molten and intruded other rocks; saw "no vestige of a beginning".

**William Smith** (around 1800): showed that each rock layer contains a characteristic set of fossils in a definite order (faunal succession), so strata could be traced across country; produced the first geological map of England and Wales (1815).

**Catastrophism** (earlier view): Earth's features formed by sudden, violent events (often linked to Biblical floods). **Gradualism**: change by slow, steady processes. Modern view: uniformity of process (the same physical laws) but not always of rate: long periods of slow change are interrupted by rare catastrophic events (large eruptions, impacts, mass extinctions). Conditions have also changed through time (for example no free oxygen early on, so banded iron formations formed then but not now).

### Facies (OCR 4.1.1(c); Eduqas F3.1c)

#### What you need to know

- A facies is a body of rock with distinctive characteristics (lithology, sedimentary structures, fossils) that reflect its environment of deposition. Lithofacies (rock characteristics) and biofacies (fossil content).
- Facies associations; facies as the basic unit of sedimentary geology; uniformitarianism applied by comparison with modern sequences.

#### Explained

A facies is defined by what you can see: grain size, composition, colour, structures, fossils. Each records particular conditions (energy, water depth, salinity, oxygen). Environments produce a group of related facies (a **facies association**): a river channel and its floodplain; a beach, shoreface and offshore muds. Interpreting an ancient environment means matching the facies association to one forming today.

### Sediment transport and sedimentary structures (OCR 4.1.2(a); Eduqas F2.1d, F2.1e)

#### What you need to know

- Characteristics of sediments transported by wind, glaciers, rivers and in shallow seas.
- Structures: bedding, laminations, cross-bedding, ripple marks (symmetrical and asymmetrical), graded bedding, desiccation cracks, salt pseudomorphs, imbricate structure, sole structures (load and flame structures, flute casts).
- Use structures to interpret way-up and environment in the field and on photographs; sketch them.

#### Explained

| Agent | Sediment characteristics |
| --- | --- |
| Wind | Very well sorted fine to medium sand; well rounded, frosted grains; no clay (carried away); large-scale cross-bedding |
| Glacier | Unsorted mixture of all sizes (till, diamictite); angular, striated clasts; no bedding |
| River | Moderately sorted; channel gravels and sands; floodplain silts and muds |
| Shallow sea | Well sorted sands near shore; muds offshore; marine fossils |

| Structure | Formed by | Shows |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Bedding / lamination | Changes in sediment supply | Layers under 1 cm are laminations; quiet water if fine |
| Cross-bedding | Migration of ripples or dunes; sand avalanches down the lee face | Current direction (dip of foresets); way-up (foresets curve into base, truncated at top) |
| Asymmetrical ripples | One-way current; gentle stoss side, steep lee side | Current direction; rivers, tidal currents |
| Symmetrical ripples | Waves moving back and forth | Shallow water with waves; way-up (sharp crests point up) |
| Graded bedding | Fall-out from a waning current (turbidity current) | Coarse at base, fine at top: way-up |
| Desiccation (mud) cracks | Mud drying out and shrinking | Exposure to air; V-shaped cracks narrow downwards: way-up |
| Salt pseudomorphs | Cubic halite crystals dissolved and replaced by sediment | Evaporation, hot arid climate |
| Imbrication | Flat pebbles stacked overlapping, dipping upstream | Current direction in rivers |
| Load and flame structures | Dense sand sinking into soft mud; mud squeezed up between | Rapid deposition; way-up |
| Flute casts | Scoured hollows on a mud surface filled by sand (seen on the base of sandstone) | Current direction (steep end upstream); way-up; turbidites |

### Graphic logs (OCR 4.1.2(b); Eduqas SP6)

#### What you need to know

Construct and interpret graphic logs of modern sediments and ancient rocks, including fossil assemblages, structures and directional data (palaeocurrents).

#### Explained

A graphic log is a vertical column drawn to scale showing each bed: the horizontal width shows grain size (wider = coarser), symbols show lithology, structures, fossils, and side columns show palaeocurrent arrows and notes. Start at the base. Look for trends: fining-upward sequences (river channels: coarse base becoming fine) and coarsening-upward sequences (deltas and prograding shorelines: offshore muds overlain by shoreface sands).

### Depositional environments (OCR 4.1.2(c) to (g); Eduqas F2.1e)

#### Explained

**Fluvial (rivers)**:

- Meandering rivers: single sinuous channel with a floodplain. The channel migrates sideways by eroding the outer bank and depositing a point bar on the inner bank. This produces fining-upward sequences: erosive base with pebble lag, cross-bedded channel sandstone, rippled finer sand, then floodplain silts and clays with roots and desiccation cracks. Channels form lens-shaped sandstone bodies within muds.
- Braided rivers: many shallow, shifting channels separated by bars, with abundant coarse sediment and variable discharge (glacial outwash, deserts). Mostly sand and gravel, little mud, sheet-like bodies.
- Alluvial fans: cone-shaped deposits where a steep mountain stream reaches a plain and drops sediment; poorly sorted angular breccias and conglomerates, debris flows.

**Hot desert**:

- Wadis: dry valleys with flash floods carrying poorly sorted gravels and breccias.
- Dunes (aeolian): very well sorted, rounded, frosted, red (haematite-coated) sands with large-scale cross-bedding (metres high, steep foresets).
- Playa lakes: temporary lakes in basins that evaporate, leaving muds with desiccation cracks and evaporites (halite, gypsum).
- Controlled by gradual wind action plus episodic high-energy flash floods.

**Shallow siliciclastic seas**: from the beach out to deeper water, energy decreases. Beach: well sorted, rounded sands, low-angle lamination, shell fragments. Shoreface and shelf above wave base: sand sheets reworked by currents and storms, cross-bedding and symmetrical ripples. Below wave base (where waves no longer stir the bed): muds deposited from suspension, with burrows and complete fossils.

**Shallow carbonate seas** (warm, clear, tropical, little sediment from land):

- Reef limestone: corals and other organisms in growth position, forming a rigid framework with no bedding.
- In front of the reef (fore-reef): broken reef debris (bioclastic limestone) on slopes.
- Behind the reef (back-reef lagoon): quiet, muddy carbonate (micrite) with whole shells.
- Oolitic limestone: ooids formed in warm, shallow, agitated water supersaturated with CaCO₃ (shoals).

**Deep-water carbonate seas** (above the carbonate compensation depth, CCD): the CCD is the depth (about 4 km) below which calcium carbonate dissolves as fast as it arrives, because cold, high-pressure water holds more CO₂. Above it, calcareous plankton accumulate:

- Chalk: very pure, fine limestone of coccoliths, deposited in quiet, clear seas with little land-derived sediment (the Cretaceous chalk of southern England).
- Micritic limestones: fine lime mud.
- Chert and flint: silica from siliceous organisms (sponges, radiolaria, diatoms) dissolves and reprecipitates during diagenesis as nodules or layers; flint in chalk. Forms where little siliciclastic sediment arrives (sediment-starved seas) and on continental slopes.

Below the CCD, only siliceous oozes and red clays accumulate.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG4 and PAG6 / Eduqas SP5, SP6**: describe sedimentary rocks fully in the field and in hand specimen; construct graphic logs for unfamiliar sequences; measure palaeocurrents from cross-bedding and plot them on a rose diagram (circular data); interpret environments.

#### Worked example

A log from base to top: erosive surface with pebbles; 3 m cross-bedded sandstone (foresets dipping east); 1 m rippled siltstone; 2 m red mudstone with root traces and desiccation cracks; then another erosive surface. Interpretation: meandering river. Fining-upward cycles record channel migration; current flowed east; floodplain was periodically exposed.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying uniformitarianism means rates were always the same.
- Reading cross-bedding current direction from the bed boundary instead of the foreset dip.
- Calling any limestone a reef limestone.

### Check questions

1. Explain how Hutton's unconformity at Siccar Point supports the idea of deep time. (3)
2. Distinguish between catastrophism and uniformitarianism, and explain the modern view. (4)
3. Define the term facies. (2)
4. Explain how cross-bedding can be used to show way-up and current direction. (3)
5. Compare sediments deposited by wind and by glaciers. (4)
6. Describe and explain the sequence produced by a meandering river. (5)
7. Explain why chalk does not form in very deep oceans. (3)
8. A sandstone is red, well sorted, with frosted grains and cross-beds 6 m high. Interpret its environment. (3)

### Mark scheme

1. Older rocks deposited, buried and lithified (1); folded to vertical and uplifted and eroded (1); new sediments deposited on top; each step needs vast time / several cycles (1).
2. Catastrophism: features formed by sudden violent events (1); uniformitarianism: slow present-day processes over long time (1); modern: same processes and laws (1) but rates vary, with occasional catastrophes such as impacts / mass extinctions (1).
3. A body of rock with distinctive characteristics / lithology, structures, fossils (1); reflecting its depositional environment (1).
4. Foresets curve tangentially into the base (1) and are truncated at the top, so the truncated side is up (1); foresets dip in the direction the current flowed (1).
5. Wind: very well sorted, fine to medium sand, rounded frosted grains (1–2); glacier: unsorted, all sizes, angular striated clasts, no bedding (1–2).
6. Erosive base with pebble lag (1); cross-bedded channel sandstone from point bar migration (1); finer rippled sands (1); floodplain muds with roots / desiccation cracks (1); fining-upward due to lateral migration of the channel (1).
7. Below the CCD (1); cold, high-pressure water has more dissolved CO₂ (1); calcium carbonate dissolves as fast as it accumulates (1).
8. Desert / aeolian dune (1); large-scale cross-bedding from dunes (1); frosted, well sorted grains from wind; red haematite coating from oxidising conditions (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/13-turbidites-deltas-and-banded-iron-formations.md -->

## Applied sedimentology: turbidites, deltas, Walther's law and banded iron formations

Some sedimentary processes are rare or hard to watch (turbidity currents in the deep sea) and some no longer happen at all (banded iron formations). Geologists understand them with physical principles, laboratory models and comparisons with modern environments. Basic sediment transport and the Hjulström curve are in [03](03-sedimentary-rocks-weathering-and-diagenesis.md).

### Physical principles and models (OCR 5.1.1(a); Eduqas G1.3a)

#### What you need to know

- Stokes' law (recall not required): v = g d² (ρp − ρw) ÷ (18η).
- Bedforms, flow velocity and grain size (flume studies).
- Flocculation of clay.
- Phi scale: recall and use φ = −log₂(D ÷ D₀).
- Model sedimentation in practicals: graded bedding, ripples, delta mouth switching.
- Eduqas: apply the Hjulström graph; slope and intercept of linear graphs.

#### Explained

**Stokes' law** gives the settling velocity v of a small sphere in still fluid: g gravity, d diameter, ρp particle density, ρw fluid density, η fluid viscosity. Because v ∝ d², halving the grain size quarters the settling velocity. So fine clay settles extremely slowly, which explains why clay stays in suspension and why graded beds form (coarse grains settle first). It applies only to small grains (silt and clay) in still water.

**Flume studies**: a channel with sand and flowing water. As velocity rises over fine to medium sand, the bed changes: flat bed with no movement, then ripples, then dunes (larger, with cross-bedding), then upper flat bed (planar lamination), then antidunes. Coarse sand skips ripples. So bedform type and cross-bedding scale indicate ancient flow velocity and grain size.

**Flocculation**: see [03](03-sedimentary-rocks-weathering-and-diagenesis.md). In salt water, clays clump and settle faster, so estuaries and deltas trap mud.

#### Worked example

Using Stokes' law: a quartz grain d = 0.05 mm (5 × 10⁻⁵ m), ρp = 2650 kg m⁻³, ρw = 1000 kg m⁻³, η = 1.0 × 10⁻³ Pa s, g = 9.81 m s⁻². v = 9.81 × (5 × 10⁻⁵)² × 1650 ÷ (18 × 10⁻³) = 9.81 × 2.5 × 10⁻⁹ × 1650 ÷ 0.018 = 2.25 × 10⁻³ m s⁻¹ (about 2 mm s⁻¹). A grain one-tenth that size settles 100 times more slowly.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG4**: settling tube: drop mixed sediment into a tall water column and observe graded bedding; flume or tray: increase flow to form ripples; delta model: sand fed into a tank with a slope, watch the channel switch (avulsion) as the delta lobe builds up. Control water depth, sediment volume and slope.

### Turbidity currents and the Bouma sequence (OCR 5.1.1(b); Eduqas G1.3a)

#### What you need to know

- Turbidity currents as dense, sediment-laden flows down underwater slopes; the Bouma model of a turbidite.
- Sole structures (flute casts, tool marks), rip-up clasts, climbing ripples, laminations; rates of deposition of turbidites compared with calcareous and siliceous oozes.

#### Explained

A turbidity current is a fast, turbulent flow of water carrying suspended sediment, denser than the surrounding water, so it rushes down continental slopes and submarine canyons. It can be triggered by earthquakes, slope failure or floods. As it slows on the basin floor it deposits a turbidite.

**Bouma sequence** (base to top), recording a waning current:

| Division | Description | Process |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Erosive base | Flute casts and tool marks (grooves cut by objects dragged along), seen as casts on the sandstone base; load casts; rip-up clasts of mud | High-velocity erosion of underlying mud |
| A | Massive or graded coarse sand to granules | Rapid dumping from suspension |
| B | Parallel-laminated sand | Upper flat bed |
| C | Cross-laminated sand, ripples, climbing ripples | Lower velocity, ripples migrating while sediment still falling |
| D | Parallel-laminated silt | Settling from a dilute current |
| E | Mud (pelagic or turbidite mud) | Slow settling after the current, then normal deep-sea mud |

Repeated turbidites give rhythmic sandstone-shale sequences (greywacke and mudstone, as in the Welsh Basin). Each turbidite is deposited in hours to days, while deep-sea oozes (calcareous and siliceous plankton) accumulate at only millimetres per thousand years. So the turbidite thickness represents almost no time compared with the thin mud between.

### Deltas (OCR 5.1.1(c), (d))

#### What you need to know

- Simple delta model (topset, foreset, bottomset) and more complex deltaic cyclothems: delta top (peat or coal, seat earth, channel sandstone), mouth bars, delta front, prodelta.
- Walther's law; comparison of Carboniferous delta sequences with modern deltas.

#### Explained

A delta forms where a river enters a standing body of water, slows, and deposits sediment faster than waves and tides remove it.

**Simple (Gilbert) model**: horizontal topsets (river deposits on top), steeply inclined foresets (sand avalanching down the front), and fine bottomsets (mud on the floor in front). Common in lakes.

**Marine delta environments**:

- Prodelta: offshore muds settling from suspension.
- Delta front and mouth bars: sands deposited where the channel meets the sea; coarser upwards as the delta builds out.
- Delta top (plain): distributary channels (sandstone), levees, swamps with peat (which becomes coal), and seat earths (fossil soils with roots below coal seams).

As the delta progrades (builds seaward), a vertical section shows a **coarsening-upward sequence**: prodelta mud → delta front silt and sand → mouth bar sand → delta-top channel sands and coal.

**Delta switching** (avulsion): the active lobe builds out, then the river breaks through its levee to a shorter route. The abandoned lobe subsides and is flooded by the sea, laying marine mud on top. The cycle repeats.

**Cyclothem**: a repeated sequence (in the Carboniferous Coal Measures of Britain) of marine shale with marine fossils → coarsening-upward mudstone, siltstone, sandstone → seat earth with roots → coal → marine shale again. Each records delta progradation, swamp growth, then flooding (by switching, subsidence, or sea-level rise). Modern analogues: the Mississippi delta (lobes switching every 1000 or so years).

### Walther's law (OCR 5.1.1(d); Eduqas G1.3b)

#### What you need to know

- In a conformable vertical sequence, the facies that succeed one another vertically were deposited side by side laterally.
- Marine transgressions and regressions; diachronous boundaries; extending 2D data (logs, sections) to 3D.

#### Explained

Environments sit side by side (beach next to shoreface next to offshore). As they shift (the shoreline moving seaward or landward), each environment's deposits are laid on top of its neighbour's. So a vertical sequence records lateral changes, provided there are no gaps (unconformities).

- **Regression** (sea retreats, or delta progrades): shallower facies on deeper ones: coarsening upwards.
- **Transgression** (sea advances): deeper facies on shallower ones: fining upwards.

Facies boundaries are **diachronous**: the beach sand at one place is older than the same beach sand further inland during a transgression. Using Walther's law with borehole logs, geologists predict where reservoir sands will continue between wells (3D interpretation).

#### Worked example

A borehole log shows, from base: marine mudstone, siltstone with marine shells, cross-bedded sandstone, rooted seat earth, coal. Walther's law: offshore, delta front, mouth bar or channel, swamp were neighbouring environments. The delta was building seaward (regression). A borehole further seaward would show the same sequence but with more mudstone and the coal thinner or absent.

### Banded iron formations (OCR 5.1.1(e))

#### What you need to know

- BIFs deposited in the Palaeoproterozoic under different atmospheric and ocean chemistry; importance as iron ore; photoferrotrophs; the Great Oxidation Event (GOE); oxidation of Fe²⁺ to Fe³⁺. Detailed redox chemistry not required; recognise minerals from given formulae.

#### Explained

BIFs are rocks of alternating thin bands of iron oxides (haematite Fe₂O₃, magnetite Fe₃O₄) and chert (silica). They mostly formed 2.5 to 1.8 billion years ago and are the world's main source of iron ore (Western Australia, Brazil).

**Formation**: the early atmosphere and deep ocean had almost no free oxygen, so iron dissolved from hydrothermal vents and weathering stayed in solution as Fe²⁺. In shallow water, photosynthetic organisms (cyanobacteria producing oxygen, and photoferrotrophs, bacteria that use light to oxidise Fe²⁺ directly) converted Fe²⁺ to insoluble Fe³⁺, which precipitated as iron oxide layers. Alternating bands may record seasonal or cyclic changes in productivity and supply.

**The Great Oxidation Event** (about 2.4 billion years ago): oxygen began accumulating in the atmosphere. Once the oceans became oxygenated, dissolved Fe²⁺ was used up, so most BIF deposition ended. BIFs cannot form in today's oxygen-rich oceans, so there is no modern analogue: an example of uniformity of process but not of conditions.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying turbidites form in shallow water because they contain sand.
- Applying Walther's law across an unconformity.
- Calling BIFs evaporites or volcanic deposits.

### Check questions

1. Use Stokes' law to explain why a grain of 0.01 mm takes 100 times longer to settle than one of 0.1 mm. (2)
2. Describe how the bedform on a sand bed changes as flow velocity increases in a flume. (3)
3. Draw and annotate a Bouma sequence. (5)
4. Explain why turbidites can be used to show way-up. (2)
5. Explain why a delta sequence coarsens upwards. (3)
6. State Walther's law and explain why it applies only to conformable sequences. (3)
7. Explain why a transgressive shoreline sandstone is diachronous. (2)
8. Explain why banded iron formations stopped forming after about 1.8 billion years ago. (3)

### Mark scheme

1. Settling velocity ∝ d² (1); (0.1 ÷ 0.01)² = 100 so velocity 100 times smaller, time 100 times longer (1).
2. No movement / flat bed at low velocity (1); ripples, then dunes with cross-bedding (1); upper flat bed / planar lamination at high velocity (1).
3. Erosive base with flute or tool marks (1); A graded / massive sand (1); B parallel laminated (1); C cross-laminated / ripples (1); D/E laminated silt and mud (1).
4. Graded bedding coarse at base fining up (1); sole marks on the base of beds / erosive bases (1).
5. Delta progrades seaward (1); higher-energy, coarser environments (mouth bar, channels) move over lower-energy prodelta muds (1); vertical sequence shows decreasing depth and increasing energy (1).
6. Facies that follow each other vertically were deposited side by side laterally (1); unconformities remove sediment / time gap (1); so neighbouring environments may not be recorded and the rule fails (1).
7. Shoreline moves inland over time (1); beach sand deposited at different times at different places (1).
8. Great Oxidation Event / oceans became oxygenated (1); Fe²⁺ oxidised and precipitated so dissolved iron used up (1); no longer large reservoir of dissolved iron in deep oceans (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/14-fluids-in-rocks-and-hydrogeology.md -->

## Fluids in rocks and hydrogeology

Water, oil and gas are stored in the pore spaces of rocks and move through them. The same principles control water supply, hydrocarbon reservoirs ([26](26-oil-and-gas-basins.md)), pollution and engineering problems ([21](21-engineering-geology.md)). Water resources from an environmental angle are in [Environmental Science 08](../environmental-science-aqa-7447/08-the-hydrosphere.md).

### Porosity and permeability (OCR 5.2.1(a); Eduqas G4.2a)

#### What you need to know

- Porosity controls storage of fluids; effects of depth, temperature; primary and secondary porosity.
- Permeability controls flow; effects of diagenesis, fracturing and capillary pressure (oil and water).
- Rock texture: grain size, shape, packing, sorting and cementation.

#### Explained

**Porosity** = (volume of pores ÷ total volume) × 100%. **Effective porosity** counts only connected pores.

- Primary porosity: formed at deposition, the spaces between grains.
- Secondary porosity: formed later: fractures and joints, dissolution of grains or cement (for example caves and solution channels in limestone), dolomitisation.

**Controls on porosity**:

| Factor | Higher porosity | Lower porosity |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Sorting | Well sorted | Poorly sorted (small grains fill gaps) |
| Packing | Loose (cubic) packing, about 48% | Tight (rhombohedral) packing, about 26% |
| Grain shape | Irregular, angular (in loose sediment) | Platy grains compact tightly |
| Cementation | Little cement | Much cement fills pores |
| Depth | Shallow | Deep: compaction and pressure solution reduce porosity |
| Temperature | | Higher temperature speeds cementation and pressure solution |

Grain size does not affect porosity in an ideal packing of equal spheres, but it strongly affects permeability.

**Permeability** is the ability to transmit fluid, measured in darcies (or m²). It needs connected pores, and large pore throats. Clay has high porosity (up to 50%) but tiny pores, so very low permeability. Coarse, well sorted, uncemented sand and gravel and fractured rock have high permeability. Diagenesis (cement, compaction) reduces it; fracturing increases it.

**Capillary pressure**: in a rock with two fluids, the one that wets the grains (usually water) clings to grain surfaces and fills small pores. Oil must overcome capillary pressure to enter small pore throats, so fine-grained rocks with narrow pores act as seals even if they have some porosity.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG8**: measure porosity of sediment by filling a known volume with water (volume of water absorbed ÷ total volume), or by weighing dry and saturated rock; measure permeability by timing a fixed volume of water flowing through a column of sediment of known length and area.

#### Worked example

A dry sandstone core weighs 220.0 g; saturated it weighs 236.5 g. Its volume is 100 cm³. Water absorbed = 16.5 g = 16.5 cm³. Porosity = 16.5 ÷ 100 × 100 = 16.5%.

### Darcy's law (OCR 5.2.1(b); Eduqas G4.2b)

#### What you need to know

- Q = −kA (h₂ − h₁) ÷ L, where Q is the discharge (volume per unit time), k the hydraulic conductivity, A the cross-sectional area, (h₂ − h₁) the difference in hydraulic head and L the flow distance.
- Hydraulic gradient = (h₂ − h₁) ÷ L (OCR: recall the hydraulic gradient but not Darcy's law); hydrostatic pressure.

#### Explained

Groundwater flows from high hydraulic head to low head. Head is the height to which water would rise in a well (elevation plus pressure). The minus sign shows flow goes "downhill" in head. Flow rate increases with conductivity, area and gradient.

Hydrostatic pressure at depth h in water: p = ρgh.

#### Worked example

An aquifer has k = 15 m day⁻¹, cross-section 2000 m², and head drops from 54 m to 46 m over 800 m. Hydraulic gradient = (46 − 54) ÷ 800 = −0.01. Q = −15 × 2000 × (−0.01) = 300 m³ day⁻¹.

### Hydrogeology (OCR 5.2.1(d); Eduqas G4.2d)

#### What you need to know

- Confined and unconfined aquifers, aquicludes, aquitards, the water table, piezometric (potentiometric) surfaces, recharge zones; springs (water) and seeps (hydrocarbons); Eduqas: cones of depression.

#### Explained

| Term | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Aquifer | A rock that stores and transmits water in useful amounts (porous and permeable: sandstone, chalk, fractured limestone) |
| Aquiclude | Rock that does not transmit water (clay, shale) |
| Aquitard | Rock that transmits water slowly (siltstone) |
| Water table | Top of the saturated zone in an unconfined aquifer; it follows topography in a subdued way |
| Unconfined aquifer | Open to the surface; water table free to rise and fall |
| Confined aquifer | Sandwiched between aquicludes; water under pressure |
| Piezometric surface | The level to which water would rise in wells in a confined aquifer |
| Recharge zone | Where water enters the aquifer, usually its outcrop |
| Artesian well | Well in a confined aquifer where the piezometric surface is above the ground, so water rises (or flows out) without pumping |
| Cone of depression | Lowering of the water table (or piezometric surface) around a pumped well |
| Spring | Where the water table meets the ground surface, often at the base of a permeable rock overlying an impermeable one, or at a fault |
| Seep | Where oil or gas reaches the surface |

The London Basin is a classic artesian system: chalk in a syncline, confined beneath London Clay, recharged where it outcrops in the Chilterns and North Downs.

**Cones of depression**: pumping lowers the water level most at the well and less with distance. Overlapping cones from many wells lower the regional water table, dry up springs, and near coasts can draw in seawater (saline intrusion).

### Groundwater quality (OCR 5.2.1(c); Eduqas G4.2d)

#### What you need to know

Controls from geochemistry (carbonates and sulfates), filtration by the aquifer, and residence time; connate fluids in sedimentary basins; sources of pollution.

#### Explained

- **Geochemistry**: water dissolves minerals from the rock. Limestone and chalk add calcium and hydrogen carbonate (hard water). Gypsum and anhydrite add calcium sulfate. Pyrite oxidation adds sulfate and iron.
- **Filtration**: fine-grained aquifers trap particles and bacteria, so water from granular aquifers is often clean; fractured limestones filter poorly and pollutants travel quickly.
- **Residence time**: longer contact with rock means more dissolved minerals; long residence also allows natural decay of bacteria and some contaminants.
- **Connate water**: water trapped in sediments when they were deposited, often very old and saline (brines) deep in basins.
- **Pollution**: nitrates from fertiliser, pesticides, leachate from landfill, hydrocarbons from leaking tanks, saline intrusion. Long residence times mean polluted aquifers take decades to clean.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying clay is a good aquifer because it has high porosity.
- Confusing the water table with the piezometric surface.
- Forgetting the minus sign or the units in Darcy's law.

### Check questions

1. Distinguish between primary and secondary porosity, with an example of each. (2)
2. Explain why a poorly sorted sandstone has a lower porosity than a well sorted one. (2)
3. Explain why clay is an aquiclude despite having high porosity. (2)
4. A core of volume 80 cm³ absorbs 12.4 cm³ of water. Calculate its porosity. (1)
5. Using Darcy's law, calculate the daily discharge through an aquifer with k = 8 m day⁻¹, A = 500 m², head difference 3 m over 600 m. (2)
6. Explain how an artesian well forms. (4)
7. Explain how over-pumping of coastal wells can lead to saline water in the supply. (3)
8. Explain why groundwater from a chalk aquifer is "hard" and why its quality can vary with residence time. (3)

### Mark scheme

1. Primary: pores formed at deposition, e.g. between sand grains (1); secondary: formed later, e.g. fractures / dissolution in limestone (1).
2. Small grains fill spaces between larger ones (1); less pore space per volume (1).
3. Pores very small / not well connected (1); water held by capillary and surface forces so flow negligible / very low permeability (1).
4. 12.4 ÷ 80 × 100 = 15.5% (1).
5. Gradient 3 ÷ 600 = 0.005 (1); Q = 8 × 500 × 0.005 = 20 m³ day⁻¹ (1).
6. Aquifer confined between impermeable layers (1); recharged at a higher outcrop (1); water under pressure; piezometric surface above ground level at the well (1); water rises without pumping (1).
7. Pumping creates cones of depression lowering the water table (1); freshwater head falls (1); dense seawater moves landward into the aquifer and reaches wells (1).
8. Chalk is calcium carbonate dissolved by slightly acidic water, adding Ca²⁺ and HCO₃⁻ (1); longer residence time means more dissolved minerals (1); but also more time for natural filtering / decay of bacteria (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/15-igneous-petrology.md -->

## Igneous petrology

Magma does not crystallise all at once. Minerals form in a sequence, and separating crystals from liquid changes the composition of the remaining magma. This explains why one parent basalt can produce a range of igneous rocks and why some intrusions contain valuable ores. Basics of magma generation are in [10](10-magma-intrusion-and-volcanism.md). The decay equation and radiometric ages (Eduqas G1.1a skills) are in [06](06-geological-time-and-dating.md).

### Substitution and solid solution (OCR 5.3.1(a)(i); Eduqas G1.1b)

#### What you need to know

- Ions substitute for others in crystal structures when their atomic (ionic) radius and charge (valency) are similar.
- Solid solution series: olivine and plagioclase feldspar.
- Zoned crystals.

#### Explained

**Olivine** (Mg,Fe)₂SiO₄: Mg²⁺ and Fe²⁺ have the same charge and similar sizes, so they substitute freely. The series runs from forsterite (Mg₂SiO₄) to fayalite (Fe₂SiO₄). Mg-rich olivine has the higher melting point.

**Plagioclase feldspar**: runs from anorthite (CaAl₂Si₂O₈, calcium-rich) to albite (NaAlSi₃O₈, sodium-rich). Ca²⁺ and Na⁺ are similar in size but differ in charge, so the substitution is coupled: Ca²⁺ + Al³⁺ replace Na⁺ + Si⁴⁺, keeping the charge balanced. Ca-rich plagioclase has the higher melting point.

**Zoned crystals**: if a plagioclase crystal grows while the magma cools, the first-formed core is Ca-rich; later layers are more Na-rich because the liquid has changed. If the crystal does not have time to react with the liquid, concentric zones remain, visible in thin section as bands under crossed polars. Zoning shows the magma cooled too quickly for equilibrium.

### Bowen's reaction series (OCR 5.3.1(a)(i); Eduqas G1.1a)

#### What you need to know

Bowen's reaction series: the discontinuous series (mafic minerals) and the continuous series (plagioclase).

#### Explained

As a basaltic magma cools, minerals crystallise in a predictable order (worked out by Bowen in experiments in the early 20th century):

- **Discontinuous series** (ferromagnesian minerals): olivine → pyroxene → amphibole → biotite. Each step is a reaction: early crystals react with the liquid to form a new mineral with a different structure (more polymerised silicate structure each time).
- **Continuous series**: plagioclase changes gradually from Ca-rich to Na-rich, keeping the same structure.
- The two series join at the bottom: orthoclase (K-feldspar), muscovite and quartz crystallise last, at the lowest temperatures.

Consequences:

- Minerals at the top (olivine, Ca-plagioclase) are typical of mafic and ultramafic rocks (high temperature); minerals at the bottom (quartz, orthoclase, muscovite) of silicic rocks.
- Olivine and quartz do not normally occur together.
- Weathering: minerals that form at high temperatures are least stable at the surface (olivine weathers fast; quartz is resistant).

### Binary phase diagrams (OCR 5.3.1(a)(ii); Eduqas G1.1b)

#### What you need to know

Interpret continuous and discontinuous binary phase diagrams: anorthite-albite (plagioclase), forsterite-fayalite (olivine), diopside-anorthite (eutectic).

#### Explained

A binary phase diagram plots temperature against composition for a two-component system.

**Continuous (solid solution) diagram: albite-anorthite**

- The upper curve is the **liquidus**: above it, all liquid. The lower curve is the **solidus**: below it, all solid.
- A melt of composition An₅₀ (50% anorthite) cools to the liquidus; the first crystals are richer in anorthite (read horizontally across to the solidus: perhaps An₈₀).
- As cooling continues, crystals and liquid both become richer in albite. If crystals keep reacting with the liquid (equilibrium crystallisation), the final solid has the starting composition An₅₀.
- If crystals are removed or do not react (fractional crystallisation), the liquid becomes ever more albite-rich, and zoned crystals form.
- The forsterite-fayalite (olivine) diagram has the same lens shape.

**Eutectic diagram: diopside-anorthite**

- Two minerals that do not form a solid solution. The liquidus has two branches falling to a lowest point, the **eutectic**.
- A melt on the diopside-rich side first crystallises pure diopside as it cools; the liquid becomes richer in anorthite and moves down the liquidus to the eutectic.
- At the eutectic temperature both minerals crystallise together until all is solid. The eutectic is the lowest temperature at which any liquid can exist; partial melting of a diopside-anorthite rock gives first a melt of eutectic composition.

#### Worked example

On an An-Ab diagram, a melt of An₆₀ starts crystallising at 1450 °C, forming crystals of An₈₅. At 1350 °C the liquid is An₃₅ and the crystals An₆₈. By the lever rule, the fraction of crystals = (60 − 35) ÷ (68 − 35) = 25 ÷ 33 ≈ 0.76, so about 76% solid at that temperature.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG3**: model crystallisation with salol and thymol, or salol in solution, to show sequential crystallisation and eutectic behaviour; observe zoning in photomicrographs.

### How magma evolves (OCR 5.3.1(b); Eduqas G1.1a)

#### What you need to know

- Assimilation, differentiation and fractionation change magma composition: magma mixing, fractional crystallisation, gravity settling, filter pressing.
- Eduqas: origin of the parent magma (mantle or crust); differentiation and fractionation (Bowen); gravity settling forming cumulates; contamination by xenoliths, magma mixing, enclaves; role of temperature, pressure and water in melting points.

#### Explained

- **Fractional crystallisation**: early crystals (olivine, pyroxene, Ca-plagioclase) are separated from the liquid, removing Mg, Fe and Ca. The remaining liquid becomes richer in Si, Na, K and water. A basalt magma can evolve towards andesite and, in small amounts, rhyolite.
- **Gravity settling**: dense early crystals (olivine, pyroxene, chromite) sink to the floor of the chamber, forming **cumulate** layers.
- **Filter pressing**: compression squeezes residual liquid out of a crystal mush, separating it from the crystals.
- **Assimilation (contamination)**: hot magma melts and absorbs country rock (xenoliths may remain as partly digested blocks), changing composition (often adding silica, potassium).
- **Magma mixing**: two magmas combine; incomplete mixing leaves rounded enclaves of one magma in another.

**Melting and water**: adding water lowers the melting point of rocks greatly (wet granite melts at about 650 °C, dry granite above 950 °C); higher pressure raises the dry melting point but lowers the wet one. This matters for melting in subduction zones and thickened crust.

### Layered intrusions and ores (OCR 5.3.1(c))

#### What you need to know

Layered intrusions and metal ores formed by magmatic differentiation: chalcophile cumulates and platinum group elements (PGE).

#### Explained

Large mafic and ultramafic intrusions (for example the Bushveld Complex in South Africa, the Skaergaard intrusion in Greenland, and the Rum layered intrusion in Scotland) show repeated layers like sedimentary beds: dunite and chromitite at the base, then pyroxenite, then gabbro and anorthosite. They form by crystals settling (or growing on the floor) as the magma chamber cools and is periodically recharged with new magma.

**Ores**:

- Chromite (Cr) forms dense cumulate layers.
- If a magma becomes saturated in sulfur, droplets of immiscible sulfide liquid form. Chalcophile (Cu, Ni) and siderophile (Pt, Pd, the platinum group elements) metals partition strongly into these droplets, which are dense and sink, forming thin but rich layers (such as the Merensky Reef in the Bushveld, a major world source of platinum).

#### Common mistakes

- Saying Bowen's series means all magmas end up as granite. Most basalt solidifies completely as basalt or gabbro; only small fractions evolve.
- Reading phase diagrams vertically instead of horizontally (crystal composition is read across to the solidus at the same temperature).

### Check questions

1. Explain why Mg²⁺ and Fe²⁺ substitute freely in olivine. (2)
2. Explain why plagioclase substitution is described as coupled. (2)
3. Describe how zoned plagioclase crystals form. (3)
4. State the order of crystallisation in the discontinuous branch of Bowen's reaction series and explain why olivine and quartz rarely occur together. (3)
5. On a diopside-anorthite eutectic diagram, describe the crystallisation of a melt with 70% diopside. (4)
6. Explain how fractional crystallisation can change a basaltic magma into an andesitic one. (4)
7. Explain how platinum-rich layers form in layered intrusions. (3)
8. A melt of An₇₀ cools to a temperature where liquid is An₄₀ and crystals An₇₅. Use the lever rule to find the percentage of crystals. (2)

### Mark scheme

1. Same charge (2+) (1); similar ionic radius (1).
2. Ca²⁺ replaced by Na⁺ needs another substitution to balance charge (1); Al³⁺ replaced by Si⁴⁺ at the same time (1).
3. First-formed crystal core is Ca-rich (1); liquid becomes Na-richer as crystallisation proceeds (1); cooling too fast for crystals to re-equilibrate so successive Na-richer layers grow (1).
4. Olivine → pyroxene → amphibole → biotite (1); olivine forms at high temperature from mafic magma (1); quartz crystallises last from silica-rich liquid; olivine would react / Mg-Fe rich liquid has little free silica (1).
5. Cools to the liquidus and pure diopside crystallises (1); liquid becomes richer in anorthite (1); liquid composition moves down the liquidus to the eutectic (1); at the eutectic diopside and anorthite crystallise together until solid (1).
6. Early minerals (olivine, pyroxene, Ca-plagioclase) crystallise (1); they are removed by settling / filter pressing (1); these remove Mg, Fe, Ca (1); remaining liquid richer in Si, Na, K = intermediate / andesitic (1).
7. Magma becomes sulfur-saturated, immiscible sulfide droplets form (1); chalcophile / PGE partition into sulfide liquid (1); dense droplets sink and accumulate in a layer (1).
8. (70 − 40) ÷ (75 − 40) = 30 ÷ 35 (1) = 86% (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/16-mid-ocean-ridges.md -->

## Mid-ocean ridges

The mid-ocean ridge system is the largest volcanic system on Earth, about 65 000 km long. Here new oceanic lithosphere is made, and hot water circulating through the young crust forms metal ores. Option T5 (Eduqas) builds on this in [Eduqas 05](../geology-eduqas-a480qs/05-geology-of-the-lithosphere.md).

### Spreading rate and ridge shape (OCR 5.3.2(a); Eduqas T5.2d)

#### What you need to know

- Relationship between spreading rate and sea-bed morphology: water depth, fast and slow ridges.
- Calculate spreading rates from different data (magnetic anomalies, radiometric ages, drilling, hotspot tracks).
- Calculate numerical ages from decay rates (recall of N = N₀e^(−λt) not required for OCR).

#### Explained

| | Slow-spreading (Mid-Atlantic Ridge, about 2 to 5 cm yr⁻¹ total) | Fast-spreading (East Pacific Rise, about 10 to 15 cm yr⁻¹) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Profile | Rugged, steep, broad | Smooth, gentle, low broad rise |
| Axis | Deep axial rift valley (1 to 3 km deep) bounded by normal faults | Axial high; no deep rift |
| Magma supply | Intermittent, small chambers | Continuous, steady magma lens |
| Faulting | Much normal faulting; core complexes | Less faulting |

**Depth and age**: as lithosphere moves away from the ridge it cools, contracts and becomes denser, so it subsides. Depth increases roughly with the square root of age: ridges stand about 2.5 km deep, old ocean floor about 5 to 6 km. Fast ridges have gentler flanks because the lithosphere has travelled further before cooling.

**Spreading rate** = distance ÷ age. Using magnetic anomalies: find the distance from the ridge axis to a magnetic stripe boundary of known age. Rates may be half-rates (one side) or full (both sides).

#### Worked example

A reversal boundary dated 4.2 Ma lies 105 km west of the ridge axis and 90 km east. Half-rates: 105 ÷ 4.2 = 25 km Ma⁻¹ = 2.5 cm yr⁻¹ west; 90 ÷ 4.2 = 21.4 km Ma⁻¹ = 2.1 cm yr⁻¹ east. Full spreading rate = 4.6 cm yr⁻¹ (asymmetric spreading).

Radiometric: a basalt has 98.5% of its ⁴⁰K left; t½ = 1.25 × 10⁹ years. t = (t½ ÷ ln 2) × ln(1 ÷ 0.985) = 1.803 × 10⁹ × 0.0151 = 2.7 × 10⁷ years (27 Ma). If it is 540 km from the ridge, half-rate = 540 ÷ 27 = 20 km Ma⁻¹ = 2.0 cm yr⁻¹.

### Evidence for ridge structure (OCR 5.3.2(b); Eduqas T5.2a, T5.2b)

#### What you need to know

- Ophiolite complexes and geophysical surveys: gravity (free-air and Bouguer anomalies), reflection seismic and geoelectrical (EM).
- Eduqas: oceanic crust has seismic layers 1, 2 and 3 (sediments; pillow lavas and sheeted dykes; gabbro), with evidence from ophiolites and ocean drilling.

#### Explained

**Ophiolite sequence** (top to bottom), seen in Troodos (Cyprus), Oman and the Lizard (Cornwall):

| Unit | Seismic layer | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Deep-sea sediments (chert, mudstone) | Layer 1 | Low velocity |
| Pillow lavas | Layer 2 | Basalt erupted under water |
| Sheeted dyke complex | Layer 2 | Hundreds of vertical dykes, each intruded into the last; records repeated spreading |
| Gabbro (isotropic, then layered cumulates) | Layer 3 | The frozen magma chamber |
| Peridotite (cumulates over depleted mantle harzburgite) | Mantle | The petrological Moho lies at the base of the cumulates |

Ophiolites are thought to be slices of oceanic lithosphere (often formed above subduction zones) thrust onto continents during collision. Ocean drilling has confirmed the upper part of this sequence directly.

**Geophysics**:

- Seismic reflection and refraction: velocities match the layers; a reflector at a few km depth beneath fast ridges marks a thin melt lens on top of a crystal mush.
- Gravity: free-air anomaly near zero along most ridges (isostatic compensation); Bouguer anomalies are low beneath the axis, showing hot, low-density mantle with melt.
- EM (geoelectrical) surveys: high conductivity beneath the ridge shows partial melt in the upwelling mantle (see [07](07-physical-structure-of-the-earth.md)).

### Making oceanic crust (OCR 5.3.2(c); Eduqas G1.1c)

#### What you need to know

Adiabatic (decompression) melting of the mantle; magma evolution; crystal mush zones; continuous and discontinuous magma chambers at fast and slow ridges. Eduqas: magma chambers interpreted from models and ocean survey data (for example ship-based surveys of slow ridges).

#### Explained

1. Plates move apart; mantle rises passively beneath the ridge.
2. Decompression melting of about 10 to 20% of the peridotite produces basaltic magma, which rises and collects in the crust.
3. **Fast ridges**: a continuous, long-lived but thin melt lens sits on top of a large zone of crystal mush (partly crystallised magma). Melt erupts as pillow lavas and fills cracks as dykes; the mush crystallises as gabbro.
4. **Slow ridges**: magma supply is low and episodic; chambers are small, short-lived and discontinuous along the ridge. Much of the spreading is taken up by faulting, and mantle rock may be exposed on the sea floor in core complexes. Crust is thinner and more variable.
5. Fractional crystallisation in the chambers produces cumulate gabbros and evolves the erupted basalts slightly (MORB, mid-ocean ridge basalt).

### Hydrothermal processes and massive sulfides (OCR 5.3.2(d))

#### What you need to know

Hydrothermal processes at ridges and the formation of massive sulfide ores; metasomatism; chalcophile elements.

#### Explained

Cold seawater sinks through cracks in the young, hot crust, is heated near the magma chamber (to 350 to 400 °C), and reacts with basalt. The hot fluid becomes acidic and reducing and leaches metals (Cu, Zn, Fe, Pb) and sulfur from the rocks. This chemical change of the rock by fluids is **metasomatism**. The buoyant hot fluid rises and vents at the sea floor as **black smokers**: when it meets cold, oxygenated seawater, metal sulfides (pyrite, chalcopyrite, sphalerite) precipitate instantly as black particles and build chimneys and mounds. Accumulations of these sulfides form **volcanogenic massive sulfide (VMS) deposits**, rich ores of copper and zinc. Ancient examples are preserved in ophiolites (the Troodos copper deposits, mined since ancient times; Cyprus gave copper its name). The process is advection: heat and chemicals carried by moving fluid.

Unusual ecosystems around vents depend on chemosynthetic bacteria that use hydrogen sulfide for energy.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG8 and PAG12**: model hydrothermal convection (heated water with dye), research and present data on ophiolites and vent systems.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying ridges are high because of volcanic piles. They are high because the lithosphere is hot and less dense.
- Calling the sheeted dykes "a single giant dyke".

### Check questions

1. Compare the shape of fast- and slow-spreading ridges and explain the difference. (4)
2. Explain why ocean depth increases with distance from a ridge. (3)
3. A magnetic anomaly dated 2.6 Ma lies 39 km from the ridge axis. Calculate the half-spreading rate in cm yr⁻¹. (2)
4. Describe the sequence in an ophiolite and relate it to seismic layers 1 to 3. (5)
5. Explain why the sheeted dyke complex is evidence for sea-floor spreading. (2)
6. Describe how melt is stored beneath a fast-spreading ridge. (2)
7. Explain how black smoker chimneys form. (4)
8. Explain why ophiolites are important to geologists. (3)

### Mark scheme

1. Fast: smooth, gentle, axial high (1); slow: rugged, axial rift valley, faulted (1); fast has continuous, high magma supply / buoyant hot mantle (1); slow has low magma supply so faulting takes up extension (1).
2. Lithosphere cools as it moves away (1); contracts and becomes denser (1); subsides isostatically (1).
3. 39 km ÷ 2.6 Ma = 15 km Ma⁻¹ (1) = 1.5 cm yr⁻¹ (1).
4. Sediments (layer 1) (1); pillow lavas (layer 2) (1); sheeted dykes (layer 2) (1); gabbro (layer 3) (1); peridotite mantle beneath, Moho at base of cumulates (1).
5. Each dyke intruded into the centre of the previous one as plates moved apart (1); repeated intrusion at the axis with no host rock between, shows continuous extension (1).
6. Thin lens of melt at the top (1); above a large zone of crystal mush (1).
7. Seawater circulates down into hot crust (1); heated and leaches metals and sulfur from basalt (1); rises and vents at the sea floor (1); cools on meeting cold seawater, sulfides precipitate forming chimneys (1).
8. Expose full sections of oceanic crust and upper mantle on land (1); allow direct study of layers that cannot easily be drilled (1); hold ores / record past oceans (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/17-metamorphic-petrology.md -->

## Metamorphic petrology

Metamorphic rocks record the temperatures and pressures they experienced, and the stresses that deformed them. This file covers how to read those conditions from minerals and fabrics, and how rock type and conditions control the style of deformation. The basic rock names are in [04](04-metamorphic-rocks.md).

### Mineral stability, grade and facies (OCR 5.4.1(a); Eduqas G1.2a, G1.2b, G1.2c)

#### What you need to know

- Minerals are stable or metastable at the conditions where they formed; changing temperature or directed stress over time grows new minerals with different stability fields.
- Mineral changes depend on parent rock composition and the temperature-pressure field.
- Metamorphic grade; metamorphic facies and stability fields; index minerals in mudstone: chlorite, biotite, garnet, kyanite, sillimanite; the Al₂SiO₅ polymorphs (andalusite, kyanite, sillimanite); retrograde metamorphism.
- Contact and regional metamorphism of mudstone and shale from low to high grade.
- Simple pressure-temperature-time (P-T-t) paths for contact and regional metamorphism.

#### Explained

**Index minerals in pelites** (regional metamorphism, Barrovian zones first mapped in the Scottish Highlands by George Barrow): chlorite → biotite → garnet → (staurolite) → kyanite → sillimanite. Each first appears at a particular grade. The parent must be aluminium-rich (mudstone) for these to form; a sandstone or limestone at the same conditions forms quartzite or marble, which do not record grade clearly.

**Al₂SiO₅ polymorphs**: andalusite, kyanite and sillimanite have the same composition but different crystal structures, each stable in a different field on a P-T diagram that meets at a triple point (about 500 °C and 0.4 GPa):

- Andalusite: low pressure (contact metamorphism, chiastolite in aureoles).
- Kyanite: high pressure, moderate temperature (deep regional metamorphism).
- Sillimanite: high temperature.

Finding one of them places the rock in that field.

**Metamorphic facies**: a facies is a range of P-T conditions that produces a characteristic mineral assemblage in rocks of a given composition (often named from metabasalt minerals). Examples: zeolite (low T and P), greenschist (chlorite, low-moderate), amphibolite (hornblende, plagioclase; moderate-high), granulite (very high T), blueschist (high P, low T: subduction zones, with blue amphibole glaucophane), eclogite (very high P), hornfels facies (high T, low P: contact aureoles).

**P-T-t paths**:

- Contact: pressure stays roughly constant (shallow); temperature rises then falls as the intrusion cools: a horizontal loop at low pressure.
- Regional (collision): burial raises pressure and temperature; peak temperature is often reached after peak pressure, as heat conducts in; then uplift and erosion lower both: a clockwise loop.
- Subduction: pressure rises much faster than temperature (cold slab): blueschist then eclogite.

**Retrograde metamorphism**: as rocks cool, high-grade minerals may change back to lower-grade ones if water is available (garnet altering to chlorite). Usually limited, because water has been driven off, which is why high-grade assemblages survive at the surface.

#### Practical

**Eduqas SP20**: the "metamorphic aureole" simulation: model heat spreading from a hot "intrusion" into surrounding material (for example a heated block or a model with thermochromic material) and relate temperature with distance to the zones of an aureole. **OCR PAG7**: plot isograds on a map from mineral data.

### Isograds (OCR 5.4.1(a)(ii))

An isograd is a line on a map joining points where an index mineral first appears. It represents a surface of roughly equal metamorphic grade. Spacing shows the gradient: closely spaced isograds mean a rapid change in grade (steep thermal gradient, as around an intrusion); widely spaced isograds mean gradual change (regional). Isograds cutting across folds show metamorphism outlasted or post-dated folding.

#### Worked example

On a map, the garnet isograd lies 4 km from the sillimanite isograd. If garnet first appears at about 500 °C and sillimanite at about 650 °C, the average horizontal gradient of peak temperature = 150 ÷ 4 = 37.5 °C km⁻¹.

### Metamorphic fabrics and textures (OCR 5.4.1(b); Eduqas G1.2d)

#### What you need to know

- Fabrics form from directed stress over time during orogeny: slaty cleavage, schistosity, gneissose banding, porphyroblastic and granoblastic textures, crenulation cleavage. Use fabrics to reconstruct conditions.
- Eduqas: granoblastic, porphyroblastic, mylonitic textures; contact, regional and dynamic metamorphism with recrystallisation, ductile flow and shear.
- Recognise fabrics in samples, photographs and thin-section diagrams.

#### Explained

| Fabric | Description | Conditions |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Slaty cleavage | Very fine aligned clay and mica; rock splits into sheets | Low grade, compression |
| Schistosity | Visible aligned mica flakes, wavy | Medium grade |
| Gneissose banding | Segregated light (quartz, feldspar) and dark (biotite, hornblende) bands | High grade |
| Porphyroblastic | Large new crystals (porphyroblasts, for example garnet) in a finer matrix; may contain inclusion trails | Growth during or after deformation |
| Granoblastic | Equal-sized interlocking polygonal grains, no alignment | Contact metamorphism or high-grade with no stress (hornfels, marble, quartzite) |
| Crenulation cleavage | An earlier cleavage folded into small crinkles, with a new cleavage along the crinkle limbs | Two deformation events |
| Mylonitic | Very fine, streaky, strongly sheared rock with stretched grains | Dynamic metamorphism in ductile shear zones |

Inclusion trails in porphyroblasts preserve an earlier fabric. If the trails are straight and continuous with the matrix fabric, the crystal grew after deformation; if curved (S-shaped), it rotated as it grew during deformation.

### Deformation style: competence and conditions (OCR 5.4.1(c); Eduqas G2.1a)

#### What you need to know

- The composition of the rock (competent and incompetent) and conditions (strain rate, temperature, pressure) decide whether rocks fracture or fold.
- Shortening by slip, buckling and pressure solution; boudinage; refolding.

#### Explained

- **Competent** rocks (sandstone, limestone, granite) are strong: they fracture or form open, rounded folds (buckling) and keep their thickness.
- **Incompetent** rocks (shale, mudstone, salt) are weak: they flow, thicken in fold hinges and thin on limbs (similar folds), and develop cleavage.
- In mixed sequences, competent layers buckle while incompetent layers flow around them. Slip between layers (flexural slip) allows folding.
- **Pressure solution**: minerals dissolve where stress is highest and redeposit elsewhere, shortening rock without fracturing (stylolites, cleavage seams).
- **Boudinage**: when a competent layer within incompetent rock is stretched, it necks and breaks into sausage-shaped blocks (boudins) while the weaker rock flows into the gaps.
- **Refolding**: a second deformation folds earlier folds, producing interference patterns (domes and basins, hooks).
- Higher temperature and confining pressure, and slower strain rate, favour ductile behaviour (see [11](11-rock-deformation-and-structures.md)).

#### Common mistakes

- Using index minerals in a limestone or sandstone. They need an aluminium-rich parent.
- Saying andalusite, kyanite and sillimanite are different compositions.

### Check questions

1. Explain why a mudstone records metamorphic grade more clearly than a pure quartz sandstone. (2)
2. Describe the sequence of index minerals in regionally metamorphosed pelites. (2)
3. A schist contains kyanite and garnet. Another contains andalusite and cordierite. Compare the conditions in which each formed. (4)
4. Sketch the P-T-t path for regional metamorphism in a collision zone and explain its shape. (4)
5. Explain why high-grade minerals usually survive when rocks return to the surface. (2)
6. Distinguish between granoblastic and porphyroblastic textures. (2)
7. Explain how boudinage forms. (3)
8. Isograds are closely spaced in one part of a map and widely spaced elsewhere. Suggest an explanation. (2)

### Mark scheme

1. Mudstone is Al-rich so forms a series of index minerals at different grades (1); quartz sandstone only recrystallises to quartzite, same mineral at all grades (1).
2. Chlorite → biotite → garnet → (staurolite) → kyanite → sillimanite (2; 1 for partially correct order).
3. Kyanite: high pressure / deep burial, regional (1); garnet moderate-high temperature (1); andalusite: low pressure (1); with cordierite: high temperature contact metamorphism (1).
4. Clockwise loop (1); burial raises pressure first (1); temperature continues rising after peak pressure as heat conducts / radiogenic heat (1); uplift and erosion lower P and T (1).
5. Water driven off during prograde metamorphism (1); retrograde reactions need water so are slow / limited (1).
6. Granoblastic: equal-sized interlocking polygonal grains, no alignment (1); porphyroblastic: large new crystals in a finer matrix (1).
7. Competent layer within incompetent rock (1); stretched / extended (1); competent layer necks and breaks into blocks while weak rock flows into gaps (1).
8. Closely spaced: steep thermal gradient, e.g. near an intrusion (1); widely spaced: gradual change in regional metamorphism (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/18-mining-geology.md -->

## Mining geology

Metals are present in the crust only at low concentrations. Geological processes must concentrate them before mining is worthwhile. This file covers how ores form, how they are found, how they are mined and processed, and how the environmental effects of mines are managed. Eduqas-specific deposits (residual and precipitated ores, china clay, aggregates, coal) are in [Eduqas 01](../geology-eduqas-a480qs/01-economic-minerals-coal-and-bulk-materials.md). The environmental science view is in [Environmental Science 09](../environmental-science-aqa-7447/09-mineral-resources.md).

### Concentration and key terms (OCR 5.5.1(a))

#### What you need to know

Resource, reserve, ore mineral, gangue mineral, average crustal abundance, cut-off grade, concentration factor.

#### Explained

| Term | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Ore | Rock containing a metal (or mineral) that can be extracted at a profit |
| Ore mineral | The valuable mineral (chalcopyrite for copper, galena for lead, cassiterite for tin, sphalerite for zinc) |
| Gangue | Worthless minerals in the ore (quartz, calcite, barite, fluorite), discarded as waste or sold separately |
| Resource | All of the material that may be extractable now or in future |
| Reserve | The known, economically extractable part |
| Average crustal abundance | Mean concentration of an element in the crust |
| Grade | Concentration of the metal in the ore |
| Cut-off grade | The lowest grade worth mining, depending on price and costs |
| Concentration factor | Ore grade ÷ average crustal abundance |

#### Worked example

Lead has a crustal abundance of 0.0013%. If the cut-off grade is 2%, the minimum concentration factor = 2 ÷ 0.0013 ≈ 1540. A lode with 6% Pb is 4600 times enriched.

Tonnage: a vein 400 m long, 2 m wide and mined to 150 m depth, with ore density 3.2 t m⁻³: volume = 400 × 2 × 150 = 120 000 m³; mass = 384 000 t. At 6% Pb, contained lead = 23 040 t.

### Processes that concentrate metals

#### Magmatic and hydrothermal (OCR 5.3.1(c), 5.3.2(d); Eduqas G4.1a)

Magmatic segregation (layered intrusions, chromite and PGE layers) is in [15](15-igneous-petrology.md); volcanogenic massive sulfides at ridges in [16](16-mid-ocean-ridges.md).

**Hydrothermal veins** (Eduqas G4.1a): hot, watery fluids released from cooling granite, or groundwater heated by it, dissolve metals and carry them through fractures. As the fluid cools, or its pressure or chemistry changes, minerals precipitate in veins (lodes), often zoned outward from the granite with higher-temperature minerals (tin, tungsten) closest and lower-temperature ones (lead, zinc) further away, as in Cornwall. Gangue (quartz, fluorite, barite, calcite) precipitates too. Map relations: veins follow faults and joints, often radiating from or near a granite.

#### Secondary enrichment of copper (OCR 5.5.1(b))

Above the water table, oxygenated rainwater oxidises primary sulfides such as chalcopyrite. Copper dissolves (as copper sulfate in acidic water) and is carried down, leaving a leached, iron-oxide-rich cap (gossan) at the surface. At and below the water table, conditions are reducing, so the copper reprecipitates as secondary copper sulfides (and native copper) on primary minerals. This forms a thin but rich enrichment zone, which can double or treble the grade. Gossans are a useful prospecting clue.

#### Placer deposits (OCR 5.5.1(c); Eduqas G4.1a)

Placers form where dense, hard, chemically resistant minerals are weathered out of rocks and concentrated by moving water. Light grains are carried away; heavy grains are dropped where velocity falls: inside meander bends, in hollows and potholes on the river bed, below waterfalls, at the base of gravels, and on beaches sorted by waves. Typical placer minerals: gold (very dense, unreactive, malleable so it does not break up), cassiterite (dense, hard, resistant), diamond (very hard, resistant). Placers are cheap to mine (loose sediment).

### Exploration (OCR 5.5.1(d), (e), (f); Eduqas G4.3a)

#### What you need to know

- Geophysical methods: magnetic, gravity (Bouguer), seismic and electrical (EM, resistivity) surveys; choosing methods for different targets; limitations.
- Geochemical methods: sampling and analysing stream (river) water, stream sediment, soil and vegetation.
- Integrating existing data and follow-up surveys: target selection, exploration drilling, estimating reserves, limitations.

#### Explained

**Geophysical**:

| Method | Detects | Example target |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Magnetic | Variations in magnetic field from magnetic minerals | Magnetite, iron ores, some sulfide bodies, mafic intrusions |
| Gravity (Bouguer anomaly) | Density contrasts | Dense sulfide or iron ore (positive anomaly); low-density granite (negative) |
| Electrical / EM / resistivity | Conductivity | Massive sulfides (highly conductive) |
| Seismic | Layering and structure | Mainly for oil and coal; less useful for irregular ore bodies |

Limitations: anomalies show physical contrasts, not metal content; other rocks can cause similar anomalies (graphite conducts like sulfides); depth resolution is limited.

**Geochemical**: weathering releases metals into streams and soils. Sampling stream sediments upstream along a drainage network finds where anomalous values begin, pointing to the source. Soil grids then narrow down the area. Some plants take up metals (or are indicator species), so analysing vegetation helps. Background concentrations must be established to recognise anomalies.

**Integrating data**: start with existing geological maps and satellite images (identifying favourable rocks and structures); then regional geochemistry and geophysics; choose targets where anomalies overlap; then exploration drilling (cores give grade, thickness and depth); interpolate between boreholes to estimate tonnage and grade. Limitations: sparse boreholes, variable grade, faulting; reserves change with prices.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG10**: analyse geochemical and geophysical data sets on maps (contouring anomalies, identifying targets), integrate fieldwork with secondary data.

### Mining and processing (OCR 5.5.2(a), (b); Eduqas T1.3a)

#### What you need to know

- Principles, economics and sustainability of surface and underground mining: open pit, stoping, longwall retreat; phased development and life cycle of a mine.
- Mineral processing: in situ and heap leaching, crushing, tailings disposal, froth flotation, smelting.
- Eduqas: problems of extraction: face stability, rock falls, subsidence, flooding, surface and groundwater pollution, waste tipping; ways to minimise them.

#### Explained

| Method | Description | Suitable for |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Open pit | Benches cut downward in a large pit | Large, low-grade deposits near the surface; cheaper per tonne; big visual and land impact; high waste-to-ore ratio |
| Stoping | Underground excavation of steep veins in "stopes" from levels and shafts | Narrow, high-grade, steep vein deposits |
| Longwall retreat | A long face in a flat seam is mined by a cutting machine; roof supports move forward and the roof collapses behind | Flat-lying coal seams; causes surface subsidence |

**Life cycle**: exploration, feasibility, planning and permission, development, production (often in phases), closure, restoration and aftercare. Planning includes restoration from the start.

**Processing**:

- Crushing and grinding release ore minerals from gangue.
- Froth flotation: powdered ore is mixed with water and chemicals that make sulfide grains water-repellent; air bubbles carry them to the surface froth, which is skimmed off; gangue sinks.
- Tailings (fine waste) go into tailings dams; failures of these dams can cause disasters, so design and monitoring matter.
- Smelting: heating concentrates (often with coke) to extract metal; releases SO₂ (now captured to make sulfuric acid).
- Heap leaching: crushed low-grade ore piled on lined pads and sprayed with a solvent (acid for copper, cyanide for gold); metal-rich solution is collected. In situ leaching pumps solvent through the ore underground. Both cheap but risk pollution.

**Problems of extraction and controls**: unstable faces (benching, slope angles, drainage, rock bolts); rock falls (netting, scaling); subsidence (leaving pillars, backfill); flooding (pumping, grouting); pollution (settling lagoons, treatment); waste heaps (stable design, revegetation).

### Minewater (OCR 5.5.2(c); Eduqas T1.3a)

#### What you need to know

Contaminated minewater from abandoned coal and metal mines: geochemistry (acidity, dissolved metals), passive and active treatment, barriers and dewatering.

#### Explained

When pumping stops after a mine closes, groundwater rises and floods the workings. Pyrite in the rocks, exposed to air during mining, has oxidised; the water dissolves the products, becoming acidic and rich in iron, sulfate and other metals (zinc, cadmium, copper). It discharges at the surface where it overflows, staining rivers orange with iron hydroxide (ochre) and harming life.

- **Active treatment**: pumping and dosing with lime to raise pH, then settling out metal hydroxides; effective but expensive and continuous.
- **Passive treatment**: water flows through aeration cascades and settling ponds, then reed beds (wetlands) or limestone drains, where bacteria and plants help precipitate metals; low running cost, needs land.
- **Barriers and dewatering**: keeping water levels low by pumping or sealing shafts, and reactive barriers in the ground that neutralise water as it passes.

### Old workings as repositories (OCR 5.5.2(d))

#### What you need to know

Geological controls on using former underground workings to store waste, including radioactive waste and carbon dioxide.

#### Explained

Good repository geology: low permeability host rocks (clay, salt, granite with few fractures) so groundwater moves very slowly; stable, aseismic area; no valuable resources (to avoid future drilling); predictable groundwater flow, with long paths and residence times; impermeable cap rocks above. Old mines raise problems: they are full of open voids, fractures and shafts that provide pathways for fluids, and they may flood. Salt mines are attractive for some wastes because salt is impermeable and creeps to seal cracks. CO₂ is better stored in porous rocks in depleted oil and gas fields beneath good caprocks than in mines ([26](26-oil-and-gas-basins.md)).

#### Common mistakes

- Using "ore" for any rock with metal in it, regardless of economics.
- Saying placer deposits contain light minerals.
- Saying gossans are rich in copper. They are the leached zone; copper is enriched below.

### Check questions

1. Define gangue and cut-off grade. (2)
2. Explain how secondary enrichment produces a rich copper zone below the water table. (4)
3. Explain why gold is concentrated in placer deposits but feldspar is not. (3)
