4. Suggest a suitable geophysical method for finding (a) a massive copper sulfide body (b) a magnetite deposit, giving reasons. (4)
5. Describe how stream sediment sampling can locate the source of a metal anomaly. (3)
6. A tabular ore body measures 250 m by 80 m by 12 m with density 3.0 t m⁻³ and grade 1.5% Cu. Calculate the mass of copper. (3)
7. Explain how froth flotation separates ore minerals from gangue. (3)
8. Compare active and passive treatment of minewater. (4)
9. Explain the geological factors that make a site suitable for a deep radioactive waste repository. (4)

### Mark scheme

1. Gangue: worthless minerals mined with the ore (1); cut-off grade: minimum grade that is economic to mine (1).
2. Oxygenated water above water table oxidises primary sulfides (1); copper dissolves and moves down (1); leaves a leached gossan at the surface (1); reducing conditions below water table, copper reprecipitates as secondary sulfides / native copper, increasing grade (1).
3. Gold is dense (1) and chemically resistant / malleable so survives transport (1); feldspar less dense and weathers to clay, carried away (1).
4. (a) EM / resistivity (1): sulfides conduct electricity (1). (b) Magnetic survey (1): magnetite strongly magnetic (1).
5. Sample sediment at intervals along streams and tributaries (1); analyse metal content (1); trace the anomaly upstream to where it begins / tributary entering, which points to the source (1).
6. Volume = 240 000 m³ (1); mass = 720 000 t (1); copper = 10 800 t (1).
7. Ore ground to powder and mixed with water and chemicals (1); chemicals make sulfide grains water-repellent so they attach to air bubbles (1); float in the froth which is skimmed off; gangue sinks (1).
8. Active: chemical dosing (lime) and settling, effective / controllable (1), expensive, continuous (1); passive: wetlands / limestone drains / settling ponds, low running cost (1), needs land, slower, less effective at high flows (1).
9. Any four: low permeability host rock; few fractures; stable / aseismic; slow groundwater with long flow paths; no resources to attract drilling; good cap rock; predictable geology.

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/19-earthquakes-and-geohazard-risk.md -->

## Earthquakes and geohazard risk

Earthquakes are a complex geohazard. Their impact depends on the energy released, how seismic waves travel and are modified by local ground, and how buildings respond. This file also covers how risk is analysed, forecast and communicated. How earthquakes are generated (elastic rebound) is in [11](11-rock-deformation-and-structures.md); mass movements and UK hazards in [20](20-geohazards-in-the-british-isles.md).

### Factors affecting earthquake impact (OCR 6.1.1(a); Eduqas T1.1a)

#### What you need to know

- Relationship between earthquakes and active fault zones.
- Absorption and attenuation of energy as waves travel.
- Moment magnitude (Mw), using given equations such as Mw = (2/3) log₁₀E − 6.1 (OCR wording; use whatever equation is given). Logarithmic scales.
- Modified Mercalli intensity scale: depends on size, depth, distance, ground conditions and building standards.
- Seismic hazards: ground shaking, liquefaction, tsunamis (after undersea earthquakes, landslides or eruptions).

#### Explained

**Magnitude** measures the energy released at the source. Moment magnitude is based on the seismic moment (fault area × average slip × rock rigidity), so it works for very large earthquakes. It is logarithmic: each whole step is about 32 times more energy (and 10 times more ground motion amplitude on a seismogram).

**Intensity** measures the effects at a particular place (Modified Mercalli, I to XII): what people felt and what damage occurred. One earthquake has one magnitude but many intensities.

Intensity is higher when:

- magnitude is larger
- the focus is shallow
- the place is close to the epicentre (energy spreads out and is absorbed with distance: attenuation; high frequencies are lost first)
- local ground amplifies shaking (soft sediments)
- buildings are poorly constructed
- the earthquake strikes when buildings are occupied.

**Tsunamis**: when an undersea fault displaces the sea floor vertically (mainly at subduction zones), the whole water column is lifted. Waves travel across oceans at 500 to 800 km h⁻¹ with low height; near the coast they slow and grow tall. Landslides and volcanic collapses also cause tsunamis.

#### Worked example

Using the given equation Mw = (2/3) log₁₀E − 6.1 (E in the units the question states): for Mw 7.0, log₁₀E = (7.0 + 6.1) × 1.5 = 19.65, so E = 4.5 × 10¹⁹. For Mw 6.0, log₁₀E = 18.15. Ratio = 10^(19.65 − 18.15) = 10^1.5 ≈ 32. So one magnitude step is about 32 times more energy.

### Ground conditions and liquefaction (OCR 6.1.1(b))

#### What you need to know

- Interaction of seismic energy with the competence of bedrock and soil; effect of velocity change on amplitude.
- Interaction of groundwater with seismic waves: liquefaction.

#### Explained

When seismic waves pass from hard bedrock (high velocity) into soft sediment (low velocity), they slow down. To carry the same energy, their amplitude increases, so shaking is stronger. Soft basins can also trap and resonate waves, prolonging shaking. Mexico City (1985) was badly damaged hundreds of kilometres from the epicentre because it stands on soft lake sediments.

**Liquefaction**: loose, saturated, fine sand or silt is shaken; grains try to pack more tightly, but water cannot escape quickly, so pore-water pressure rises until grains are no longer pressed together. The soil loses strength and behaves like a liquid: buildings tilt and sink, sand boils erupt, buried tanks float up, and slopes flow.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG2**: model liquefaction by shaking a container of saturated sand with a weight on top; investigate wave speed in different materials with a hammer source and a phone geophone app.

### Reducing impact through engineering (OCR 6.1.1(c); Eduqas T1.2e)

#### What you need to know

Civil engineering reduces impact; natural frequency of structures; aseismic building design; reducing ground accelerations; tsunami defences.

#### Explained

Every structure has a natural frequency at which it sways most easily. Tall buildings have low natural frequencies; short buildings high ones. If the ground shakes at the building's natural frequency, resonance makes the swaying grow and the building may collapse. Engineers design buildings so their natural frequency differs from the expected ground shaking, and add damping.

Aseismic design:

- base isolation: bearings (rubber and lead layers, sliders) between the building and its foundation reduce the ground acceleration transmitted
- cross-bracing and shear walls to resist sideways forces
- reinforced concrete with steel that can bend without snapping (ductile)
- dampers (large weights or hydraulic dampers) to absorb energy
- symmetrical design without heavy roofs or weak ground floors
- deep foundations into firm ground; avoiding liquefiable sites
- retrofitting older buildings.

Tsunami defences: sea walls, offshore breakwaters, coastal forests and mangroves, vertical evacuation buildings, warning systems and education.

### Seismic hazard analysis and communication (OCR 6.1.1(d); Eduqas T1.2a, T1.2b)

#### What you need to know

- Geohazard: the probability of a change in the geological environment of a given magnitude within a specific time period in a given area. Risk: the consequent threat of loss of life or damage to property and infrastructure.
- Risk assessment: nature of the hazard, probability and return period, communication to the vulnerable population.
- Role of geologists in building codes, disaster planning, public education and communication; limitations and usefulness of hazard maps for non-specialists.

#### Explained

Risk = hazard × vulnerability (× exposure). A large earthquake in an empty desert is a hazard but a low risk; a moderate earthquake under a crowded city of weak buildings is a high risk. Seismic hazard maps show the probability of exceeding a level of ground shaking (for example, peak ground acceleration with a 10% chance of being exceeded in 50 years). They are used to set building codes and plan emergency services. Limitations: based on the short historical record and known faults; unknown faults and rare large events may be missed; non-specialists may misread probabilities.

### Probabilistic forecasting and return periods (OCR 6.1.2(a); Eduqas T1.2b)

#### What you need to know

- Effectiveness and limitations of probabilistic forecasting.
- Calculate probability and return periods from an annual maximum time series: return period T = (n + 1) ÷ m, where n is the number of years of record and m the rank of the event (largest = 1).
- Communicate probability and return periods to non-specialists.

#### Explained

A **forecast** gives the probability of an event in a time period (for example a 30% chance of a magnitude 6.7 or larger in 30 years). A **prediction** states a specific time, place and size (deterministic), which is usually not possible for earthquakes.

**Annual maximum series**: list the largest event in each year, rank them from largest (m = 1), and calculate T. Annual probability = 1 ÷ T. The probability of at least one event in N years = 1 − (1 − 1/T)^N.

#### Worked example

A 59-year record. The third-largest annual maximum earthquake was Mw 5.4. T = (59 + 1) ÷ 3 = 20 years. Annual probability = 0.05 (5%). Probability of at least one such event in the next 10 years = 1 − 0.95¹⁰ = 0.40 (40%). Communicate as "about a 2 in 5 chance within 10 years", not "it will happen in 20 years".

Limitations: short records; events are not regular; the assumption that the future resembles the past; return period misunderstood as a timetable.

### Deterministic prediction (OCR 6.1.2(b); Eduqas T1.2c)

#### What you need to know

- Seismic gap theory; changes in ground conditions near faults; microseismic activity (including as an eruption predictor); tsunami warning systems.
- Eduqas: monitoring seismic activity, groundwater levels and pressure, ground deformation (creep meters, strain meters, tiltmeters), radon gas emissions, electrical resistivity. Evaluate success.

#### Explained

- **Seismic gap theory**: sections of an active fault that have not slipped recently while neighbouring sections have are likely to be storing strain and are the next to rupture. Useful for long-term forecasts but timing is uncertain.
- **Precursors**: foreshocks and microseismicity; ground deformation (tilt, strain, creep, GPS); changes in groundwater levels and well water chemistry; increased radon gas from opening cracks; changes in electrical resistivity of rocks; unusual animal behaviour (anecdotal). None is reliable: precursors often do not occur, or occur without an earthquake.
- **Volcanoes**: microseismic swarms and harmonic tremor predict eruptions more reliably than earthquakes are predicted ([10](10-magma-intrusion-and-volcanism.md)).
- **Tsunami warning systems**: seismometers detect a large undersea earthquake; ocean-bottom pressure sensors and buoys confirm a tsunami; warnings are sent to coasts minutes to hours before arrival. Very effective far from the source, less so close to it.

Overall, earthquake prediction has had very limited success; forecasting, building codes and preparedness save most lives.

### GIS in disaster planning (OCR 6.1.2(c))

#### What you need to know

Using geographic information systems to combine geological and geographical data for disaster planning and communication to non-specialists.

#### Explained

A GIS overlays layers of mapped data: faults, geology, soil type and liquefaction potential, slope, flood zones, population, buildings, hospitals, roads and schools. Overlaying shows where hazards and vulnerable people coincide, where to place emergency services and evacuation routes, and produces clear maps for planners and the public. Limits: data quality and resolution; maps can give false precision.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG9**: build a simple hazard map in GIS by overlaying geology, slope and population data.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying magnitude 8 is twice as strong as magnitude 4.
- Confusing magnitude (energy at source) and intensity (effects at a place).
- Treating a 100-year return period as meaning "next one in 100 years".

### Check questions

1. Distinguish between the magnitude and the intensity of an earthquake. (2)
2. Explain why two places the same distance from an epicentre may experience different intensities. (3)
3. Explain how liquefaction happens. (4)
4. Explain why buildings of a particular height may suffer most in an earthquake. (3)
5. In a 79-year record, an earthquake ranked 4th in the annual maximum series. Calculate its return period and the probability of at least one such event in 25 years. (3)
6. Distinguish between geohazard and risk. (2)
7. Evaluate the use of precursors to predict earthquakes. (5)
8. Explain how a tsunami warning system works and why it is less effective near the source. (4)

### Mark scheme

1. Magnitude: energy released at the source, one value (1); intensity: effects / damage at a particular place, varies (1).
2. Any three: different ground conditions (soft sediment amplifies); building standards; liquefaction potential; slope / landslides; population density.
3. Loose saturated sand / silt (1); shaking makes grains try to pack closer (1); water cannot escape, pore pressure rises (1); grains lose contact, soil loses strength and flows (1).
4. Each building has a natural frequency (1); if ground shaking matches it, resonance occurs (1); swaying amplified so collapse (1).
5. T = 80 ÷ 4 = 20 years (1); annual p = 0.05 (1); 1 − 0.95²⁵ = 0.72 (1).
6. Geohazard: probability of a geological event of given size in given place and time (1); risk: likely loss of life / damage resulting (1).
7. Up to 5: precursors such as foreshocks, radon, groundwater, deformation (1); sometimes observed before events (1); often absent or occur without earthquakes / false alarms (1); timing not precise enough to evacuate (1); forecasting and building codes more effective (1); judgement (1).
8. Seismometers detect large undersea earthquake (1); buoys / pressure sensors detect wave (1); warning issued to coasts with arrival times (1); near source, tsunami arrives in minutes, little time to warn (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/20-geohazards-in-the-british-isles.md -->

## Geohazards in the British Isles

The British Isles lie far from plate boundaries, yet geohazards cost a lot of money and occasionally lives. The most costly is shrinking and swelling of clay soils. This file covers clays, subsidence, landslides and other mass movements, slope monitoring and stabilisation, and evidence of past tsunamis. Small British earthquakes are covered in principle in [19](19-earthquakes-and-geohazard-risk.md).

### Shrinking and swelling clays (OCR 6.1.3(a); Eduqas T1.1c)

#### What you need to know

- Causes of physical and chemical change in clays in excavations: comparing kaolinite and smectite; dewatering, oxidation, compaction; forces from interaction with groundwater.
- Effects on structures and engineering solutions.

#### Explained

Clay minerals are sheet silicates. **Smectite** (montmorillonite) has weak bonds between its sheets, so water molecules enter between them: it swells greatly when wet and shrinks when dry. **Kaolinite** has strongly bonded layers that water cannot enter, so it barely swells. Clays rich in smectite (for example the London Clay, which also contains illite and smectite) are high-shrink-swell soils.

**Causes of volume change**:

- Seasonal wetting and drying: clay shrinks in dry summers and swells in wet winters.
- Trees and shrubs extract water by their roots, drying clay near foundations; removing trees lets the clay swell (heave).
- Dewatering for excavations or leaking drains changes moisture.
- Oxidation of pyrite in some clays forms sulfuric acid and gypsum, which can expand and attack concrete (sulfate attack).
- Compaction of loose fills.

**Effects**: shallow foundations move unevenly (differential movement), causing cracked walls, sticking doors and broken pipes. Swelling clays exert large uplift pressures on floor slabs.

**Engineering responses**: deeper foundations (piles below the zone of seasonal moisture change); reinforced raft foundations; compressible void formers under slabs to absorb heave; controlling vegetation near buildings (or not removing old trees suddenly); sulfate-resisting concrete; good drainage.

### Subsidence (OCR 6.1.3(b); Eduqas T1.1c)

#### What you need to know

Causes and effects of subsidence: shrinking clays, limestone (solution sinkholes) and mining subsidence; engineering to mitigate effects.

#### Explained

| Cause | Process | Mitigation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Shrinking clays | Drying clay contracts | See above |
| Solution of limestone, chalk, gypsum or salt | Groundwater dissolves soluble rock, forming cavities; the roof or overlying soil collapses (sinkholes, dolines). Leaking water mains and altered drainage speed it up. Gypsum (Ripon) and salt (Cheshire, where brine pumping caused subsidence) dissolve fastest | Site investigation (boreholes, geophysics), grouting cavities, rafts, controlling drainage |
| Mining | Collapse of old shallow workings (bell pits, pillar-and-stall coal mines) produces crown holes; longwall mining causes a broad, predictable trough of subsidence that travels with the face | Mine plans and shaft records; drilling and grouting old workings; flexible structures; compensation schemes |

### Landslides and mass movements (OCR 6.1.3(c); Eduqas T1.1b, T1.2d, T1.2e)

#### What you need to know

- Mechanisms and triggers of rock avalanches, landslides and debris flows: slope angle, lithology, weathering, load, groundwater, rainfall, ground vibration, vegetation cover.
- Characteristics and controls of slope failure in competent and incompetent rocks.
- Monitoring potential failures: ground levelling and surveying; microseismic events; borehole distortion (inclinometers); ground deformation (creep, strain, tilt); groundwater pressures (piezometers); electronic distance measurement (EDM), satellite and GPS.
- Stabilisation: slope stabilisation, drainage control, retaining structures.

#### Explained

A slope fails when the downslope force (shear stress, from the weight of material) exceeds the resisting shear strength (friction and cohesion). Factor of safety = resisting forces ÷ driving forces; failure when it falls below 1.

**Factors**:

- Slope angle: steeper means more driving force.
- Lithology and structure: in competent rocks (sandstone, limestone, granite), failure is controlled by discontinuities: rockfalls, planar slides along bedding or joints that dip out of the slope, and wedge failures where two joint sets intersect. In incompetent rocks (clay, shale), failure is through the material: rotational slumps on curved slip surfaces, and flows.
- Weathering weakens rock and clay.
- Load: building on the top of a slope or dumping spoil adds weight.
- Groundwater and rainfall: water adds weight and raises pore-water pressure, which pushes grains apart and reduces friction (effective stress falls). Most landslides follow heavy or prolonged rain.
- Ground vibration: earthquakes, blasting, traffic.
- Vegetation: roots bind soil and plants take up water; clearing them reduces stability.
- Undercutting by rivers or the sea removes support at the toe.

**Types**: rockfalls (free-falling blocks), rock avalanches, translational slides, rotational slumps, debris flows and mudflows (water-rich, fast), and slow creep.

British examples: coastal landslides on clay and soft rock cliffs (for example the Isle of Wight, Dorset and Yorkshire coasts), mine spoil heap failure (Aberfan 1966: a colliery waste tip on a spring became saturated and flowed onto a school).

**Monitoring**: survey markers and levelling; GPS and EDM measure movement to millimetres; satellite radar (InSAR) maps slow movement over large areas; tiltmeters, strain meters and creep meters; inclinometers in boreholes show the depth of the slip surface; piezometers measure groundwater pressure; microseismic sensors detect cracking. Rising pore pressure and accelerating movement warn of failure.

**Stabilisation**:

- Drainage: surface drains, deep drains and pumping to lower pore pressure (often the most effective).
- Regrading: reducing slope angle or removing weight from the top; adding weight (a berm) at the toe.
- Retaining structures: retaining walls, gabions, piles.
- Rock bolts and anchors pinning blocks to stable rock behind; wire mesh, shotcrete on rock faces.
- Vegetation.
- Toe protection from erosion (sea walls, rock armour).

#### Worked example

Simple rock slope stability: a block on a bedding plane dipping out of the face at 35°. If the friction angle of the surface is 30°, the block will slide when dry (dip > friction angle), unless cohesion holds it. If the bedding dipped at 25°, it would be stable when dry, but raised water pressure could reduce effective friction and cause failure.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG9**: investigate slope angle at failure for different materials and water content in a tray model; measure friction angles of rock surfaces with a tilt table.

### Tsunamis in Britain's past (OCR 6.1.3(d); Eduqas T1.1d)

#### What you need to know

Geological evidence for significant tsunamis in the recent geological past, their causes and future risk, including the Storegga Slide.

#### Explained

About 8150 years ago a huge submarine landslide on the continental slope off Norway (the Storegga Slide) caused a tsunami that struck the coasts of Norway, Shetland and eastern Scotland. Evidence: a layer of marine sand with shells and microfossils inland within peat and estuary muds, tapering inland; run-up heights reached over 20 m in Shetland. The slide was probably triggered by an earthquake on slopes destabilised by rapid sediment build-up after the ice age, possibly with gas hydrate release.

Other events: historical records and sand layers suggest the 1755 Lisbon earthquake tsunami reached south-west England; there is debate over a possible 1607 Bristol Channel flood (storm surge or tsunami).

Future risk: large slides on the Norwegian margin are thought unlikely in the near future since that sediment has gone; other sources include Atlantic earthquakes and volcanic flank collapse in the Canary Islands (debated). Risk to Britain is low probability but high impact.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying kaolinite is a swelling clay.
- Saying water "lubricates" slopes as the main cause of failure. The main effect is raised pore pressure reducing effective friction, plus added weight.

### Check questions

1. Explain why smectite clays swell much more than kaolinite clays. (2)
2. Explain why removing a large tree next to a house on clay can damage the house. (3)
3. Describe how limestone solution can cause subsidence. (3)
4. Compare slope failure in a well-jointed sandstone with that in a thick clay. (4)
5. Explain how heavy rainfall triggers landslides. (3)
6. Describe two methods used to monitor a slope at risk of failure. (4)
7. Evaluate methods of stabilising a coastal clay cliff. (5)
8. Describe the evidence for the Storegga tsunami in Scotland. (3)

### Mark scheme

1. Smectite has weakly bonded sheets so water enters between layers (1); kaolinite layers strongly bonded so water cannot enter (1).
2. Tree roots had dried the clay (1); after removal, clay absorbs water and swells / heave (1); uneven uplift of foundations cracks walls (1).
3. Groundwater / acidic water dissolves limestone forming cavities (1); cavities enlarge, overlying rock or soil unsupported (1); roof collapses forming sinkhole (1).
4. Sandstone: failure along discontinuities / joints / bedding (1); rockfalls, planar or wedge slides (1); clay: failure through the material (1); rotational slumps and flows (1).
5. Water infiltrates and raises pore pressure (1); reduces effective stress / friction between grains (1); adds weight to the slope, increasing driving force (1).
6. Two methods, each with description: GPS / EDM survey of markers measuring displacement; inclinometers showing depth of movement; piezometers measuring water pressure; tiltmeters; InSAR (2 each).
7. Up to 5: drainage lowers pore pressure (1); toe protection prevents undercutting (1); regrading reduces angle but loses land (1); costs and visual impact (1); may move erosion elsewhere / managed retreat alternative (1); judgement (1).
8. Layer of marine sand with marine shells / microfossils (1); within peat or estuary muds above high tide level / inland (1); dated to about 8150 years ago, thins inland, same age over a wide area (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/21-engineering-geology.md -->

## Engineering geology

Every large structure depends on the ground beneath it. Engineering geology assesses rock and soil strength, groundwater and structures before and during construction, so projects are safe and economical. Quarrying and mine-related problems are in [18](18-mining-geology.md); slopes in [20](20-geohazards-in-the-british-isles.md); using maps for site assessment in [Eduqas 02](../geology-eduqas-a480qs/02-geological-maps.md).

### Rock strength (OCR 6.2.1(a))

#### What you need to know

- Interlocking and cementation of minerals control strength; typical strengths of igneous, metamorphic and sedimentary rocks.
- Measuring strength under compression and shear; qualitative peak and residual strength (Young modulus not required).
- Rock density; lithostatic pressure p = ρgh; density of water.

#### Explained

- Igneous and many metamorphic rocks have **interlocking crystals**, so they are strong (granite, basalt, quartzite: very high compressive strength).
- Sedimentary rocks are grains held by **cement**; strength depends on cement type and amount: silica-cemented sandstones are strong, poorly cemented ones and mudstones are weak. Porous rocks are weaker.
- **Uniaxial compressive strength**: a rock core is squeezed until it breaks. Shear strength: a sample is pushed along a plane under a normal load.
- **Peak strength**: the maximum stress before failure. **Residual strength**: the lower strength after failure, once the surface has slipped (important for old landslides, which move more easily).
- Simple field tests: point load test on cores; Schmidt hammer rebound.

**Lithostatic pressure** = ρgh, the weight of overlying rock per unit area. This matters for tunnels and deep mines.

#### Worked example

Rock with density 2600 kg m⁻³ at 400 m depth: p = 2600 × 9.81 × 400 = 1.02 × 10⁷ Pa = 10.2 MPa. Water pressure at the same depth (if connected to the surface) = 1000 × 9.81 × 400 = 3.9 MPa.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG9**: measure rock strength with a point load tester or by loading model samples (for example plaster blocks with different "cement" content); measure densities.

### Weakening by weathering, fractures and pore water (OCR 6.2.1(b), (c); Eduqas T1.3b)

#### What you need to know

- Strength is reduced by weathering, fracture density and structures: bedding, joint sets, foliation, faults.
- Hydrostatic (pore-water) pressure reduces strength; resolve forces.
- Eduqas: ground instability from weathering, dip of strata, folding, faulting, cleavage, joint patterns and fracture density; interference with the hydrological system: pore pressure, drainage, porosity, permeability, water table, aquifers; simple rock slope stability with friction angle and orientation of discontinuities.

#### Explained

The strength of a rock mass is much lower than that of an intact sample, because it fails along **discontinuities**: bedding planes, joints, foliation, cleavage and faults. More fractures (high fracture density) means weaker rock and more water flow. Orientation matters: discontinuities dipping out of a cutting face (towards the excavation) at an angle steeper than their friction angle can slide; those dipping into the face are stable. Weathering opens joints, turns feldspar to clay and reduces strength near the surface.

**Pore-water pressure** acts outwards on grain contacts, pushing them apart. Effective normal stress = total normal stress − pore pressure. Friction resistance depends on effective stress, so raising pore pressure (heavy rain, a reservoir filling, blocked drains) reduces resistance and can trigger failure.

**Resolving forces on a block on an inclined plane** (dip θ, weight W): component down the slope = W sin θ; component normal to the plane = W cos θ. Friction resistance = (W cos θ − U) tan φ, where U is the water uplift force and φ the friction angle. Factor of safety = (W cos θ − U) tan φ ÷ W sin θ.

#### Worked example

A 500 kN block rests on a joint dipping 30° with friction angle 35°. Dry: FS = (500 cos 30 × tan 35) ÷ (500 sin 30) = (433 × 0.700) ÷ 250 = 303 ÷ 250 = 1.21 (stable). With a water uplift of 120 kN: FS = ((433 − 120) × 0.700) ÷ 250 = 219 ÷ 250 = 0.88 (fails).

### Site investigation (OCR 6.2.1(d); Eduqas T1.3b)

#### What you need to know

Integrating existing data and ground investigations: existing BGS maps and records; geological site mapping; drilling for cores; laboratory testing; slope mapping for slope stability risk analysis. Eduqas: analysing the suitability of sites using geological and geotechnical data.

#### Explained

1. **Desk study**: British Geological Survey maps and memoirs, borehole records, old mine plans, aerial photographs and satellite images, historical maps (for old quarries, landfill, industrial use).
2. **Walkover and geological mapping**: rock types, structures, springs, signs of instability.
3. **Ground investigation**: boreholes and trial pits; cores logged for rock type, weathering and discontinuities; groundwater levels in piezometers; geophysics to fill gaps (resistivity for cavities, seismic for depth to bedrock).
4. **Laboratory tests**: strength, density, clay swell potential, chemistry (sulfate, contamination).
5. **Interpretation**: a ground model with cross-sections, hazards and design parameters; slope stability analysis from mapped discontinuities.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG12**: use secondary data (maps, borehole logs) to produce a site assessment report.

### Tunnelling (OCR 6.2.2(a); Eduqas T1.3c)

#### What you need to know

- Engineering geology applied to tunnels through hard rock and unconsolidated material.
- Monitoring and mitigating the impacts of tunnelling, including management and use of spoil.

#### Explained

- **Hard rock**: drill and blast, or tunnel boring machines (TBMs). Stability depends on discontinuities: blocks can fall from the roof. Support with rock bolts, mesh and sprayed concrete (shotcrete), steel arches. Faults and crushed zones are weak and often carry water. Deep tunnels face high lithostatic stress (rock bursts in strong rock, squeezing in weak rock) and high temperatures.
- **Unconsolidated material** (clay, sand, gravel): soft ground needs immediate lining (segmental concrete rings) and pressurised-face TBMs to stop collapse and water entry. London Clay is ideal: soft enough to dig, strong enough to stand briefly, nearly impermeable.
- **Water inflow**: from aquifers and fault zones; managed by grouting, freezing the ground, dewatering, or pressurised machines.
- **Surface impacts**: ground settlement above tunnels can damage buildings: monitored with surveying and controlled by compensation grouting. Dewatering can lower water tables and affect wells and wetlands. Vibration and noise.
- **Spoil**: large volumes of excavated material; reused for embankments, land raising and habitat creation (for example new wetlands and coastal nature reserves built from tunnel spoil) where it is not contaminated.

### Dams and reservoirs (OCR 6.2.2(b); Eduqas T1.3c)

#### What you need to know

- Dam types: earth (embankment), gravity, arch; grouting; clay or geomembrane lining; cut-off curtain.
- Hydrogeological impacts: hydrostatic uplift, erosion of earth dams.
- Slope stability around reservoirs.
- Reservoir-induced seismicity.
- Impacts of historical and new dams.

#### Explained

| Type | Description | Geological needs |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Earth (embankment) dam | Built of compacted soil and rock fill with an impermeable clay core | Can be built on weaker foundations; materials available locally |
| Gravity dam | Massive concrete; its weight resists the water | Strong, unfractured bedrock foundation |
| Arch dam | Thin curved concrete wall transferring water pressure into the valley sides | Narrow valley with very strong, stable rock abutments |

**Leakage**: permeable rocks, joints, faults, solution cavities (limestone) or old mines under or around the reservoir can let water escape. Solutions: **grout curtain** (cement or chemical grout injected into boreholes to seal fractures); **cut-off trench** of clay or concrete under an earth dam down to impermeable rock; lining the reservoir with clay or a geomembrane.

**Hydrostatic uplift**: water seeping under a dam pushes up on its base, reducing its effective weight; drainage galleries and grout curtains reduce it. **Piping**: water flowing through an earth dam can wash out fine particles, forming channels that grow until the dam fails; prevented with filters and drains.

**Slopes**: filling a reservoir saturates valley sides and raises pore pressure; drawdown can leave slopes saturated without support. The 1963 Vajont disaster in Italy: a huge landslide on a slope with clay layers dipping into the reservoir fell into it, sending a wave over the dam that killed about 2000 people. Slopes must be mapped and monitored.

**Reservoir-induced seismicity**: the weight of water and increased pore pressure along existing faults can trigger earthquakes, especially in the first years of filling and in deep reservoirs on stressed faults. Evidence: increases in earthquakes linked in time and location to filling and water-level changes.

Other impacts: sediment trapped in reservoirs (reduced supply downstream, coastal erosion), loss of land and habitat.

### Waste disposal and radon (Eduqas T1.3a, T1.3b)

#### Explained

**Landfill**: needs low-permeability ground (clay) or engineered liners (compacted clay and plastic geomembrane), a leachate collection system, gas (methane) extraction, capping and monitoring wells around the site. Poorly sited landfill on permeable or fractured rock above an aquifer can pollute groundwater; methane can migrate through permeable strata into buildings. Former quarries in impermeable clay are better sites than those in limestone or sandstone.

**Toxic and radioactive waste**: needs multiple barriers (waste form, container, engineered backfill, host rock) and a geological setting with very slow groundwater movement ([18](18-mining-geology.md)).

**Radon**: a radioactive gas from the decay of uranium in rocks such as granite, some shales and limestones. It moves through fractures and permeable soils into buildings and accumulates in poorly ventilated rooms; long exposure raises lung cancer risk. High-risk areas in Britain include Cornwall and Devon (granites) and parts of Northamptonshire (ironstones). Protection: radon-proof membranes and sumps under floors, ventilation; maps of risk based on geology.

### Contaminated land (OCR 6.2.2(c); Eduqas T1.3a)

#### What you need to know

- Managing and remediating contaminated land and groundwater (brownfield sites).
- Heavy metal contamination: adsorption on clay minerals, solubility, bioavailability, pH. Organic contamination: hydrocarbons and solvents in soils and groundwater.
- Remediation: phytoremediation; stabilisation and solidification.
- Eduqas: issues at one industrial brownfield site: toxic chemicals, ground instability, subsidence, groundwater pollution.

#### Explained

Brownfield land (old gasworks, factories, mines) may contain metals, hydrocarbons, solvents, asbestos, unstable fill and old foundations.

- **Heavy metals** (lead, cadmium, zinc, arsenic): many adsorb onto clay minerals and organic matter, which limits movement. Their solubility increases at low pH, so acidic conditions mobilise them, making them more bioavailable (able to be taken up by organisms). Liming raises pH and immobilises them.
- **Organic contaminants**: light oils (LNAPLs) float on the water table; dense solvents (DNAPLs, chlorinated solvents) sink through aquifers and are very hard to remove.

**Remediation**:

- Excavate and remove to licensed landfill ("dig and dump"): quick but moves the problem.
- **Stabilisation/solidification (S/S)**: mixing cement, lime or other binders into soil to lock contaminants in place and reduce leaching.
- **Phytoremediation**: plants take up metals or help microbes break down organics; slow, cheap, only shallow.
- Bioremediation of hydrocarbons; capping with clean soil; pump-and-treat for groundwater; permeable reactive barriers.

#### Common mistakes

- Judging a rock mass by the strength of an intact sample.
- Saying grout curtains strengthen dams. They reduce seepage and uplift.

### Check questions

1. Explain why granite is generally stronger than sandstone. (2)
2. Calculate the lithostatic pressure at 800 m depth in rock of density 2500 kg m⁻³. (2)
3. Explain why raising pore-water pressure can cause a rock slope to fail. (3)
4. A 300 kN block lies on a joint dipping 28° with friction angle 32°. Calculate the factor of safety when dry. (3)
5. Describe the stages of a site investigation for a new road cutting. (5)
6. Compare the geological requirements for a gravity dam and an arch dam. (3)
7. Explain how a grout curtain reduces problems at a dam. (3)
8. Explain how reservoir filling can trigger earthquakes. (3)
9. Explain why heavy metals in brownfield soils are more hazardous if the soil becomes acidic. (3)

### Mark scheme

1. Granite has interlocking crystals (1); sandstone grains held by cement, may be porous / weakly cemented (1).
2. 2500 × 9.81 × 800 (1) = 1.96 × 10⁷ Pa ≈ 19.6 MPa (1).
3. Pore pressure pushes grains / blocks apart (1); reduces effective normal stress (1); reduces friction resistance below the driving force (1).
4. Driving = 300 sin 28 = 140.8 kN (1); resisting = 300 cos 28 × tan 32 = 264.9 × 0.625 = 165.5 kN (1); FS = 1.18 (1).
5. Desk study of BGS maps, records, mine plans (1); geological mapping / walkover (1); boreholes, trial pits, cores (1); lab testing of strength / chemistry (1); analysis of discontinuities and stability / ground model (1).
6. Gravity: strong unfractured foundation to bear heavy weight (1); arch: narrow valley (1) with very strong abutments in valley sides (1).
7. Grout injected into fractures beneath dam (1); seals pathways for seepage / leakage (1); reduces uplift pressure under the dam / piping (1).
8. Weight of water increases stress on faults (1); water penetrates fractures raising pore pressure (1); reduces friction on already-stressed faults so they slip (1).
9. Metals more soluble at low pH (1); released from clay / organic adsorption sites (1); more mobile in groundwater and bioavailable to plants and animals (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/22-the-changing-earth-climate-and-sea-level.md -->

## The changing Earth: continents, climate and sea level

Over geological time the positions of continents, the climate, the composition of the atmosphere and sea level have all changed greatly. This file covers the patterns of change, the Wilson cycle that links them, how they are read from rocks, fossils and isotopes, and how present-day change compares. Quaternary climate (Eduqas option T3) is in [Eduqas 03](../geology-eduqas-a480qs/03-quaternary-geology.md); British examples in [Eduqas 04](../geology-eduqas-a480qs/04-geological-evolution-of-britain.md).

### Long-term changes (OCR 7.1.1(a); Eduqas G3.2a, G3.2b)

#### What you need to know

- Changes in the distribution of continents from the Neoproterozoic: breakup of Pannotia; assembly and breakup of Pangaea.
- Long-term changes in climate and atmospheric composition: icehouse-greenhouse cycles; the role of volcanic activity.
- Long-term changes in global sea level.
- The Wilson cycle as a framework, and links to mass extinctions.
- Eduqas: natural changes in greenhouse gases (CO₂ and CH₄) from volcanic activity, rock weathering and warming of methane hydrates.

#### Explained

**Continents**: a supercontinent (Pannotia, about 600 million years ago) broke up in the late Neoproterozoic and early Cambrian. The fragments drifted and collided again (closing oceans such as Iapetus) to form **Pangaea** by about 300 million years ago (late Palaeozoic). Pangaea broke up from the Jurassic onward, opening the Atlantic.

**Icehouse and greenhouse**: the Earth alternates between icehouse states (polar ice caps, low sea level: late Ordovician, Permo-Carboniferous, the last 34 million years) and greenhouse states (no permanent ice, high CO₂, high sea level: most of the Mesozoic, especially the Cretaceous).

**Natural controls on CO₂ and CH₄**:

- Volcanic activity releases CO₂; large igneous provinces release huge amounts quickly (warming), while sulfate aerosols from big eruptions cool for a few years.
- Chemical weathering of silicate rocks removes CO₂ (carbonic acid reacts with silicates; carbon ends up in carbonate rocks). Uplift of mountain belts (Himalaya) speeds weathering and may cool the climate.
- Burial of organic carbon (coal, black shales) removes CO₂.
- Warming of the oceans can destabilise methane hydrates on continental slopes, releasing CH₄ (a strong greenhouse gas) as a positive feedback.

**Sea level**: global (eustatic) sea level changes with:

- the volume of ice on land (glacio-eustatic: up to about 120 m change in ice ages)
- the volume of the ocean basins: fast sea-floor spreading makes broad, hot, shallow ridges that displace water, so sea level is high (as in the Cretaceous); supercontinent assembly leaves old, deep oceans, so sea level is low.

**The Wilson cycle** (J. Tuzo Wilson): the opening and closing of ocean basins:

1. Continental rifting (East African Rift).
2. Young narrow ocean (Red Sea).
3. Mature ocean with passive margins (Atlantic).
4. Subduction begins; ocean shrinks (Pacific).
5. Closing ocean (Mediterranean).
6. Continental collision and mountain building (Himalaya), forming a supercontinent.

Then the cycle repeats over a few hundred million years. It links climate and sea level (supercontinent times: low sea level, continental interiors arid; breakup: high sea level, more volcanic CO₂), and habitats: assembly reduces shallow shelf seas and mixes faunas, which may contribute to extinctions; breakup isolates populations and increases diversity.

### Evidence in the rock record (OCR 7.1.1(b); Eduqas G3.3a, G3.3b, G3.3c, G3.3d)

#### What you need to know

- Palaeontological evidence (corals, plants); lithological evidence (coal, desert sandstones, evaporites, tillites, reef limestones).
- Oxygen isotopes (¹⁸O and ¹⁶O) and carbon isotopes (¹³C and ¹²C).
- Eustatic sea level change and Vail sea level curves.
- Eduqas: icehouse deposits (for example the Carboniferous) and tropical greenhouse deposits (for example the Cretaceous); oxygen isotopes in fossil shells as a guide to ocean temperature; analyse present oceanic and atmospheric circulation and its climatic effects.

#### Explained

**Climate indicators**:

| Evidence | Climate shown |
| --- | --- |
| Reef corals (warm, shallow, clear sea) and reef limestones | Tropical (within about 30° of the equator) |
| Coal (from swamp forests) | Wet, warm to temperate, high plant productivity |
| Land plants: leaf shape (large leaves with smooth edges and drip tips in warm wet climates; serrated edges in cool climates), absence of growth rings (tropics) | Temperature and rainfall |
| Desert sandstones (red, aeolian) and evaporites | Hot and arid (about 15 to 30° latitude) |
| Tillites (lithified glacial till), dropstones in marine muds, striated pavements | Glacial |

Plotting these on palaeogeographic maps shows climate belts that shift as continents move, and global changes when such deposits appear at unusual latitudes.

**Oxygen isotopes**: water containing the lighter ¹⁶O evaporates more easily. In icehouse times, ice sheets store ¹⁶O-rich water, so seawater becomes enriched in ¹⁸O. Also, shells formed in colder water take up relatively more ¹⁸O. So a higher ¹⁸O/¹⁶O ratio (δ¹⁸O) in fossil marine shells (foraminifera) indicates colder water and/or more ice. In ice cores, the opposite: lower δ¹⁸O in the ice means colder air.

**Carbon isotopes**: photosynthesis takes up ¹²C preferentially, so organic matter is rich in ¹²C. When large amounts of organic carbon are buried, seawater and carbonates become enriched in ¹³C (higher δ¹³C). A sudden drop in δ¹³C in carbonates shows a release of ¹²C-rich carbon (from methane hydrates, burning organic matter, or volcanic release through coal), often at extinction events.

**Sea-level curves**: seismic reflection profiles of continental margins show sequences of sediment that onlap (step landward) during rises and offlap during falls. Vail and colleagues used these to construct global sea-level curves through time. They show long-term highs (Cretaceous) and lows, with many shorter cycles. Limitation: local subsidence and uplift can be mistaken for global changes.

**Present circulation**: ocean currents and atmospheric cells move heat from the equator to the poles; the positions of continents and gateways (open or closed seaways) control this. Studying modern circulation helps interpret past climate patterns ([Eduqas 03](../geology-eduqas-a480qs/03-quaternary-geology.md)).

### Snowball Earth (Eduqas G3.3e)

#### Explained

The Snowball Earth hypothesis proposes that in the Neoproterozoic (around 720 to 635 million years ago) ice covered all or nearly all of the Earth's surface at least once.

Evidence:

- glacial deposits (diamictites, dropstones) on every continent, including some with palaeomagnetic evidence of low (tropical) latitudes at the time
- "cap carbonates": thick limestones directly on top of glacial deposits, suggesting a sudden switch to hot conditions
- carbon isotope swings showing collapse of biological productivity
- the reappearance of banded iron formations, suggesting oceans sealed from the atmosphere by ice became oxygen-poor.

Mechanism: once ice reached low latitudes, high albedo produced runaway cooling. Volcanic CO₂ then built up over millions of years (no weathering or photosynthesis to remove it) until a strong greenhouse melted the ice rapidly.

Evaluation: some argue for a "slushball" with open tropical water, because life survived; palaeolatitude data and dating are debated.

### Present-day change and the Anthropocene (OCR 7.1.1(c); Eduqas G3.2c, G3.2d)

#### What you need to know

- Current rates and scale of environmental and biological change, compared with past geological change, using geochronological principles; evidence for an Anthropocene epoch.
- Eduqas: rates of change differ from those in the past; the Anthropocene is a proposed epoch with no consensus; evaluate the debate.

#### Explained

Geological boundaries are defined by changes that can be traced worldwide in rocks (for example a global marker layer, the "golden spike"). Proposed markers for the Anthropocene: plutonium and other radionuclides from nuclear weapons tests (from the 1950s), plastics, fly ash particles, a sharp shift in carbon isotopes and CO₂ in ice, nitrogen isotopes from fertiliser, and biological changes (spread of domestic chickens, species extinctions and invasions).

**Rates**: atmospheric CO₂ has risen faster in the past 150 years than in any known natural change in the ice core record; current extinction rates are far higher than background rates. Past changes of similar size (for example the Palaeocene-Eocene Thermal Maximum, end-Permian) took thousands of years or more.

**Debate**: for: global, synchronous, preservable markers; changes comparable to those defining other boundaries. Against: too short a time to judge permanence; human influence began gradually much earlier (farming, deforestation); possibly better treated as an informal "event". In 2024 the international stratigraphy body voted not to formally adopt an Anthropocene epoch, so it remains informal, and the debate continues.

#### Worked example

A δ¹⁸O record from foraminifera rises by 1.5‰ over an interval. If about 1‰ corresponds to roughly 4 °C of cooling (ignoring ice volume), the change suggests cooling of about 6 °C. If part of the change is due to ice growth, the temperature change is smaller. This shows why oxygen isotope data must be interpreted with care.

#### Common mistakes

- Reversing the meaning of δ¹⁸O in shells (higher in shells means colder/more ice).
- Saying sea level has only changed because of ice.
- Treating the Anthropocene as an officially adopted epoch.

### Check questions

1. Describe the stages of the Wilson cycle with present-day examples. (5)
2. Explain how fast sea-floor spreading can raise global sea level. (3)
3. Explain how chemical weathering of silicate rocks affects atmospheric CO₂ over long timescales. (3)
4. Name three lithological indicators of past climate and state the climate each shows. (3)
5. Explain why foraminifera shells from glacial periods have a higher ¹⁸O/¹⁶O ratio. (3)
6. A carbonate sequence shows a sudden fall in δ¹³C at a mass extinction horizon. Suggest an explanation. (3)
7. Evaluate the evidence for the Snowball Earth hypothesis. (6)
8. Assess the case for recognising an Anthropocene epoch. (5)

### Mark scheme

1. Rifting, e.g. East Africa (1); young ocean, e.g. Red Sea (1); mature ocean with passive margins, e.g. Atlantic (1); subduction / shrinking ocean, e.g. Pacific / Mediterranean (1); collision and mountain building, e.g. Himalaya (1).
2. Fast spreading produces large volumes of young hot lithosphere (1); ridges broad and shallow (1); displace seawater onto continents (1).
3. CO₂ dissolves in rain forming carbonic acid (1); reacts with silicate minerals (1); carbon transported to the sea and locked in carbonate rocks, lowering CO₂ (1).
4. Any three with climate: coal (warm, wet); desert sandstone (hot arid); evaporites (hot arid); tillite (glacial); reef limestone (tropical marine).
5. ¹⁶O evaporates more readily (1); during glacials ¹⁶O stored in ice sheets so seawater enriched in ¹⁸O (1); colder water also increases ¹⁸O taken into shells (1).
6. Release of large amounts of ¹²C-rich carbon (1); e.g. methane hydrates / volcanic heating of coal / organic matter (1); or collapse of productivity so less ¹²C removed by photosynthesis (1).
7. Level-marked: glacial deposits at low palaeolatitudes on all continents (1); cap carbonates (1); BIF return (1); carbon isotope excursions (1); counter: life survived / slushball / dating and palaeomagnetic uncertainty (1); judgement (1).
8. Up to 5: global markers (radionuclides, plastics, isotopes) (1); rates of change exceed past (1); extinctions comparable to past boundaries (1); against: short time span / diachronous start / may be an event not epoch (1); formal vote rejected so remains informal (1); judgement (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/23-evolution-and-applied-palaeontology.md -->

## Evolution and applied palaeontology

The fossil record shows life becoming more diverse and complex over time, and shows how body plans were adapted to different ways of life. This file covers the main stages in the history of life and the adaptations of the key groups. Fossil identification basics are in [05](05-fossils-and-the-fossil-record.md); extinctions in [24](24-mass-extinctions.md); exceptional preservation in [25](25-lagerstatten.md).

### The history of life (Eduqas G3.1a, G3.1d)

#### What you need to know

- Precambrian life (possibly from 3.8 billion years ago); the Ediacaran fauna (about 565 Ma), the oldest diverse multicellular soft-bodied organisms.
- The Cambrian explosion: mineralised skeletons and a wide variety of marine invertebrates by the early Cambrian; possible causes.
- Diversification in stages: Cambrian, Palaeozoic and modern faunas; Sepkoski's diversity curves.
- Migration onto land in the Palaeozoic: plants; vertebrates from fish to amphibians, amphibians to reptiles, reptiles to mammals and birds. Morphology of fossil vertebrates (including dinosaurs) to interpret mode of life.
- Gradualism versus punctuated equilibrium.

#### Explained

- **Early life**: chemical and isotopic traces of life may date back about 3.8 billion years; stromatolites (layered mats built by cyanobacteria) are known from about 3.5 billion years. For most of Earth history, life was single-celled.
- **Ediacaran fauna** (late Neoproterozoic): soft-bodied organisms, some frond-like (for example *Charnia*, found in Charnwood Forest, England), some disc or quilted forms; their relationships to later animals are debated.
- **Cambrian explosion** (about 540 to 520 Ma): most major animal groups (phyla) appear rapidly in the fossil record, many with hard parts (shells, skeletons). Possible causes: rising oxygen allowing larger, more active animals and skeletons; predation driving the evolution of armour; evolution of genes controlling body plans; increased nutrients and calcium in seawater after the Snowball glaciations; the evolution of eyes. Hard parts also made fossilisation more likely, exaggerating the "explosion".
- **Three evolutionary faunas** (Sepkoski's analysis of marine families): the Cambrian fauna (trilobites, early brachiopods) dominated the Cambrian; the Palaeozoic fauna (articulate brachiopods, crinoids, rugose and tabulate corals, cephalopods) from the Ordovician to the Permian; the modern fauna (bivalves, gastropods, fish, echinoids) dominant since the Permian extinction. Diversity curves show a rise in the Ordovician, a long plateau through the Palaeozoic, then steady growth through the Mesozoic and Cenozoic, interrupted by mass extinctions.
- **Life on land**: plants colonised land in the Ordovician to Silurian; vascular plants spread in the Devonian; large forests and coal swamps in the Carboniferous. Arthropods followed plants. Vertebrates: fish → amphibians (Devonian) → reptiles (Carboniferous, with the amniotic egg) → mammals (Triassic) and birds (Jurassic, from theropod dinosaurs).

**Gradualism versus punctuated equilibrium**:

- Gradualism: species change slowly and steadily through time.
- Punctuated equilibrium: species remain largely unchanged (stasis) for long periods, then change rapidly during speciation, often in small isolated populations, so transitional forms are rarely preserved.
- The fossil record shows both patterns; gaps in the record make it hard to tell them apart.

### Marine invertebrates (OCR 7.1.2(a))

#### What you need to know

- Trilobite adaptations to benthonic epifaunal, benthonic infaunal, nektonic and planktonic niches in the Palaeozoic.
- Ecology of modern reef-building (scleractinian) corals, including symbiotic algae, to interpret fossil tabulate and rugose corals as indicators of reefs.
- Brachiopod adaptations to high- and low-energy environments: cemented, free-lying, pedicle-attached and reef-building forms.
- Morphological similarities and differences between brachiopods and bivalves.

#### Explained

**Trilobites**:

| Niche | Adaptations |
| --- | --- |
| Benthonic epifaunal (on the sea floor) | Flattened; broad genal spines spreading weight on soft mud; eyes on top of the head; moderate size |
| Benthonic infaunal (burrowing) | Smooth, streamlined, often reduced or no eyes; spade-like cephalon for ploughing |
| Nektonic (active swimmers) | Streamlined, large eyes giving wide field of view, light build |
| Planktonic (drifting) | Small, light, spiny for buoyancy, or with very large bulging eyes (all-round vision in the water column) |

Enrolment (rolling up like a woodlouse) for protection; trilobite eyes were made of calcite lenses.

**Corals**: modern scleractinian reef corals contain symbiotic algae (zooxanthellae) that photosynthesise and provide food; so reefs need light (shallow, clear water), warmth (about 20 to 29 °C), normal salinity and low sediment. By uniformitarianism, fossil reefs built by corals (tabulate and rugose corals in the Palaeozoic; they are not related closely to scleractinians and whether they had symbionts is uncertain) are taken as indicators of warm, shallow, clear tropical seas. Colonial corals in growth position with reef framework suggest reefs; solitary rugose corals can live in deeper or muddier water.

**Brachiopods**:

- High-energy (shallow, rough water): thick, ribbed shells; attached firmly by a strong pedicle to rock, or cemented by one valve; strophic (straight) hinges in some.
- Low-energy, soft muddy floors: thin shells, broad flattened shapes or spines that spread weight; free-lying (pedicle lost in adults), some with one convex valve resting in mud.
- Reef builders: some Permian forms (richthofenids) cemented together and resembled corals.

**Brachiopods versus bivalves**: both have two valves and filter-feed, an example of **convergent evolution** (similar form from different ancestors). Differences: symmetry (brachiopods: plane of symmetry through each valve; bivalves: between the valves), muscles, the brachiopod pedicle and lophophore (feeding organ supported by an internal brachidium) versus bivalve gills and siphons; brachiopods only marine. Brachiopods dominated Palaeozoic shelves; bivalves dominated after the Permian extinction.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG5**: identify and draw fossil trilobites, corals, brachiopods and bivalves; interpret mode of life from morphology.

### Vertebrates on land (OCR 7.1.2(b); Eduqas G3.1a)

#### What you need to know

- How amphibians evolved from marine (lobe-finned) fish in the Devonian and adapted to land in the Carboniferous: skull morphology, fin bones, limb bones, teeth, body shape, tail fin, scales.
- The amniotic egg and its advantage for life on land.
- Dinosaur adaptations: saurischian (sauropod herbivores and theropod carnivores) and ornithischian dinosaurs.
- Birds evolved from theropods; function of feathers; convergent evolution between birds and pterosaurs.

#### Explained

**Fish to amphibians**: lobe-finned fish had fleshy fins with an internal bone pattern (one upper bone, two lower bones) like the limbs of tetrapods; lungs; and labyrinthodont (folded-enamel) teeth like early amphibians. Transitional forms (for example *Tiktaalik*, with a flattened head, a neck and sturdy fins; and early tetrapods like *Acanthostega* with limbs bearing digits but still with gills and a tail fin) show stepwise change in the Devonian, perhaps in shallow, oxygen-poor streams and swamps. By the Carboniferous, amphibians had stronger limbs and girdles to support the body out of water, but still needed water to breed.

**Amniotic egg**: a shelled egg with membranes (amnion, chorion, allantois) and a yolk, enclosing the embryo in its own fluid, allowing gas exchange through the shell and storing waste. It frees reproduction from water, so reptiles (and later birds and mammals) could colonise dry land.

**Dinosaurs**: classified by hip structure.

- **Saurischians** ("lizard-hipped", pubis pointing forward):
  - Sauropods: huge quadrupedal herbivores with long necks (reaching high vegetation), small heads, peg-like teeth (stripping leaves; swallowed stones to grind food), pillar-like legs, air sacs in bones to reduce weight.
  - Theropods: bipedal carnivores with sharp serrated teeth, clawed hands, hollow bones, balancing tail; includes the ancestors of birds.
- **Ornithischians** ("bird-hipped", pubis pointing backward): all herbivores, with a beak at the front of the jaw and grinding teeth (ornithopods such as *Iguanodon*, armoured ankylosaurs, plated stegosaurs, horned ceratopsians).

Mode of life from morphology: teeth show diet; limb proportions and trackways show posture and speed; armour and horns show defence.

**Birds and pterosaurs**: birds evolved from small feathered theropods (shared features: hollow bones, wishbone, three main fingers, feathers). Feathers probably first evolved for insulation and display, later for flight. *Archaeopteryx* (Jurassic) has a mixture of bird and reptile features ([25](25-lagerstatten.md)). Pterosaurs were flying reptiles (not dinosaurs) with wings of skin stretched on a very long fourth finger. Birds' wings are feathers on the arm and fused hand. Both evolved flight independently: convergent evolution (lightweight hollow bones, keeled breastbone, large brain and eyes).

#### Practical

**OCR PAG12**: research and compare evidence for the origin of birds, citing sources.

#### Common mistakes

- Calling pterosaurs or marine reptiles dinosaurs.
- Saying birds evolved from ornithischian ("bird-hipped") dinosaurs. They evolved from saurischian theropods.
- Saying brachiopods are a type of bivalve.

### Check questions

1. Suggest three possible causes of the Cambrian explosion. (3)
2. Describe Sepkoski's three evolutionary faunas. (3)
3. Distinguish between gradualism and punctuated equilibrium. (2)
4. A trilobite is flattened, with long genal spines and eyes on top of its head. Deduce its mode of life. (3)
5. Explain why fossil coral reefs are used as indicators of tropical seas. (3)
6. Compare brachiopods adapted to high-energy and low-energy environments. (4)
7. Describe the evidence that amphibians evolved from lobe-finned fish. (4)
8. Explain the evolutionary importance of the amniotic egg. (3)
9. Explain why the wings of birds and pterosaurs are an example of convergent evolution. (3)

### Mark scheme

1. Any three: rising oxygen; predation / arms race; evolution of hard parts / skeletons; body-plan genes; more nutrients / calcium after glaciation; evolution of eyes.
2. Cambrian fauna: trilobites, early brachiopods (1); Palaeozoic fauna: articulate brachiopods, crinoids, corals, cephalopods (1); modern fauna: bivalves, gastropods, echinoids, fish (1).
3. Gradualism: slow continuous change (1); punctuated: long stasis with short bursts of rapid change at speciation (1).
4. Benthonic / bottom-dwelling on soft mud (1); spines spread weight so it did not sink (1); eyes on top to see above while resting on the floor (1).
5. Modern reef corals have symbiotic algae (1); need light, warmth, clear shallow water (1); uniformitarianism: assume fossil reefs formed in similar conditions (1).
6. High-energy: thick shells, ribbed (1), strong pedicle or cemented (1); low-energy: thin shells, spines / flattened shape to spread weight (1), free-lying (1).
7. Fin bones in the same pattern as limbs (one, two, many) (1); similar skull bones and labyrinthodont teeth (1); lungs (1); transitional fossils with features of both, e.g. tail fin and gills with limbs (1).
8. Shell and membranes protect embryo and keep it moist (1); yolk food supply (1); no need for water to reproduce so animals could live on dry land (1).
9. Both evolved flight independently from different ancestors (1); bird wings: feathers on arm; pterosaur wings: skin on elongated fourth finger (1); similar function / features from similar selection pressures (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/24-mass-extinctions.md -->

## Mass extinctions

Most of the time life changes gradually, but the fossil record shows several short intervals when a large proportion of species died out worldwide. These mass extinctions mark major boundaries in the geological timescale and reset the course of evolution.

### What a mass extinction is (Eduqas F3.2f, G3.1b)

#### What you need to know

- The rock record shows long periods of slow change interrupted by sudden catastrophes causing mass extinctions.
- Possible causes: asteroid impact (Alvarez hypothesis), large-scale volcanism (flood basalts), changes in land and sea level, rapid climate change. Evaluate contrasting hypotheses.

#### Explained

A mass extinction is the loss of a large proportion (often over half) of species across many groups and environments, worldwide, in a geologically short time. Background extinction continues all the time at a low rate. Five major mass extinctions are usually recognised: end-Ordovician, late Devonian, end-Permian, end-Triassic and end-Cretaceous.

Kill mechanisms often combine: rapid warming or cooling, ocean acidification (CO₂ dissolving), anoxia (low oxygen) in the oceans, collapse of food chains when photosynthesis stops, and loss of habitat when sea level falls.

### The three named extinctions (OCR 7.1.3(a); Eduqas G3.1c)

#### End-Ordovician (Ordovician-Silurian boundary, about 444 Ma)

- **Effect**: second largest; about 85% of marine species lost (trilobites, brachiopods, graptolites, corals hit).
- **Cause**: a short, intense glaciation as Gondwana sat over the South Pole. Ice sheets grew, sea level fell sharply, draining the shallow shelf seas where most life lived (habitat elimination); oceans cooled. A second pulse came as the ice melted and sea level rose, with low-oxygen water spreading.
- **Evidence**: tillites and glacial striations in North Africa (then near the pole); a positive oxygen isotope shift (colder, more ice); a sea level fall recorded in shelf sequences; extinction concentrated in warm-water tropical faunas.

#### End-Permian (Permian-Triassic boundary, about 252 Ma)

- **Effect**: the largest; over 90% of marine species and about 70% of land vertebrate species lost; trilobites, rugose and tabulate corals, and most brachiopods vanished.
- **Cause**: the Siberian Traps, a huge flood basalt province, erupted millions of cubic kilometres of lava. Magma intruded and baked coal and evaporite deposits, releasing extra CO₂, methane and halogens. Effects: rapid global warming, ocean acidification, ocean anoxia (black shales), possibly ozone damage.
- **Evidence**: radiometric dating of the Siberian Traps matching the extinction; a sharp negative carbon isotope shift (input of ¹²C-rich carbon); black shales and pyrite showing anoxia; oxygen isotopes indicating very high sea temperatures; "coal gap" (no coal for millions of years after, as forests died); fungal spike in some sections.

#### End-Cretaceous (Cretaceous-Palaeogene, also called K-T or K-Pg, about 66 Ma)

- **Effect**: about 75% of species lost, including non-bird dinosaurs, ammonites, belemnites, marine reptiles and many plankton.
- **Causes**:
  - An asteroid about 10 km across struck Chicxulub, Mexico (Alvarez hypothesis). Dust and soot blocked sunlight (impact winter, photosynthesis collapsed); sulfur from evaporites in the target rocks produced acid rain and cooling; wildfires; tsunamis.
  - The Deccan Traps (India) flood basalts erupted over the same interval, releasing CO₂ and SO₂ and stressing ecosystems.
- **Evidence for impact**: a thin clay layer worldwide at the boundary rich in iridium (rare in the crust but common in meteorites); shocked quartz (with lamellae formed only by extreme pressures); glassy spherules (tektites) from melted rock; soot; the buried Chicxulub crater (about 180 km wide), dated to the boundary; tsunami deposits around the Gulf of Mexico.
- **Evidence for volcanism**: dating of Deccan lavas shows major eruptions shortly before and after the boundary; some groups were declining before the impact.
- **Evaluation**: the impact timing and global layer are strongly supported; volcanism may have weakened ecosystems or prolonged recovery. Most geologists consider the impact the main trigger.

### Replacement of dominant groups (OCR 7.1.3(b))

#### What you need to know

How mass extinctions led to new groups replacing the dominant forms in major habitats.

#### Explained

Extinctions remove dominant groups, leaving empty niches. Survivors diversify rapidly (adaptive radiation) to fill them.

| Extinction | Groups lost or reduced | Groups that took over |
| --- | --- | --- |
| End-Permian | Brachiopods, rugose and tabulate corals, trilobites, many synapsids | Bivalves on the sea floor; scleractinian corals as reef builders (Triassic); archosaurs and then dinosaurs on land |
| End-Triassic | Many large amphibians and other reptiles | Dinosaurs became dominant on land in the Jurassic |
| End-Cretaceous | Non-bird dinosaurs, ammonites, marine reptiles | Mammals radiated into large-bodied herbivores and carnivores; birds diversified; teleost fish in the seas |

Survivors are often small, generalist, widely distributed species able to tolerate harsh conditions, not necessarily the "best adapted" before the event.

#### Worked example

A section shows 120 genera below a boundary and 45 above, of which 15 are new. Genera surviving = 45 − 15 = 30. Percentage of genera lost = (120 − 30) ÷ 120 × 100 = 75%.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying all dinosaurs died out at the end of the Cretaceous. Birds are surviving dinosaurs.
- Saying the iridium layer proves an impact happened in Mexico. The crater does; the layer shows an impact somewhere.
- Treating gradual decline before a boundary as evidence against any catastrophe; both can operate.

### Check questions

1. Define a mass extinction. (2)
2. Explain how glaciation caused the end-Ordovician extinction. (3)
3. Describe the evidence linking the Siberian Traps to the end-Permian extinction. (4)
4. Explain why a negative carbon isotope excursion is found at the Permian-Triassic boundary. (2)
5. Describe four pieces of evidence for an asteroid impact at the end of the Cretaceous. (4)
6. Evaluate the relative importance of impact and volcanism in the end-Cretaceous extinction. (6)
7. Explain how mammals came to dominate land after the end-Cretaceous extinction. (3)
8. A boundary section has 80 species below, 32 above, of which 12 are new. Calculate the percentage of species lost. (2)

### Mark scheme

1. Loss of a large proportion of species across many groups worldwide (1); in a geologically short time (1).
2. Gondwana over the South Pole, ice sheets grew (1); sea level fell, draining shallow shelf seas / habitat lost (1); oceans cooled, tropical faunas died (1).
3. Dating of Traps matches extinction (1); huge volumes of lava / gas (1); intrusion into coal / evaporites released CO₂ / methane (1); carbon isotope shift / warming / anoxia evidence at the boundary (1).
4. Release of ¹²C-rich carbon (methane, heated coal, organic carbon) into ocean and atmosphere (1); collapse of productivity so less ¹²C removed (1).
5. Iridium-rich clay; shocked quartz; spherules / tektites; Chicxulub crater dated to the boundary; tsunami deposits; soot (any four).
6. Level-marked: impact evidence strong and timing exact (1); global effects explain sudden loss (1); Deccan eruptions before and after (1); some groups declining earlier (1); combined stress model (1); judgement (1).
7. Dinosaurs removed, leaving empty niches (1); surviving small mammals adaptive radiation (1); evolved larger sizes and diverse diets to fill herbivore / carnivore roles (1).
8. Survivors = 20 (1); (80 − 20) ÷ 80 × 100 = 75% (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/25-lagerstatten.md -->

## Lagerstätten

A Lagerstätte (plural Lagerstätten) is a deposit with exceptionally complete preservation of fossils, often including soft tissues that normally decay. Because the usual fossil record is so biased towards hard parts ([05](05-fossils-and-the-fossil-record.md)), Lagerstätten give rare views of whole ecosystems. OCR studies two Cambrian examples and one Jurassic one.

### Conditions for exceptional preservation

#### What you need to know

Key rock types and sedimentary structures that show rapid burial; how conditions and early diagenesis control the level of detail preserved.

#### Explained

Soft tissues survive only if decay, scavenging and burrowing are stopped quickly. Typical conditions:

- **Rapid burial** by fine sediment (mudflows, storm deposits, turbidity currents), which also prevents scavenging.
- **Anoxic** or otherwise hostile bottom water (no oxygen, high salinity), so no scavengers or burrowers and slow bacterial decay.
- **Fine grain size** (clay, lime mud) that records fine detail.
- **Early mineralisation**: soft tissues replaced or coated by minerals (clay films, pyrite, phosphate, carbon films) before they decay.

Evidence in the rocks: fine laminations undisturbed by burrows (no bioturbation); graded beds or event layers showing sudden deposition; dark colour and pyrite showing anoxia; articulated (joined-up) skeletons.

### Burgess Shale and Chengjiang (OCR 7.2.1(a))

#### What you need to know

- Geological settings and sedimentary conditions of the Middle Cambrian Burgess Shale (Canada) and the Lower Cambrian Chengjiang Formation (China).
- How hard and soft tissues were preserved.
- How their fossil assemblages provide evidence for the Cambrian explosion and their depositional palaeoenvironments.

#### Explained

**Burgess Shale** (British Columbia, about 508 Ma):

- **Setting**: fine mudstones deposited in deep water at the foot of a steep submarine cliff at the edge of a carbonate reef platform.
- **Preservation**: animals living near the base of the cliff were swept down by mudflows (slurries of fine sediment) into deeper, oxygen-poor water and buried instantly. Soft tissues were preserved as thin carbon films with clay minerals coating them, forming silvery films on the bedding planes. Later the rocks were mildly metamorphosed during mountain building but detail survived.
- **Fossils**: arthropods dominate (trilobites, but mostly soft-bodied arthropods such as *Marrella*), sponges, worms, early chordates (*Pikaia*), and strange forms such as *Opabinia* (five eyes, a frontal grasping nozzle), *Anomalocaris* (a large predator with grasping appendages), *Hallucigenia* (a spiny lobopodian).
- **Significance**: most animals in the community were soft-bodied, so the normal fossil record misses the majority of Cambrian life. It shows a complex food web with predators already present, supporting the rapid diversification of body plans.

**Chengjiang Formation** (Yunnan, China, about 518 Ma):

- **Setting**: shallow marine shelf with fine muds, below normal wave base but affected by storms.
- **Preservation**: event layers from storm-driven or flood-driven mud flows buried organisms rapidly; soft parts preserved as films, often with iron oxides that replaced original pyrite.
- **Fossils**: arthropods, sponges, worms, the earliest known chordates and fish-like vertebrates (*Haikouichthys*, *Myllokunmingia*), lobopodians, and comb jellies.
- **Significance**: it is about 10 million years older than the Burgess Shale, showing that diverse animal body plans, including early vertebrates, were already present early in the Cambrian, strengthening the case for a rapid "explosion".

**Interpreting palaeoenvironments**: the assemblages include filter-feeders (sponges), burrowers, swimmers and predators, showing a normal marine environment with oxygenated water where the animals lived; the burial site was anoxic. Lack of bioturbation in the event beds and fine laminae show quiet bottom conditions after burial.

### Solnhofen Limestone (OCR 7.2.1(b))

#### What you need to know

- Setting and conditions of the Jurassic Solnhofen Limestone (Germany): changes in salinity in a shallow lagoon.
- Preservation of feathers and of hard and soft tissues in a range of organisms.
- Evidence for the evolution of *Archaeopteryx*: similarities to both dinosaurs and birds.

#### Explained

- **Setting** (Late Jurassic, about 150 Ma): a group of shallow lagoons behind sponge and coral reefs, in a hot, arid climate near the Tethys Ocean.
- **Conditions**: restricted circulation and high evaporation made the bottom water hypersaline and anoxic, so few organisms could live on the floor, there were no scavengers or burrowers, and decay was slow. Animals that were washed in from the open sea, or fell in from the air or nearby land, died and sank. Storms periodically brought fine lime mud that buried them.
- **Rock**: very fine, evenly laminated limestone (lithographic limestone, once used for printing) that splits along bedding planes.
- **Preservation**: complete skeletons, impressions of soft tissues (jellyfish, wing membranes of pterosaurs), and even feathers and the death tracks of horseshoe crabs that crawled briefly before dying.
- **Fossils**: fish, ammonites, crustaceans, horseshoe crabs, insects, pterosaurs, the small theropod dinosaur *Compsognathus*, and *Archaeopteryx*.

**Archaeopteryx**:

| Bird-like features | Dinosaur (reptile) features |
| --- | --- |
| Feathers, including asymmetric flight feathers | Teeth in jaws |
| Wings | Long bony tail |
| Wishbone (furcula) | Clawed fingers on wings |
| | Unfused hand bones, no large keeled breastbone, theropod-like skull and hips |

It is a transitional fossil supporting the evolution of birds from small theropod dinosaurs ([23](23-evolution-and-applied-palaeontology.md)). Later discoveries of feathered dinosaurs in China (in other Lagerstätten) strengthened the link.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG5**: study images and replicas of Lagerstätten fossils; draw and label *Archaeopteryx* features.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying Lagerstätten form where life was abundant. They form where conditions stop decay; organisms often lived elsewhere and were transported.
- Saying *Archaeopteryx* proves birds came from pterosaurs.

### Check questions

1. Define the term Lagerstätte. (1)
2. Describe three conditions that favour preservation of soft tissue. (3)
3. Explain how organisms in the Burgess Shale were preserved. (4)
4. Explain why the Burgess Shale changed ideas about Cambrian life. (3)
5. Explain the importance of the Chengjiang Formation for understanding the Cambrian explosion. (2)
6. Explain how conditions in the Solnhofen lagoons allowed exceptional preservation. (4)
7. Describe how *Archaeopteryx* shows features of both dinosaurs and birds. (4)

### Mark scheme

1. A deposit with exceptional preservation of fossils, including soft tissues / complete organisms (1).
2. Any three: rapid burial; anoxic / hypersaline bottom water; no scavengers / burrowers; fine sediment; early mineralisation.
3. Animals living near the base of a submarine cliff (1); swept away by mudflows (1); buried rapidly in deep anoxic water (1); soft tissues preserved as carbon films coated with clay minerals (1).
4. Most organisms soft-bodied (1); so the normal record misses most species (1); shows diverse body plans / complex food webs including predators (1).
5. About 10 million years older than Burgess (1); shows diverse animals including early vertebrates already present early in the Cambrian (1).
6. Restricted lagoon with high evaporation (1); hypersaline, anoxic bottom water (1); no scavengers or burrowers / slow decay (1); fine lime mud from storms buried organisms (1).
7. Bird: feathers / wings / wishbone (any two, 2); dinosaur: teeth / long bony tail / clawed fingers (any two, 2).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/26-oil-and-gas-basins.md -->

## Oil and gas basins

An oil or gas field needs a petroleum system: a source rock, burial to the right temperature, a migration route, a porous and permeable reservoir, a seal (cap rock) and a trap, in the right order of time. The North Sea Basin is the standard British example. Porosity, permeability and Darcy's law are in [14](14-fluids-in-rocks-and-hydrogeology.md); coal for Eduqas in [Eduqas 01](../geology-eduqas-a480qs/01-economic-minerals-coal-and-bulk-materials.md).

### Source rocks and maturation (OCR 7.2.2(a); Eduqas G4.1d)

#### What you need to know

- Settings and conditions that led to oil and gas in the North Sea Basin; burial history curves.
