- Palaeoenvironments of source, reservoir and cap rocks.
- Maturation in the source rock: temperature and pressure with burial; oil and gas windows.

#### Explained

**Source rocks** are organic-rich mudstones. Plankton (algae) settle in quiet water where the bottom is anoxic, so the organic matter is not destroyed and is buried. In the North Sea the main oil source is the Upper Jurassic Kimmeridge Clay, deposited in deep, poorly oxygenated rift basins. Gas comes mainly from Carboniferous coals (southern North Sea), formed from land plants.

**Maturation**: with burial, temperature rises (about 25 to 30 °C km⁻¹).

1. Organic matter becomes kerogen (insoluble organic material) during shallow burial.
2. **Oil window**: about 60 to 120 °C (roughly 2 to 4 km): kerogen breaks down (cracks) into liquid oil.
3. **Gas window**: about 120 to 225 °C: oil and kerogen crack to gas (methane). Above that, organic matter is overcooked (graphite).
4. Algal (marine) kerogen produces mainly oil; woody (land plant) kerogen and coal produce mainly gas.

**Burial history curves** plot depth of a layer against time. Combined with the geothermal gradient, they show when the source rock entered the oil window (for the Kimmeridge Clay, mainly in the Cretaceous and Cenozoic). Traps must exist before oil is generated, or the oil escapes.

#### Worked example

A source rock lies at 3200 m. Surface temperature 10 °C, gradient 30 °C km⁻¹. Temperature = 10 + 3.2 × 30 = 106 °C, so it is in the oil window. At 4500 m it would be 145 °C, in the gas window.

**Palaeoenvironments (North Sea)**: source rock (Kimmeridge Clay): deep anoxic marine rifts; reservoirs: Jurassic deltaic and shallow marine sandstones (the Brent Group), Permian desert sandstones (Rotliegend, southern gas fields), Palaeogene turbidite sandstones, Cretaceous chalk (fractured); cap rocks: marine mudstones (Kimmeridge Clay itself, Cretaceous clays) and Permian Zechstein evaporites (salt).

### Migration and traps (OCR 7.2.2(b); Eduqas G4.2c)

#### What you need to know

- Migration from source to reservoir under a cap rock; porosity and permeability of reservoirs; suitable cap rocks; controls on migration (buoyancy).
- Rifting and synsedimentary faulting in the North Sea Basin forming traps.
- Traps: anticline, fault, salt dome, unconformity and lithological (stratigraphic) traps; how oil and gas may be lost from traps.

#### Explained

**Migration**: as the source rock matures, oil and gas are squeezed out (primary migration) into permeable carrier beds and faults. Oil and gas are less dense than water, so they rise buoyantly through water-filled pores (secondary migration) until stopped by an impermeable layer. Gas rises above oil, and oil above water, in the trap.

**Reservoir rock**: porous (to store) and permeable (to flow): well sorted, poorly cemented sandstones; fractured limestones and chalk. **Cap rock (seal)**: impermeable: mudstone, shale, evaporites (salt is ideal because it flows and seals fractures).

**Traps**:

| Trap | How it forms |
| --- | --- |
| Anticline | Fold arch; hydrocarbons rise to the crest under the cap rock |
| Fault trap | A fault places impermeable rock against the reservoir, sealing it |
| Salt dome | Salt rises buoyantly (diapir), arching and faulting overlying beds and sealing them at its flanks |
| Unconformity trap | Tilted reservoir truncated by an erosion surface and sealed by impermeable rock above |
| Lithological (stratigraphic) trap | Reservoir pinches out or changes laterally into impermeable rock (for example a channel sandstone within mudstone, or a reef) |

**North Sea rifting**: in the Jurassic the North Sea area was stretched, forming rift basins (the Viking and Central Grabens) bounded by normal faults. Faulting during deposition (synsedimentary) tilted fault blocks, so Brent sandstones lie in the raised crests of rotated blocks sealed by the Kimmeridge Clay and later Cretaceous mudstones (for example the Brent field). The deep grabens buried the source rock deep enough to mature.

**Loss of oil and gas**: faults or erosion breaching the trap; cap rock fractured; uplift bringing the trap near the surface where oil is degraded by bacteria and water (biodegradation); overheating cracking oil to gas.

### Exploration (OCR 7.2.2(c); Eduqas G4.3a, G4.3b)

#### What you need to know

- Geophysics: seismic reflection and gravity surveys to interpret structure and locate reserves.
- Exploration drilling and downhole logging: porosity, gamma ray and resistivity logs; primary and secondary recovery; planning field development.
- Microfossils in biostratigraphy and palaeoenvironmental analysis: foraminifera, coccolithophores, palynomorphs (pollen, spores, dinoflagellates).
- Eduqas: seismic reflection sections; cross-sections from borehole data with dating and correlation by microfossils.

#### Explained

**Seismic reflection**: sound pulses (from air guns at sea or vibrator trucks on land) travel into the ground and reflect from boundaries between layers of different acoustic impedance (density × velocity). Recording travel times builds a cross-section image of the layers. Geologists identify anticlines, faults, salt domes and unconformities. Bright spots (strong reflections) can indicate gas. 3D seismic surveys image whole fields.

**Gravity surveys**: negative anomalies over thick low-density sediments (basins) and salt domes; positive over basement highs.

**Drilling and logs**: an exploration well tests a prospect. Downhole tools measure properties along the hole:

| Log | Measures | Use |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Gamma ray | Natural radioactivity (mainly K, Th, U in clays) | High in shales, low in clean sandstones and limestones: identifies lithology and correlates wells |
| Resistivity | Electrical resistance | Salty water conducts (low resistivity); oil and gas do not (high resistivity) in a porous bed |
| Porosity (neutron, density, sonic) | Pore space | Identifies good reservoir; neutron-density crossover flags gas |

Cores and cuttings are examined for lithology and fossils.

**Recovery**: primary recovery uses the natural pressure in the reservoir; secondary recovery injects water or gas to maintain pressure and sweep oil to producing wells; enhanced methods follow (see [Environmental Science 13](../environmental-science-aqa-7447/13-securing-future-energy-supplies.md)). Field development planning uses all data to place production and injection wells.

**Microfossils**: in drill cuttings (small chips), large fossils are destroyed but microfossils survive in large numbers. Foraminifera (marine single-celled organisms with chambered shells), coccolithophores (calcareous plankton plates), and palynomorphs (organic-walled: pollen and spores from land plants, and dinoflagellate cysts) evolve quickly and are widespread, so they date and correlate rocks between wells (biostratigraphy). They also indicate environment (marine or terrestrial, water depth, salinity), and the colour of palynomorphs shows thermal maturity.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG5 and PAG10**: interpret graphic logs and well logs; correlate boreholes using gamma ray signatures and microfossil zones; locate traps on seismic sections. **OCR PAG8**: model migration with oil and water in sand columns.

### Onshore basins (OCR 7.2.2(d))

#### What you need to know

How the same principles apply to onshore basins, including directional drilling and hydraulic fracturing (fracking).

#### Explained

Onshore Britain has small conventional oil fields (Wytch Farm in Dorset, the largest onshore field in western Europe, in a Triassic sandstone reservoir; East Midlands Carboniferous fields) and shale gas potential in Carboniferous shales (Bowland Shale in northern England). Directional (extended-reach and horizontal) drilling allows wells from one site to reach far beneath sensitive areas (from land under Poole Harbour at Wytch Farm). Fracking opens fractures in tight shale to release gas; it caused small earthquakes in Lancashire in 2011 and 2019, and a moratorium in England followed in 2019. Concerns: induced seismicity, groundwater contamination, water use, traffic, methane leakage and climate impact.

#### Common mistakes

- Saying oil forms from dinosaurs. Mostly from marine plankton.
- Saying oil is stored in underground lakes. It fills pore spaces.
- Forgetting timing: trap must form before migration.

### Check questions

1. Explain why the Kimmeridge Clay is a good source rock. (3)
2. A source rock is at 3800 m. The surface is 8 °C and the gradient 28 °C km⁻¹. Calculate its temperature and state whether it is in the oil or gas window. (2)
3. Explain why oil and gas migrate upwards. (2)
4. Describe the formation of a fault trap and a salt dome trap. (4)
5. Explain how North Sea rifting created conditions for oil accumulation. (4)
6. Explain how gamma ray and resistivity logs can identify an oil-bearing sandstone. (4)
7. Explain why microfossils are used for correlation in oil exploration rather than larger fossils. (3)
8. Evaluate the development of onshore shale gas in Britain. (5)

### Mark scheme

1. Rich in organic matter from plankton (1); deposited in anoxic conditions so organic matter preserved (1); buried deep enough to mature / in the oil window (1).
2. 8 + 3.8 × 28 = 114 °C (1); oil window (1).
3. Less dense than water (1); buoyant, rise through water-filled pores until stopped by a seal (1).
4. Fault: displacement places impermeable rock against reservoir, sealing it (2). Salt dome: rising salt arches and faults beds; reservoir sealed against the impermeable salt flank (2).
5. Extension formed grabens and tilted fault blocks (1); deep grabens buried Kimmeridge Clay into the oil window (1); fault block crests trapped Brent sandstones (1); sealed by overlying mudstones (1).
6. Gamma ray low in clean sandstone (shales high) (1); identifies the reservoir bed (1); resistivity high where pores hold oil (1); low where saline water (1).
7. Drill cuttings small so large fossils broken (1); microfossils abundant in small samples (1); evolve rapidly / widespread, give good zones (1).
8. Up to 5: energy security, lower emissions than coal (1); jobs and tax (1); induced seismicity (1); groundwater / water use / traffic (1); methane leakage and climate goals (1); judgement (1).

<!-- source: notes/a-level/smaller-stem/geology-ocr-h414/27-whole-basin-facies-analysis.md -->

## Whole basin facies analysis

Basin analysis combines sedimentology, palaeontology, structure and dating to reconstruct the history of a sedimentary basin. The graphic log is the main tool. OCR uses two British basins: the Lower Palaeozoic Welsh Basin and the Jurassic rocks of the UK. Facies and environments are in [12](12-uniformitarianism-facies-and-sedimentary-environments.md); turbidites in [13](13-turbidites-deltas-and-banded-iron-formations.md); zone fossils in [06](06-geological-time-and-dating.md).

### The Welsh Basin (OCR 7.2.3(a))

#### What you need to know

- Geological setting and sedimentary conditions in the Welsh Basin through the Cambrian, Ordovician and Silurian.
- Palaeoenvironments from facies analysis (sediments and fossils): deep-sea turbidites and shelf deposits; reef limestones.
- Zonation using trilobites, corals and graptolites as zone fossils.

#### Explained

**Setting**: in the early Palaeozoic, Wales lay on the micro-continent Avalonia, on the southern side of the Iapetus Ocean, in southern latitudes moving north. The Welsh Basin was a subsiding marine basin on the edge of Avalonia, bordered to the south-east by a stable shelf (the Midland Platform, in the Welsh Borders and English Midlands). Subduction of Iapetus produced volcanic arcs, so the Ordovician includes thick volcanic rocks (Snowdonia). The basin closed during the Caledonian orogeny at the end of the Silurian and into the Devonian, folding and cleaving the rocks (slates of North Wales).

**Facies**:

| Facies | Rocks and fossils | Environment |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Basin turbidites | Thick sequences of graded greywacke sandstones with sole marks, alternating with mudstones (Bouma sequences) | Deep basin floor, sediment from turbidity currents off the shelf margin |
| Black graptolitic shales | Dark mudstones with pyrite and abundant graptolites, laminated, no burrows | Deep, quiet, anoxic basin; planktonic graptolites sank into it |
| Shelf sandstones and mudstones | Cross-bedded sandstones, siltstones, brachiopods, trilobites, burrows | Shallow, oxygenated shelf (Midland Platform) |
| Reef limestones | Limestones with tabulate and rugose corals, stromatoporoids, crinoids, brachiopods (Silurian Wenlock Limestone of Wenlock Edge and Dudley) | Warm, clear, shallow tropical shelf sea |
| Volcanic rocks | Lavas, tuffs, ash-flow deposits (Ordovician) | Volcanic arc and back-arc activity |

Moving from the Midland Platform north-westwards into the basin, facies change from shelf limestones and sandstones to deep-water turbidites and graptolitic shales (a facies change across the basin margin). Through time, Silurian facies show Wales moving into tropical latitudes (reefs) and the basin filling as Iapetus closed.

**Zonation**:

- Graptolites are the main zone fossils for the Ordovician and Silurian in the deep-water facies (rapid evolution, planktonic and widespread; see the trends in [06](06-geological-time-and-dating.md)).
- Trilobites zone the Cambrian (for example *Paradoxides* in the Middle Cambrian) and shelf facies of the Ordovician.
- Corals and brachiopods are less useful as zone fossils (facies-dependent, slower evolution) but indicate shelf environments.
- Using different groups in different facies, and places where they occur together, allows correlation from basin to shelf.

### The UK Jurassic (OCR 7.2.3(b))

#### What you need to know

- Geological setting and cyclical sedimentation in shallow seas.
- Facies analysis of the sediments: structures, sediment types (shales, limestones, siltstones, sandstones, ironstones) and fossils; burrows and other trace fossils for palaeoenvironments.
- Key macrofossils (ammonites, belemnites, bivalves); marine reptiles (ichthyosaurs, plesiosaurs) and flying reptiles (pterosaurs).
- Zonation and correlation using ammonites and belemnites; their use for palaeoenvironments.

#### Explained

**Setting**: in the Jurassic, Britain lay at about 30 to 40° N as Pangaea broke apart. Warm, shallow seas covered much of England, with islands of older rocks (Wales, the London-Brabant Massif, Scotland) supplying sediment. Rifting in the North Sea created deeper basins. Sea level rose and fell repeatedly.

**Cyclical sedimentation**: the Jurassic of England (for example the Dorset coast, the Cotswolds and the Yorkshire coast) shows repeated cycles: deeper-water mudstones and shales, passing upwards into siltstones, sandstones and shallow-water limestones (often oolitic) or ironstones, then back to mudstones. These reflect shallowing-upward cycles from sea-level changes and sediment supply (Walther's law).

| Facies | Environment |
| --- | --- |
| Dark shales and mudstones with ammonites, belemnites, bivalves, sometimes complete marine reptiles; fine laminations (for example the Lias of Lyme Regis and Whitby) | Offshore, quiet, sometimes poorly oxygenated sea floor below wave base |
| Siltstones and sandstones with burrows and cross-bedding | Shallower shelf, above storm wave base |
| Oolitic limestones (Bath stone) with cross-bedding | Very shallow, warm, agitated carbonate shoals |
| Shelly and coral limestones (for example the Corallian) | Shallow, clear, warm water; patch reefs |
| Ironstones (ooidal iron-rich rocks; Cleveland, Northamptonshire) | Shallow sea with slow deposition and iron supply from weathered land |
| Deltaic sandstones and coals (Middle Jurassic of Yorkshire) | Delta top and channels |

**Trace fossils**: vertical burrows show shallow, high-energy sands; horizontal feeding traces show quieter muds; absence of burrows and laminated dark shales show anoxic bottoms.

**Fossils as environment indicators**: ammonites and belemnites were free-swimming in open marine water; bivalves show substrate and energy (burrowers in sand, cemented oysters on hard grounds); marine reptiles (ichthyosaurs, fish-shaped hunters; plesiosaurs, long-necked paddle swimmers) show open sea; pterosaurs flew from nearby land.

**Zonation**: ammonites evolved very rapidly, swam freely and were widespread, so the Jurassic is divided into many ammonite zones (each lasting well under a million years), allowing very fine correlation across Britain and Europe. Belemnites are useful where ammonites are scarce (in the later Jurassic and Cretaceous of northern Europe). Limitation: ammonite distributions can be facies-related and shells could drift after death.

### Basin-wide investigation (OCR 7.2.3(c))

#### What you need to know

A practical investigation combining fieldwork and secondary data (maps, seismic data, well logs, fossils) to understand palaeoenvironments and geological history across a basin.

#### Explained

Steps in a basin study:

1. Log sections in the field (graphic logs), recording lithology, structures, fossils and palaeocurrents.
2. Collect secondary data: geological maps, borehole logs, seismic sections.
3. Interpret facies and environments for each section.
4. Correlate sections using zone fossils and marker beds; build a fence diagram or cross-section.
5. Map facies across the basin at each time slice (palaeogeographic maps).
6. Relate changes to tectonics (subsidence, faulting) and sea-level changes; identify unconformities.

#### Practical

**OCR PAG4 and PAG12**: construct and correlate graphic logs from Jurassic or Lower Palaeozoic sections; integrate fieldwork with published maps and well data.

#### Common mistakes

- Using corals as fine-scale zone fossils in the Welsh Basin.
- Ignoring facies when correlating: the same rock type is not always the same age.

### Check questions

1. Describe the tectonic setting of the Welsh Basin in the Ordovician. (3)
2. Explain how black graptolitic shales and greywacke turbidites show deep-water conditions. (4)
3. Explain why graptolites are better zone fossils than corals in the Welsh Basin. (3)
4. Describe how facies change from the Midland Platform into the Welsh Basin. (3)
5. Explain the cyclical sedimentation seen in the English Jurassic. (4)
6. Explain why ammonites are excellent zone fossils for the Jurassic. (3)
7. Describe how trace fossils can be used to interpret Jurassic palaeoenvironments. (3)
8. Outline how you would investigate the palaeoenvironments across a sedimentary basin. (5)

### Mark scheme

1. On Avalonia, southern margin of Iapetus (1); subsiding marine basin (1); subduction producing volcanic arc activity (1).
2. Black shales: fine, laminated, no burrows, pyrite: quiet anoxic deep water (2); turbidites: graded beds, sole marks, Bouma sequences: turbidity currents into deep basin (2).
3. Graptolites planktonic so widespread / facies independent (1); rapid evolution (1); corals restricted to shallow shelf facies / slower evolution (1).
4. Shelf: shallow sandstones, limestones, reef limestones with shelly fauna (1); margin: transitional (1); basin: turbidites and graptolitic shales (1).
5. Repeated cycles of mudstone → siltstone / sandstone → limestone or ironstone (1); shallowing-upward (1); due to sea-level changes / sediment supply (1); Walther's law links vertical and lateral facies (1).
6. Rapid evolution / short ranges (1); free-swimming so widespread (1); abundant, easily identified (1).
7. Vertical burrows: shallow high-energy sands (1); horizontal feeding traces: quiet muds (1); absence of burrows with laminations: anoxic bottoms (1).
8. Field logs (1); secondary data: maps, boreholes, seismic (1); facies interpretation (1); correlation with zone fossils / marker beds (1); palaeogeographic maps relating to tectonics / sea level (1).
